# Sūrah Al-Baqarah (Chapter 2) — Verse-by-Verse Tafsir

## Introduction to the Sūrah

The sūrah after the opening prayer is the longest in the Qur'an, and much of the Book's shape is set by it. Al-Baqarah is the title the community knows it by. Ibn Masʿūd, in a report al-Ālūsī carries, called the place where it was revealed the standing-place of the one upon whom it came down. Its verses number two hundred and eighty-six, and the count of two hundred and eighty-five or two hundred and eighty-seven is also reported. The title comes from the story of the cow the Children of Israel were ordered to slaughter, a story that sits inside the sūrah and gives it a name no other chapter shares.

Where it came down is agreed. Ibn Kathīr states that no one disputes that al-Baqarah was revealed in Madīnah in its entirety, and that it was among the first sūrahs to come down there. It came over scattered periods, as al-Qurṭubī notes. He also records the report that one verse near its end, (2:281), was the last revelation to come down from heaven. It came at Minā, on the day of sacrifice, during the farewell pilgrimage. The verses on usury belong to the same late period.

ʿUmar took twelve years over it, learning it with its understanding and everything it holds, and his son ʿAbdullāh took eight; the account is in al-Qurṭubī. A thousand commands, a thousand prohibitions, a thousand rulings and a thousand reports: that is how its contents are summed up in a saying al-Qurṭubī takes from Ibn al-ʿArabī. Khālid b. Maʿdān used to call it the pavilion of the Qur'an, and Ibn Kathīr reports the same nickname. The reports of its worth in battle are in Ibn Kathīr too. When the Muslims who had broken ranks were called back, the cry that turned them was *"O Companions of Surat Al-Baqarah!"*

The reports of virtue attach a protection to the house where it is recited. Muslim carries, on Abū Hurayrah's authority, the Prophet's ﷺ words: *"Do not turn your houses into graves. Verily, Shaytan does not enter the house where Surat Al-Baqarah is recited."* Ibn Kathīr records the hadith, gives its grading, and adds the saying of Ibn Masʿūd that Shaytan flees from the house where al-Baqarah is heard.

A description of the opening comes from Mujāhid, carried by Ibn Kathīr: four verses speak of the believers, two of the disbelievers, and thirteen of the hypocrites. The reader is told early, then, which kinds of heart the Book will meet. The same stretch is arranged in Maʿārif al-Qurʾān as a row of portraits: the God-fearing first, then those who reject, then the double-faced.

The sūrah's own order decides what comes next. It opens with letters that stand alone, then names the Book those letters introduce, then begins to describe the readers the Book will find.

## Verse 2:1

> Alif-Lãm-Mĩm

**THE LETTERS THAT OPEN THE SŪRAH**

The sūrah opens with three letters standing alone. A reader meets ***“Alif-Lãm-Mĩm”*** before he meets any statement about the Book. Each letter is pronounced on its own, without a vowel sound after it, as Maʿārif al-Qurʾān explains, and the exegetes class them among the isolated letters that open several sūrahs of the Qur'an. Their presence at the head of the chapter, before any claim is made, is treated in the sources as deliberate.

**WHAT THE LETTERS MIGHT STAND FOR**

Several readings of the letters are carried in the sources. The report held for this sūrah, from Ibn ʿAbbās, takes them as abbreviations of names: *"Alif stands for Allah, Lam for the Archangel Gabriel (Jibril) and Mim for Muhammad."* The same report offers others. On one, Alif stands for God's blessings, Lam for His kindness and Mim for His dominion. On another, the three are the openings of the names Allah, the Kind and the Glorious.

The views al-Ṭabarī found are filed under three heads. One party held that the letters are a name of the Qur'an, and he reports it from Qatādah, Mujāhid and Ibn Jurayj. A second held that they are openings by which God opens the Qur'an. A third held that they are letters of the alphabet, set at the head of sūrahs as God names what He wills, and that no one should seek a sense past that. Because the reports do not converge, al-Ṭabarī leaves the question open. The point of gathering them is not to pick a winner, since the early community held all of them in view. What remains is a set of readings rather than a settled meaning, and that is what the chapter hands the reader.

The position of al-Shaʿbī and a group with him is recorded by al-Baghawī: the letters belong to the elusive verses that God kept for Himself. The community believes in them, recites them, and leaves the knowledge of them to God. A related view reported by Ibn Kathīr holds that the letters were repeated across the Qur'an so that the challenge to produce anything like it becomes bolder. On that reading the letters are a summons to the doubters rather than a puzzle for the believers.

**WHY THE EXEGETES STOPPED**

The safest course, in al-Saʿdī's summary of it, is silence about the letters without a report from the Lawgiver, paired with certainty that God did not send them in play. They came for a wisdom the creation does not know. The same conclusion is stated by al-Jalālayn in a single line: *"God knows best what He means by these letters."*

The reason the early scholars stopped where they did is that the letters belong to the class the Qur'an itself calls elusive. (3:7 — **“some verses are precise—they are the foundation of the Book—while others are elusive”**). The same verse describes the hearts that chase such verses, and the early community refused to be counted among them. So the silence was not laziness; it was a discipline.

The saying al-Baghawī carries from Abū Bakr gives the mood of that refusal: *"In every book there is a secret, and God's secret in the Qur'an is the openings of the sūrahs."* ʿAlī's words stand beside it: *"For every book there is a choicest part, and the choicest part of this Book is the letters of spelling."* The same position is reported by al-Qurṭubī from al-Shaʿbī and a company of the traditionists. On their account the community believes in the letters, recites them as they came, and does not speak about what God has not explained.

**WHAT THE HOUSEHOLD THAT RECITES IT GAINS**

The sūrah that opens this way carries a promise for the place where it is read. The report from Abū Hurayrah, carried by Muslim and others, is cited by Ibn Kathīr: the devil does not enter a house where al-Baqarah is recited. The saying of Ibn Masʿūd stands beside it: the devil leaves a house where al-Baqarah is heard. That is why the sūrah is read in homes at night, and why the reading is a protection rather than only a recitation.

The discussion over the title itself is recorded by al-Ālūsī. Some of the early community disliked naming sūrahs after the creatures mentioned in them, and the practice was later accepted. The Prophet ﷺ himself spoke of the Companions of al-Baqarah on a day of battle, so the name was in use in his lifetime. The title is therefore a small lesson in how the community settled its habits.

Think of a locked room inside a well-built house. The family eats and sleeps and lives in the house, and that one door stays shut. None of them moves out because of it, and none of them breaks the door down. The house is still home, and the closed door says something about the one who built it: the owner keeps some rooms to himself.

The letters are that room for a reader of the Qur'an. He believes them, recites them, and leaves their knowledge where it belongs. The leaving is part of the reading, because a Book that explained everything to the last letter would leave no room for trust. Since the letters stand at the head of the sūrah, the lesson comes before any other lesson in it.

---

## Verse 2:2

> This is the Book! There is no doubt about it—a guide for those mindful ˹of Allah˺

**A BOOK THAT LEAVES NO ROOM FOR DOUBT**

The first words the sūrah speaks after the letters are a claim about the Book itself: ***“This is the Book!”*** The demonstrative in the Arabic points to something far off. The exegetes read it as pointing to what is present, as al-Ṭabarī reports, and four early authorities said one after another that it means this Book here: Mujāhid, ʿIkrimah, al-Suddī and Ibn ʿAbbās. The objection is answered by al-Ṭabarī himself: the far pointer is used because of the Book's rank, not because it is out of reach.

A promise carried by al-Baghawī puts the same point in narrative form. God had told His Prophet ﷺ that He would send down upon him a Book that water would not wash away and that would not wear out from much repetition. When the Qur'an came, the announcement was that this was the Book promised in the Torah and the Gospel and on the tongues of the prophets before him.

The Book is announced elsewhere in the Qur'an with the same phrases. (32:2 — **“The revelation of this Book is—beyond doubt—from the Lord of all worlds”**). Sūrah Hūd opens on the same note: (11:1 — **“a Book whose verses are well perfected and then fully explained”**). The claim is repeated because the reader will meet objections, and the Book answers them before they are raised.

**WHY THE POINTER STANDS AT A DISTANCE**

A second answer is found in Maʿārif al-Qurʾān. The pointer used here reaches far, and the sūrah before this one closed with a prayer for guidance. On this reading the far pointer turns back to that request. The Book is the detailed answer to the servant who asked to be guided, so the request made in al-Fātiḥah is answered in al-Baqarah.

What follows from that answer is drawn out by al-Saʿdī. The Book is guidance for the whole of life, not for a single matter, and because the object of the guidance is left unstated, the gift covers everything a person needs. It settles the roots of belief and the details of conduct, and it separates sound from unsound in both. A reader who holds the Book to one question has not yet taken it as it comes.

**WHAT NEGATION ADDS TO THE CLAIM**

The next words are ***“There is no doubt about it”***, and their force is more than a denial. Negation on its own is empty, as al-Saʿdī argues. When a negation is used to praise, the thing removed must have an opposite that is being affirmed, so the removal of doubt from the Book affirms certainty in it. The point stands even at the level of grammar: what is denied is denied so that its opposite may be credited.

The reports gathered by Ibn Kathīr show the same reading in the earliest community: al-Suddī, reporting from Ibn ʿAbbās and Ibn Masʿūd, said the words mean that there is no doubt about the Book. The same survey adds that no disagreement over that reading is recorded.

Where a reciter pauses was treated as part of the sense. The reports in Ibn Kathīr divide over where a reciter pauses. Some readers ended the sentence at the doubt and began the next with the guidance, and the better pause, on that account, comes after the doubt has been fastened to the Book. On that pause the guidance is described as a quality of the Book itself. The difference between the two pauses is the difference between a Book doubted and a Book guiding, and the second reading is the one the early scholars preferred.

**THE READERS THE BOOK REACHES FIRST**

The last words name those the Book guides: ***“a guide for those mindful ˹of Allah˺”***. The mindfulness named in it is a guarding of oneself, and al-Jalālayn explains the people described as those who keep themselves from the Fire by keeping the commands and avoiding what is forbidden.

Three readings are offered by Ibn ʿAbbās in the report held for this sūrah. The Book is an exposition for those who ward off disbelief, idolatry and indecency; it is a favour to the believers; and it is a mercy to the God-fearing among this community. Each reading keeps the same people in view: those who hold back from what harms them.

A saying of Ibn ʿUmar carried by al-Baghawī brings the matter home: *"Mindfulness of God is that you do not see yourself better than anyone."* The same writer notes that the God-fearing are singled out here as an honour, since they are the ones who take the guidance the Book offers. Because the gift is taken rather than announced, the reader is left to ask which group he belongs to.

What the wider form adds is pointed out by al-Saʿdī. The Book guides for all people, as the Qur'an says of it in another place, (2:185 — **“as a guide for humanity with clear proofs of guidance”**), and the God-fearing are those who take what is offered and walk with it. This is why the same Book that is a mercy to one reader is an argument against another. The gift is the same in both hands, and the difference is in the hand that receives it.

Both words are applied to the Book elsewhere in the Qur'an: (41:44 — **“It is a guide and a healing to the believers”**). Picture a lamp standing in a street. It is a mercy to every passer-by, and it lights the path only of the one who walks in its light. A man who turns his back on it and blames the dark has gained nothing from it. The lamp has not failed him, and what turns on the difference is what he does with the light.

---

## Verse 2:3

> who believe in the unseen, establish prayer, and donate from what We have provided for them

**FAITH IN WHAT THE SIGHT DOES NOT REACH**

The qualities of the God-fearing now begin to be listed, and the first is ***“who believe in the unseen, establish prayer”***. The Arabic behind the unseen is ghayb, and al-Qurṭubī explains it from the language first: everything absent from your sight is ghayb. The same writer reports the disagreement over what it covers here. One group said it is God Himself, and Ibn al-ʿArabī judged that reading weak. Others said it is the revelation, or the resurrection, or the reward and the punishment.

The report held for this sūrah, from Ibn ʿAbbās, lists what the unseen includes: Paradise, the Fire, the Bridge over the Fire, the Scale, the Resurrection and the Reckoning. The same report adds that a believer accepts what is revealed in the Qur'an and what is not revealed in it. The same ground is kept in al-Jalālayn, which describes the unseen as what is hidden from the servant of resurrection, paradise and fire.

The point of trusting in what cannot be seen is that it separates the believer from the one who trusts only what he can inspect. The distinction is put plainly by al-Saʿdī. Belief in things one sees is shared by everybody, so it cannot distinguish one person from another. What marks a believer is that he accepts what the prophets reported on the strength of the One who reported it, whether he sees it or not. Because the object of that trust is out of sight, the trust cannot be performed for an audience.

The earliest community read the words the same way. Al-Rabīʿ b. Anas, reported by Ibn Kathīr, said the words mean that they fear God. The reading Ibn Jarīr preferred, which Ibn Kathīr reports, is that the God-fearing believe in the unseen with tongue, heart and hand, so that fear of God sits inside the belief rather than beside it. The Qur'an describes the same people elsewhere: (67:12 — **“Indeed, those in awe of their Lord without seeing Him will have forgiveness and a mighty reward”**).

**PRAYER SET UP, NOT ONLY SAID**

The next words are ***“establish prayer”***, and the Arabic verb is stronger than the ordinary one for performing a prayer. The difference is noted by al-Saʿdī: the Qur'an did not say that they perform the prayer or that they come to it, because bringing out its outward shape does not by itself meet what is asked. Setting the prayer up means giving every part its due on the outside and bringing the heart inside it. What is asked is the presence of the worshipper, and attention to what he says and does.

The report held for Ibn ʿAbbās spells out the outward side. The five prescribed prayers are performed to completion, with the ablution that comes before them, the bowing and prostration that are part of them, and the appointed times kept. The same report notes that the words cover the prayer's obligations and its voluntary parts.

The same sense is given in short form by al-Jalālayn: the prayer is maintained by giving it its proper due. The fruit of the setting-up is named in a verse al-Saʿdī cites: (29:45 — **“Indeed, ˹genuine˺ prayer should deter ˹one˺ from indecency and wickedness”**). The effect is the test of the setting-up. A prayer that leaves the worshipper exactly where it found him has not been set up, because the deterrence is part of what the prayer is for.

Where the first Muslims prayed belongs to the report al-Qurṭubī carries. They used to call on their Lord quietly while the community was weak, and the report describes them addressing Him in secret and in public until the answer came in both. The habit is a reminder that the prayer was not always a public act, and that its foundation was laid in private.

**WHAT IS GIVEN OUT OF WHAT WAS GIVEN**

The last words are ***“and donate from what We have provided for them”***. Two things sit together in them: the gift comes from God, and the giving comes from the servant. Both halves are worked out by al-Saʿdī. The provision is God's before it is the servant's, since He is the One who provided it; and what is asked is not the whole of it. The believer gives out of what he has been given and keeps what he needs.

The spending named here covers obligations and more. The spending al-Saʿdī includes under it runs from the poor-due to the support of wives, relatives and those under a man's care, and on to all the voluntary giving that follows the paths of good. The report held for this sūrah agrees: it covers the poor-due given from wealth, and names Abū Bakr and his companions as the first believers described by it. So the verse draws a line from the duty that keeps a household standing to the free gift that costs the giver something.

The Qur'an repeats the command with its urgency attached: (63:10 — **“And donate from what We have provided for you before death comes to one of you”**). The same act is promised a return that no trade promises, (2:261 — **“The example of those who spend their wealth in the cause of Allah”**). Because the provision came first, the spending is not a loss but an answer.

Think of a purse handed by a master to a servant to carry across town. The servant has not been made rich by the purse; he has been trusted with it. Money kept is a claim on the person who keeps it, and money given under this verse is a claim handed back to its owner. What the servant passes on at the door he will be thanked for, and what he keeps for himself he will still have to answer for.

---

## Verse 2:4

> and who believe in what has been revealed to you ˹O Prophet˺ and what was revealed before you, and have sure faith in the Hereafter

**FAITH IN EVERY BOOK THAT WAS SENT**

A third quality is added to the list, and it turns on revelation: ***“and who believe in what has been revealed to you ˹O Prophet˺”***. The report held for this sūrah, from Ibn ʿAbbās, explains the words as belief in what God sent the Prophet ﷺ with, and belief in what the earlier messengers were sent with, without setting one above another and without rejecting what any of them brought from his Lord.

The Qur'an states the same demand at the head of a longer list: (4:136 — **“Have faith in Allah, His Messenger, the Book He has revealed to His Messenger, and the Scriptures He revealed before”**). The order in that verse, and in this one, puts the revelation given to the Prophet ﷺ first.

The order is deliberate, as al-Saʿdī reads it. The revelation given to the Prophet ﷺ is named first because it is the standard by which every earlier book is now known. What came before is accepted as part of the same trust, since the One who sent Moses and Jesus is the One who sent Muhammad ﷺ. So the verse asks for one faith, not for a set of loyalties divided among messengers.

**WHO THE VERSE DESCRIBES**

A report carried by Maʿārif al-Qurʾān from Ibn Masʿūd and Ibn ʿAbbās draws the line between the two verses. The God-fearing around the Prophet ﷺ came from two backgrounds. Some had been idolaters and became Muslims, and the earlier verse describes them. Others had been Jews or Christians and then accepted Islam, and this verse describes them. This is why ***“and what was revealed before you”*** is joined to their description.

The same report adds the reward that follows from the double acceptance. A man who believed in his own book and then believed in the Qur'an when it came is promised a double recompense, once for what he followed first and once for what he accepted later. The same report is carried by al-Ālūsī, who adds a fine point of grammar. For these believers faith in both revelations stands on its own, while the faith of others in the earlier books is bound up with their faith in the Qur'an, since the earlier scriptures are believed in through it.

**THE CERTAINTY THAT MOVES THE HAND**

The last words are ***“and have sure faith in the Hereafter”***, and the certainty named in them carries weight. The certainty in it carries weight in the Arabic, and al-Qurṭubī gathers it under one idea: knowledge that has no doubt in it, of the kind that moves the person who holds it. Al-Jalālayn describes the same certainty as assurance about the resurrection and the accounting.

The contents of the Hereafter are listed by Ibn Kathīr for the believer: the resurrection, the standing for judgment, Paradise, the Fire, the reckoning and the scale that weighs deeds. The name is given to all of it because it comes after this life, and the verse singles it out because it is a pillar of the faith.

Certainty of that kind is placed last among the qualities because it drives everything before it. Al-Saʿdī argues the order: the one who is sure of the meeting with God works for it, and the one who doubts it works for whatever he can see. The Hereafter is named on its own even though it belongs to the unseen, because it is the strongest motive a person has for wanting what God promises and fearing what He warns against. The point of the whole list comes together here: belief in what is hidden, prayer set up, and wealth given out all rest on a day the giver cannot see.

The same certainty is joined to a full list of belief elsewhere, (2:177 — **“who believe in Allah, the Last Day, the angels, the Books, and the prophets”**), and states the even-handedness this verse asks for in another place, (2:285 — **“We make no distinction between any of His messengers”**). The same word is used of the very people this verse names: (74:31 — **“so that the People of the Book will be certain”**).

Think of a farmer planting in the wet season because he is certain the dry months will end. He carries water, pulls weeds and stays in the field when nothing in front of him suggests a crop. His certainty about a day he cannot see decides how he spends the day he can. The believer's certainty works the same way, because it is the reason his prayers, his giving and his refusals all point somewhere.

---

## Verse 2:5

> It is they who are ˹truly˺ guided by their Lord, and it is they who will be successful

**WHAT THE GUIDANCE IS AND WHERE IT COMES FROM**

The description of the believers closes with a verdict on them: ***“It is they who are ˹truly˺ guided by their Lord”***. The demonstrative points back over everything just said: trust in the unseen, prayer set up, wealth given out, belief in the books, and certainty about the Hereafter. The verdict gathers all of it into one sentence about where these people stand.

Ibn Kathīr draws out what the words cover. They are on a light from their Lord, and on guidance and insight. The guidance is not something they produced for themselves, because it came from the One who made them. That is why the verse describes them by what they receive rather than by what they achieve.

The two routes are held side by side in one sentence elsewhere, (34:24 — **“one of our two groups is ˹rightly˺ guided; the other is clearly astray”**). A reader who hears that line is asked to place himself on one of the two sides, and the sūrah has already described who stands where.

Al-Saʿdī notices the preposition the verse uses. The guided are described as being *on* guidance, a word that carries height and standing, while the lost are described as being *in* misguidance, sunk inside it. Because the Arabic keeps the two images apart, the reader sees guidance as ground under the feet and error as something a man can sink into. The difference is not decoration; it is the shape of the two conditions.

The indefinite form carries weight too. Al-Saʿdī reads the wording as leaving the guidance undescribed because of its greatness, since what the Lord gives is beyond any name the recipient could supply. The same writer joins it to the verse in which hearts find their rest, (13:28 — **“Surely in the remembrance of Allah do hearts find comfort”**).

**WHO THE SUCCESSFUL ONES ARE**

The verse closes with a second verdict: ***“and it is they who will be successful”***. The Arabic root behind success is a word for cutting and turning the soil, and al-Baghawī notes that a ploughman is named from it because he works the earth. Inside that word sits the picture of a man who labours over ground until it gives something back.

Ibn ʿAbbās, in the report held for this sūrah, explains the successful ones as those who escape the wrath and the torment. The same report adds that they are the people who found what they were seeking and escaped what they were fleeing, and that those meant are the Companions of Muhammad ﷺ. So that word covers both sides of a life: what is gained, and what is spared.

The same reading is given by al-Jalālayn in one line: they enter Paradise and are saved from the Fire. Ibn Kathīr frames it a little wider. They have what they seek and are safe from the evil they tried to avoid, and what waits for them is reward, eternal life in the garden, and safety from the punishment. Because the two verdicts stand together, the guidance is the road and the success is the arrival.

**TWO TRAVELLERS IN ONE FLOOD**

Think of two travellers caught in a flood. One has taken the raised path and keeps his feet; the other has stepped into the ditch beside it and cannot climb out alone. Both heard the same warning, and what separates them is where they were standing when the water came. The men on the ditch's edge were not stronger than the others; they had simply taken the ground that was offered.

Guidance works that way in this verse: it is ground given under the feet. This is why the sūrah ends its portrait of the believers not with a list of their deeds but with a statement about where their Lord has placed them, and why the next verse turns to those who were offered the same ground and refused it.

---

## Verse 2:6

> As for those who persist in disbelief, it is the same whether you warn them or not—they will never believe

**THE MESSAGE THAT CHANGES NOTHING**

The sūrah turns from the believers to their opposite, and the sentence is blunt: ***“As for those who persist in disbelief, it is the same whether you warn them”***. The warning was the whole work of the Messenger of God, and for these people the message changes nothing: ***“or not they will never believe”***.

The word for disbelief is explained first by Ibn Kathīr: they covered the truth and hid it. He then draws the consequence. Since God has written that they would do this, it makes no difference whether the warning is given or withheld. The same is said of another people in the Qur'an, (36:10 — **“It is the same whether you warn them or not—they will never believe”**), and of those whose sentence has already fallen, (10:96 — **“Indeed, those against whom Allah’s decree ˹of torment˺ is justified will not believe”**).

**WHAT THE WORD DISBELIEF MEANS**

The Arabic word is built on a root that means covering. al-Baghawī sets out the pictures inside it. Night takes its name from the same root because it covers what lies under it, and a farmer takes it because he covers seed with soil. A disbeliever covers the truth with his denial. The same writer lists four kinds: the unbelief of ignorance, of stubborn rejection, of obstinacy, and of hypocrisy. Each kind hides something different, so the reader should not imagine that a single word describes one single attitude.

An older sense of the word is added by al-Qurṭubī. In Arabic it can also mean ingratitude for a blessing, and he cites the report in which the Prophet ﷺ described the people of the Fire as mostly women, and explained on that day that they were ungrateful to those who had cared for them. Disbelief in the Qur'an's usage is therefore not only denial of God's existence. It can be the burying of a known gift, which means a believer's own life can carry traces of it.

The range is given in brief by Ibn ʿAbbās: the people are firm in their denial, or they do not want to believe, or what is described is what God already knows about them. The difference between the readings matters, since the first describes a settled state and the last describes knowledge that never changes. The type is named by al-Jalālayn: men like Abū Jahl and Abū Lahab, whose opposition was settled, and it explains that warning here means making someone fear. The reading of the verse's verb differs among the reciters, and al-Baghawī lists the readers who pronounce the two hamzas and those who drop one. The point is that the plain sense of the verse does not depend on which reading is followed.

**WHY THE WARNING CANNOT REACH THEM**

What the call can still do for such people is worth stating plainly, and it is stated by al-Saʿdī. The invitation adds nothing to their case except the establishment of the proof against them. Because the refusal is settled, the preaching does not leave them exactly where it found them; it leaves them with less to say on the day the account is taken. This is why the call is still to be made, since it either turns a heart or closes a case.

The verse also lifts a weight from the one who carried the call. Ibn Kathīr reads it as cutting off the hope that the call would turn such men, and al-Saʿdī says the same: the sorrow over them is not to consume the one who calls them, because the outcome is settled with their Lord. The passage has its place in the sūrah, and Maʿārif al-Qurʾān sets it out. The first five verses describe those who take the guidance, and the fifteen that follow describe those who refuse it, a group the sūrah will then divide into the openly hostile and the double-faced.

Think of a bell rung outside a locked house. The sound is real and the rope is pulled, but the man inside decided long before not to come to the door when it rang. The bell has not failed, and the householder has settled the question for himself. The same is true of the warning: its power is not in doubt, and their answer was given in advance.

---

## Verse 2:7

> Allah has sealed their hearts and their hearing, and their sight is covered. They will suffer a tremendous punishment

**THE STAMP ON THE HEART AND THE EAR**

The next verse names what stands in the way: ***“Allah has sealed their hearts”***, and it joins the hearing to the heart: ***“and their hearing, and their sight is covered”***. The Arabic behind the seal is a word for stamping, and al-Ṭabarī begins there. A seal is what is pressed onto a container, and a letter is sealed when it is stamped. The objection he raises himself is fair: a heart is not a jar, so how is it stamped? His answer is that a heart is a vessel too, holding whatever is placed in it, and the same is true of the hearing, through which news of the unseen arrives.

The sense is gathered by al-Qurṭubī: covering a thing and securing it so that nothing else can enter, the way a book or a door is sealed. The same writer notes that the hearts of the rejecters are described in the Qur'an with ten qualities, and that this verse carries the first and heaviest of them. The point of the seal, therefore, is not damage to the heart but a lock upon it.

The explanations of the earliest readers are carried by Ibn Kathīr. al-Suddī said simply that God has sealed them, and Qatādah said that the devil controlled them when they obeyed him, so that God then sealed their hearts, their hearing and their sight. On that account they could neither see the guidance nor hear it. Mujāhid described the seal as a mark that comes from sin: when sin settles in the heart and surrounds it, the heart is stamped by it.

**WHY A HEART COMES TO BE SEALED**

The sealing is not arbitrary, and the sources are careful about the order. The verse is read by al-Ālūsī as a proof attached to the sentence before it: the stamp and the covering follow from the rejection, and from the habits of sin that the rejecters themselves piled up. The verse describes a consequence, therefore, and not a decision made without cause behind it.

The stamp is described by al-Saʿdī in the same way. It is a mark that faith does not enter and does not pass through, so that its bearers neither understand what would help them nor hear what would benefit them. The same picture is drawn out in al-Jalālayn: the seal fixes the heart so that no good enters it, the hearing holds something that keeps it from profiting by the truth, and the eye carries a veil so that the truth is not seen.

The verse is also given faces by Ibn ʿAbbās, in the report held for this sūrah. He names men of Madīnah from the People of the Book, among them Ka'b b. al-Ashraf, Huyayy b. Akhtab and Judayy b. Akhtab, and he adds that others took the verse to mean the idolaters of Makkah such as Utbah, Shaybah and al-Walid. The report shows that the description was applied to particular men the first hearers knew, and the warning in it was for anyone who followed them.

**THE VEIL OVER THE SIGHT AND WHAT FOLLOWS IT**

The verse ends with the outcome: ***“They will suffer a tremendous punishment”***. Described in al-Jalālayn as intense and lasting, the punishment matches what the verse listed a moment before, since the loss of hearing, sight and understanding is itself a penalty. The eyes are the last of the three to be mentioned, and the sources treat the order as deliberate: the heart leads, the hearing carries what the heart will use, and the eye shows what the heart will judge.

The same sealing is described in other sūrahs in the same words. (45:23 — **“Allah left them to stray knowingly, sealed their hearing and hearts, and placed a cover on their sight”**) ties the seal to a life spent following desire; (47:16 — **“These are the ones whose hearts Allah has sealed”**) attaches it to men who listened to the words and mocked them on the way out; and (17:46 — **“We have cast veils over their hearts—leaving them unable to comprehend it—and deafness in their ears”**) shows the veil and the deafness as a pair. What the eye loses is named elsewhere as well: (22:46 — **“It is not the eyes that are blind, but it is the hearts in the chests that grow blind”**).

Think of a jar whose lid has been soldered shut. Water can be poured over it all day, and what is inside stays exactly as it was. The solder did not choose the contents; it fixed them. That is the sense in which the verse speaks of a stamp, because the heart that keeps refusing is finally fixed in its refusal, and the fixing is announced so that nobody is deceived by appearances. The three verses together leave the reader with a question to carry: the same message reaches every ear, and what decides the outcome is what the heart does with it while the seal is still absent.

---

## Verse 2:8

> And there are some who say, “We believe in Allah and the Last Day,” yet they are not ˹true˺ believers

**A THIRD KIND OF HEART**

The sūrah has shown the believers, then the hardened rejecters, and now it turns to a third group: ***“And there are some”***. The report held by al-Suddī identifies them at once as the hypocrites, and Mujāhid's count, carried by al-Qurṭubī, gives the opening of the sūrah its shape: four verses describe the believers, two the disbelievers, and thirteen the hypocrites. So the reader learns early that the Book will deal with a heart that faces two ways.

The meaning of hypocrisy is set out plainly by Ibn Kathīr. The sense is set out plainly by Ibn Kathīr: a man who shows agreement and conceals evil. Al-Saʿdī states the same definition, and divides it in two. One kind sits inside belief itself, and it is what this sūrah describes. Another kind sits in behaviour, and it belongs to the sins that a believer can fall into.

The second kind is described in a report al-Saʿdī cites, carried by al-Bukhārī and Muslim: the signs of the hypocrite are three — when he speaks he lies, when he makes a promise he breaks it, and when he is trusted he betrays. One narration of the same report adds that when he argues, he behaves immorally. The point is that a man can carry the manners of hypocrisy while still belonging to the faith, which means the reader should test himself by those three signs before judging anyone else.

**WHY THE TYPE APPEARED IN MADĪNAH**

Hypocrisy of the deeper kind had no place in Makkah, and the reason is in al-Saʿdī's account. In Makkah the believers were few and weak, and a man who did not accept the message simply said so. Nothing was gained by pretending. The situation changed after the migration, and it changed sharply after Badr, when God honoured the believers and gave them the upper hand. Men in Madīnah who had not submitted began to fear for their lives and their property, and some of them brought their Islam out into the open with their tongues while their hearts stayed where they were.

Al-Saʿdī calls the outcome a mercy for the believers, because God set out the marks of these people so that they could be recognised. Without the description, a community cannot guard itself against a man who prays beside it and works against it. The verses that follow are therefore a warning with a practical purpose, and not only a portrait.

**A CLAIM, AND THE VERDICT ON IT**

The words they use are given exactly: ***“who say, “We believe in Allah”*** and ***“and the Last Day,”***. Maʿārif al-Qurʾān notices what the claim leaves out. The hypocrites named faith in God and faith in the Day of Judgment, and they avoided naming faith in the Prophet ﷺ, because that would have been a lie too heavy for them to carry. Even the men who were working against the message tried to keep their words as close to the truth as they could, and the sūrah's word for lying is applied to them afterwards.

The verdict follows in four words: ***“yet they are not ˹true˺ believers”***. Ibn ʿAbbās explains the two sides of the sentence. They said with their tongues what they did not hold in their hearts. Al-Jalālayn notes a small matter of wording: the Last Day is called by that name because it is the last of the days, so a man who claims faith in it claims faith in everything that has come before it, including the message standing in front of him.

The Qur'an will meet these people again, and its description there matches this one. (63:1 — **“We bear witness that you are certainly the Messenger of Allah”**) records the same testimony on the same tongues. (9:64 — **“The hypocrites fear that a sûrah should be revealed about them, exposing what is in their hearts”**) records the fear that follows it. A claim that needs a covering is not a claim a man has settled in himself.

Think of a house whose owner whitewashes the front wall every spring. From the road it looks kept, and guests who never enter believe what they see. Inside, the beams are soft with damp. The paint is not the crime; the hiding is, and the day the roof falls, the neighbours will say that the outside was the best part of that house. The reason the sūrah spends so long on this kind of person is that his danger is invisible, since his tongue and his hand both look right. A community cannot guard itself against a man it cannot name, which is why the portrait is given before any command is issued, and that is why the reader is taught to test himself by the signs rather than by the reputation he carries.

---

## Verse 2:9

> They seek to deceive Allah and the believers, yet they only deceive themselves, but they fail to perceive it

**WHAT DECEIT MEANS IN THE LANGUAGE**

Their aim is named in the opening words: ***“They seek to deceive Allah”***. Maʿārif al-Qurʾān makes the first point, and it is a blunt one. Not one of them could have believed in his own mind that God was deceived. What they were doing was aimed at the Prophet ﷺ and the Muslims, and God attached their attempt to Himself, so the reader learns that impertinence toward a prophet is impertinence toward the One who sent him.

The Arabic root behind the word is opened up by al-Ālūsī. It carries concealment and the planting of a false impression: a lizard is described by it when it hides itself, a storeroom is described by it because what is inside is kept out of sight, and the two veins beside the throat carry it because they lie hidden. A man who deceives works in the open while keeping his purpose hidden, which is exactly what a hypocrite does with his tongue.

The deed is described in the same terms by al-Ṭabarī. He reads the words as the hypocrite's declaration of belief made with the tongue against what the heart holds, put forward in order to turn aside from himself the judgment that would otherwise fall on him, which is the work of a man playing a part rather than a man persuaded. Al-Qurṭubī gathers three early answers to the question of how anyone can be said to deceive God. One, reported by our scholars, is that the attempt exists only in the deceiver's own estimation and never in fact, which is why the verse can speak of self-deceit without contradicting its own first words. Another is that the men are named after the act they chose: since the deed was a deception, they are spoken of as deceivers, and what they achieved is not taken into account. The third reads an ellipsis in the sentence, so that the one they set out to deceive was the Messenger of God himself, and God attached the offence to His own name, because an insult aimed at a messenger reaches the One who sent him. All three answers meet at the same point, and the reason the verse can turn the plot back on the plotters is that a lie told about God is told in front of God.

**THE BOOMERANG**

The second half of the verse turns the attempt around: ***“and the believers, yet they only deceive themselves”***, and the last stroke lands it: ***“but they fail to perceive it”***. Al-Jalālayn explains the reversal in two stages. The harm of the deception returns to the deceiver, who loses standing among the believers in this life and meets the punishment in the next, and the success of the plot is an illusion because God knows what is inside them.

Ibn ʿAbbās gives a sharp detail here. The people meant did not know that God tells His Prophet ﷺ what is hidden in hearts. On that account they believed they were working in the dark, and all the while their names and intentions were being made known. The Qur'an states the same law in another place, (4:142 — **“Surely the hypocrites seek to deceive Allah, but He outwits them”**). The plotter never has the last move, because the one he is trying to fool holds the information he is hiding.

The Qur'an will speak of that uncovering later in this sūrah, and the account of the same fear in a later sūrah shows where it led: (9:64 — **“The hypocrites fear that a sûrah should be revealed about them, exposing what is in their hearts”**). The fear is itself the proof that they knew the concealment could not be permanent.

Think of a man who digs a pit across the path his neighbour uses, covers it with leaves, and then walks the same road home after dark. The trap is real, the disguise is neat, and the one who dug it carries no lantern. That is an illustration of what the verse describes: the deception is genuine, and the danger of it lands on the man who set it.

The warning in the verse is not aimed at clever men only. Every reader carries some part of this habit — the gentle answer that hides a grudge, the work done well for the eye of the employer and done badly for the eye of God. The point of the verse is not that such things fail to fool people; it is that they never fool the One who matters.

---

## Verse 2:10

> There is sickness in their hearts, and Allah ˹only˺ lets their sickness increase. They will suffer a painful punishment for their lies

**THE DISEASE IN THE HEART**

The diagnosis comes first: ***“There is sickness in their hearts”***. The illness is read as doubt in the report held for Ibn ʿAbbās, and Ibn Masʿūd agrees with him, while Mujāhid, ʿIkrimah, al-Ḥasan al-Baṣrī, Abū al-ʿĀliyah, al-Rabīʿ and Qatādah all say the same, as Ibn Kathīr reports. The hearts are called sick because doubt has taken up residence in them.

The reason the description fits is added by al-Qurṭubī. A heart is diseased once it loses the balance that keeps it working, and these hearts have let go the guarding and the care that God gives to those who turn to Him. He also records the linguist Ibn Fāris's rule: anything that takes a man out of the boundaries of health, whether a bodily illness or a doubt or a shortfall in a duty, belongs to disease.

The condition is not a small one. Ibn ʿUthaymīn works through the verse and draws the first of its lessons: the disease is settled deep in the heart, wrapped inside it, and it consists of doubt, unbelief, denial, deception and plotting. A man may carry a disease like that for years and never feel it, for the reason that a sickness of the heart does not hurt until the moment it decides his eternity.

**THE INCREASE, AND WHO ALLOWS IT**

The verse continues with the most solemn phrase in the section: ***“and Allah ˹only˺ lets their sickness increase”***. Doubt does not stand still when it is fed; God lets the growth proceed. The order is explained by al-Saʿdī: the corruption was theirs to begin with, and the increase is the answer to it, which means the second stage of the illness is a punishment that arrives before the greater one.

The increase is attached by al-Jalālayn to the revelation itself. Every part of the Qur'an that comes down and is disbelieved adds to what they carry, and the same is stated elsewhere in a fuller sentence, (9:125 — **“it has increased them only in wickedness upon their wickedness”**). The Book that heals the believer's heart is the thing that deepens the disease of the one who has already refused it, because the remedy and the proof are the same object.

Ibn ʿUthaymīn draws the second lesson from that: whoever God knows to be carrying evil, He increases him in it, and the increase flows from the same knowledge that made the plan of the hypocrite transparent. Nothing is hidden from the One who decides.

**THE PUNISHMENT AND THE CAUSE NAMED LAST**

The ending is announced twice: ***“They will suffer a painful punishment”***, and the cause is given at the very end of the verse, ***“for their lies”***. The last word carries two readings among the reciters, and al-Jalālayn sets both out. One reading makes the hypocrites the ones who used to call the Prophet ﷺ a liar. The other makes it stand for their own lying, the false claim of faith that ran through everything they said.

The report held for Ibn ʿAbbās names men the first hearers knew: Abdullah b. Ubayy, Jadd b. Qays and Muattib b. Qushayr. Because the verse was applied to particular neighbours, the warning it carries cannot be dismissed as a portrait of a distant sort of person; the men who heard it could walk to those houses.

Think of a cut left unwashed on a damp day. It does not stay the size it was when it was made, and each day of neglect adds a little more fever and a little more waiting. The same is true of a doubt that is fed: the portion a man keeps is the portion that grows, and the growth stops only when the heart does. Since nothing in the man is working against the growth, the sūrah's warning is aimed at the first day rather than the last, because that is where the feeding begins. Because the growth is the punishment, the verse warns a man about what he is feeding rather than about what he already holds.

---

## Verse 2:11

> When they are told, “Do not spread corruption in the land,” they reply, “We are only peace-makers!”

**THE COMMAND AND THE ANSWER**

The next scene is a conversation. Someone warns them: ***“When they are told, “Do not spread corruption in the land,””*** and the reply is a claim about themselves: ***“they reply, “We are only peace-makers!””***. What corruption means here is spelled out in the report al-Suddī carries from Ibn ʿAbbās and Ibn Masʿūd: it is unbelief and the acts of disobedience that go with it. The practical side is added by al-Jalālayn, since a hypocrite's unbelief also works by hindering people from faith.

The same reading is given in his report by Ibn ʿAbbās: the mischief is preventing people from the religion of the Prophet ﷺ. So the corruption in question is not only an act done by a man against himself. It is done against a community, by standing in the road of those who are coming to the message, and the damage it leaves is the loss of the message itself.

A report carried by al-Ṭabarī warns against pinning the verse on one set of names. Salman al-Farisi used to say that these people had not yet come. In his view the description belonged to a kind of person who would appear later, and the sūrah was setting out the marks in advance. A reader in any generation can therefore read the two verses as addressed to what he might become.

What the corruption includes is set out by al-Saʿdī. The harm in his reading is not a private opinion held against the message; it is acting on unbelief and disobedience, and among its forms he names the passing of the believers' secrets to their enemy and the taking of the disbelievers as allies. A community loses more through that channel than through open argument, since an argument can be answered and a leak cannot. Al-Ālūsī adds a grammatical point that carries meaning: the sentence may be joined to the accusation of lying that stands before it, so that the lie and the corruption form two counts in one indictment and the punishment gathers both. Since the corruption is named as a cause of the sentence that follows, the reader learns that the harm a man does to a community is also the harm he does to himself. The order of the verse is therefore kind, because the warning is given while the men who would take the community apart are still standing in its street.

**WHY THE CLAIM DOES NOT DECIDE THE QUESTION**

The case is put plainly by Maʿārif al-Qurʾān. The meaning of a man's work is not fixed by his own description of it. Nobody sits down and decides to be a thief on the ground that stealing is unpleasant; the same men were producing chaos in the community while calling themselves the men of order.

The test the verse uses is the result. If a man's activity ends in mischief, he is the mischief-maker, no matter what he intended, and the reason is that a community is judged by what it receives, not by what the one who harmed it had in mind. The point is pressed from the other side as well by Maʿārif al-Qurʾān: it asks who the real reformers are, and answers that the reform belongs to the one whose work leaves the land and the religion more sound than he found them.

**THE TWO CLAIMS SIDE BY SIDE**

The verse is a small anatomy of self-deceit. The speaker is not lying in the way a witness lies about a fact; he has built a picture of himself and now answers from inside it. Because the picture is comfortable, he never tests it against the state of the ground he has been working.

Think of a man who pulls up the young shoots in a neighbour's field, explaining cheerfully that he is loosening the soil. He may believe the sentence while he says it; the furrow is still wrecked, and the harvest will still fail. The same holds for a man whose dealings scatter a community and who answers a warning with a slogan about his own good intentions.

That is why the next verse does not argue with their claim. It simply says who they are, and the reader who has watched the scene knows which verdict is warranted.

---

## Verse 2:12

> Indeed, it is they who are the corruptors, but they fail to perceive it

**THE VERDICT WITH THE EMPHASIS ON IT**

The answer to the slogan is a verdict: ***“Indeed, it is they”*** — and the words are placed first in the sentence so that the identification cannot be missed. Then comes the charge: ***“who are the corruptors”***, and then the reason the charge was resisted: ***“but they fail to perceive it”***.

The opening particle is noted by al-Jalālayn: it is used in Arabic to alert the hearer before a heavy statement. The placement is deliberate, because the sentence goes against what the accused believe about themselves. The same reading of the passage is recorded by Ibn Kathīr from the earliest commentators: the men are the makers of mischief while they think of themselves as the ones putting things right.

A specific shape is given to the failure of perception in the report held for Ibn ʿAbbās. The followers do not know that their leaders are leading them astray. So the verse describes two levels of blindness at once: the chief who mistakes his leadership for reform, and the follower who mistakes his following for loyalty.

Al-Ālūsī explains why the verdict is delivered as a fresh sentence rather than folded into the argument that came before. The claim was made in one voice and answered in another, and the answer stands where the hearing will settle on it: the men are named, the charge is set down, and the reason for their confidence is left for the last words. The line is read by al-Ṭabarī as God's own denial of the claim, spoken at the moment the warning was still being given, so that the refusal of the correction becomes part of the record. Al-Qurṭubī carries a sentence from the masters of meanings: *"whoever proclaims his own claim has lied."* The rule fits the scene, because the men were not asked what they were; they were told what their work was doing, and they answered with a title. What follows from the verse is therefore a caution about self-description, since the man who writes his own verdict has already stopped listening to the evidence. The pair of verses is read by al-Saʿdī as an inversion of realities: the corruption was done first and dressed afterwards as reform, which is why the verdict has to answer the dressing rather than the deed.

**WHAT CORRUPTION DOES TO A LAND**

The same theme returns outside this passage, and each return sharpens what is at stake. (7:56 — **“Do not spread corruption in the land”**) places the command where the land has already been put in order, which means the mischief is a spoiling of something good rather than a failure to build. (30:41 — **“Corruption has spread on land and sea as a result of what people’s hands have done”**) connects the damage to human action. It shows a condition that grows rather than a single act, and the warning is aimed at the direction of travel. And the Qur'an asks the hypocrites themselves, (47:22 — **“perhaps you would then spread corruption throughout the land”**), whether the turning away they are contemplating leads anywhere else.

Read together, the verses leave no room for the comfortable idea that a private intention is enough. Corruption is described by what it does, and it does its work whether or not the one who causes it calls it reform.

**THE DIFFICULTY OF SEEING ONE'S OWN WORK**

The last words of the verse are its real subject. Corruption that no one recognised would still be corruption; the verse adds the blindness so that the reader will not trust his own reading of himself. A man is the worst judge of the harm he has done, because his account of his own intentions is always on his side.

Think of a driver who reverses over a garden while believing he is following the path exactly. The footprints of his own tyres are behind him, and he cannot see them from where he sits. The voice that tells him he is on the path is his own, and the damage is on the ground for everyone else to see.

Because the sense of being right is not evidence of being right, the verse ends where it does. The reader who takes it seriously will ask for information from outside himself — from the Book, from the community, from the people his conduct touches — and will treat the warm confidence inside him as a witness that needs checking.

---

## Verse 2:13

> And when they are told, “Believe as others believe,” they reply, “Will we believe as the fools believe?” Indeed, it is they who are fools, but they do not know

**FAITH MEASURED AGAINST A COMMUNITY**

The next exchange begins with an invitation: ***“And when they are told”***, and the standard is named in the same breath: ***“Believe as others believe,”***. The agreement of the commentators is reported by Maʿārif al-Qurʾān: the people meant here are the Companions of the Prophet ﷺ, the ones who accepted the faith while the Qur'an was coming down in front of them.

The verse therefore does two things at once. The invitation calls the hypocrites to believe, and it also fixes the shape of belief by pointing at a body of people who had already done so. Maʿārif al-Qurʾān draws the consequence: the faith that is acceptable to God is the faith that resembles theirs, which means a believer does not get to invent a private version of Islam and keep the name.

The scope of the invitation is listed by Ibn Kathīr, item by item: belief in God, His angels, His books, His messengers, the resurrection after death, Paradise and the Fire, and then obedience to God and His Messenger by keeping the commands and avoiding what is forbidden. The scope is wide because the word is wide. Ibn ʿAbbās, in the report held for this sūrah, gives the human faces behind it: Abdullah b. Salam and his companions are named among the believers meant.

**THE INSULT AND THE ANSWER TO IT**

The reply is sneering: ***“they reply, “Will we believe”***, and it closes with the word they chose for the believers: ***“as the fools believe?”***. The fools in their mouths were the men who had left their old position, paid what it cost, and taken up a faith with no worldly advantage attached.

The answer is exact: ***“Indeed, it is they”***, and the verdict is the same word they had used, now returned: ***“who are fools, but they do not know”***. The word is explained by Ibn ʿAbbās as the stupid and the ignorant, and al-Jalālayn reads it as the people who do not know what is in front of them, since a man who cannot see his own loss is in a poor position to grade the eyesight of others.

The same wording appears of a smaller failure in the same chapter's family of stories: (2:130 — **“And who would reject the faith of Abraham except a fool”**). The connection is worth noticing, because the men of Madīnah were rejecting the faith of the man they claimed as their ancestor, while the Companions were taking it up.

**A WORD FROM THE READERS**

One small technical note belongs here, and it is supplied by al-Baghawī. The word for "it was said" in this verse can be recited with a slight rounding of the first vowel or with the plain vowel. The readers of the Qur'an differed over which to use: al-Kisāʾī rounded the first vowel before the letter that carries it, and the others recited it plain. The difference is a matter of recitation and not of meaning, and the same choice occurs in a handful of words through the Qur'an.

Think of a traveller who refuses a map because he does not like the hands holding it. The map is accurate and the road is the same road, but the man's objection is not to the map; it is to the company he would have to keep if he used it. That is the exchange in this verse. The invitation was not an invitation to an idea but to a community, and that is precisely what the men could not accept, because joining it would have cost them the standing they were protecting.

The verses that follow keep the same company in view and let the reader see what the double language costs when it is actually spoken.

---

## Verse 2:14

> When they meet the believers they say, “We believe.” But when alone with their evil associates they say, “We are definitely with you; we were only mocking.”

**THE PUBLIC VOICE AND THE PRIVATE ONE**

The behaviour is now shown in two rooms. In the first, ***“When they meet the believers they say, “We believe.””*** In the second, ***“But when alone with their evil associates they say, “We are definitely with you”***, and the confession that closes the sentence is ***“we were only mocking.””***.

The motive behind the first voice is explained by Ibn Kathīr. The claim gained them standing in the community and a share of whatever good came to it, so it was worth making to anyone who could not see inside them. The second voice was the price they paid to keep their real companions, who would have turned on them had the pretence been allowed to stand.

**WHO THE ASSOCIATES ARE**

Those companions carry a heavy designation, and al-Jalālayn explains it as their leaders and chiefs. The report held for Ibn ʿAbbās names five of them: Ka'b b. al-Ashraf, who was in Madinah, Abu Burdah al-Aslami among Banu Aslam, Ibn al-Sawda in Syria, Abd al-Dar among Juhaynah, and Awf b. Amir among Banu Amir. These were the men the hypocrites returned to when they left the Prophet's ﷺ company, and they returned with the day's news.

The names matter for the shape of the story. These were not men who merely doubted quietly. They kept a channel open to the enemies of the community, and they carried what they heard back along it, which is why the devils are named as their counterparts. The verse exposes a whole working arrangement: a public claim for the benefit of the believers, and a private loyalty to those who were working against them.

A small point of recitation in the opening words is noted by Qurṭubī: the letter is moved and dropped so that it is light on the tongue, and the alternative recitation of Muhammad b. al-Samayqa keeps the longer form. The detail is a reminder that the text was received and passed on with care, down to the vowels.

Why the scene is shown at all, rather than left where the story began, is set out by al-Ālūsī. The point is the habit and not the incident, since the men are displayed in the two places where a life is spent, the open street and the closed room, so that the reader learns the pattern rather than an episode. The two verses that describe them are joined by al-Ṭabarī as one account told twice for emphasis, and his reading keeps the mockery from looking like a slip of the tongue. Al-Saʿdī gives the word for their confidants the same weight: the great ones among them in evil, the men whose approval they could not do without. None of the three writers softens the matter into a story about strangers, because the confession in the verse comes from the speakers themselves. What follows from that is a rule the reader can apply: since a man's second voice is the one he keeps for the people he trusts, the room he chooses says more about him than the greeting he gives at the gate.

**WHAT FOLLOWS THE MOCKERY**

The verse ends with an admission that the claim to the believers was never honest, and the Qur'an's next words take up the mockery they confess to. (2:15 — **“Allah will throw their mockery back at them, leaving them to continue wandering blindly in their defiance”**). The answer to mockery is not an argument but an abandonment to the condition the mocker has chosen.

The passage is read by Ibn Kathīr as a warning about divided loyalty. A man who keeps two accounts open with two enemies cannot be a believer in either direction, and the outcome is stated plainly: the laughter they aimed at the believers comes back at them, and the blindness they thought they were hiding becomes their road.

Think of a worker who greets his employer warmly at the gate and speaks differently in the yard where his friends gather. He may hold both conversations for a while, and every one of them costs him something: at the gate he is a servant, in the yard he is a hero, and he is never entirely in either place. The verse follows such a man past the gate and lets the reader hear the second voice, because that is where the truth of a life is kept. The reason the second voice matters more is that a man does not perform for the people he fears; because nobody is watching in that room except his own side, the words spoken there are the words he means. A man's true position is therefore decided in the room where he has nothing to gain, and that is why the sūrah lets the reader hear the second voice and not only the first.

The verses collected here are a single portrait, and the parts of it belong together. A claim made by the tongue, a plot that rebounds, a sickness that grows, a command answered with a slogan, a verdict the accused cannot see, an invitation refused with an insult, and a mockery confessed in private. The reader who meets the sequence in order is being taught to recognise the pattern in himself before he is asked to find it in others.

---

## Verse 2:15

> Allah will throw their mockery back at them, leaving them to continue wandering blindly in their defiance

**MOCKERY ANSWERED IN KIND**

The sentence that follows the hypocrites' private confession is a single phrase, and it turns their weapon around: ***“Allah will throw their mockery back at them, leaving them to continue wandering blindly in their defiance”***. The first words are read by al-Jalālayn as requital: God repays them for the mockery they aimed at His servants, so that the treatment they handed out becomes the treatment they receive.

The closing words describe a state rather than an act. They are left in their transgression, bewildered, going forward without knowing where the road ends. The last word describes them as wandering in perplexity, al-Jalālayn notes, which means the punishment begins as a condition of the mind before it becomes a sentence on the Day of Judgment.

**THE LONG ROPE AND THE LIGHT TAKEN AWAY**

The delay that the verse assumes is explained by Maʿārif al-Qurʾān. The hypocrites were pleased with themselves for the trick they thought they were working, and God, in His forbearance, gave them a long rope. The rope is not a kindness in the end, because its purpose is to let a man go far enough to be caught in the open, and that is how the mockery is settled.

The forms the answer takes are worked through by al-Saʿdī. In this life the false position is made to look comfortable to them, and later the same answer is given in front of everyone. He cites the scene in which a light is given to the hypocrites along with the believers, and then the believers walk on by theirs while the light of the others goes out. The full account is in the Qur'an, (57:13 — **“Wait for us so that we may have some of your light”**), where the request is refused and a wall is set between the two groups.

The requital itself is discussed by al-Ṭabarī and al-Qurṭubī under the question of what the words can mean when they are spoken of God. Three views are set out in his treatment: some held that the answer belongs to the day when the covers are torn away and the secrets are shown, and others that it begins in this life, since the men already reap in their standing what they sowed in their laughter. The reading that the majority took is gathered by al-Qurṭubī: the punishment is named by the name of the offence, so that the sentence answers the mockery in its own currency and no change of mood is ascribed to God. The early authorities who kept the words in their apparent sense, al-Ālūsī notes, did not make a jest of God; they understood the matter as the humbling of the mockers at the moment their confidence collapses. The reason the verse can put it this way is that a jest aimed at the truth lands on the man who made it, and the One who answers it never speaks in play.

**WHERE THE LAUGHTER ENDS**

The report held for Ibn ʿAbbās fills in the scene. A gate of Paradise is opened for them in the hereafter and then shut in their faces, and the believers who were mocked in the world look on. The Qur'an states the reversal directly, (83:34 — **“But on that Day the believers will be laughing at the disbelievers”**). It also gives the reason they are left in their condition: their blindness in this world is the same refusal carried forward, because the man who will not see is not shown a second road.

Think of a man who spends his morning making jokes about a queue outside a doctor's door. He enjoys the laughter and takes his time, and in the middle of it his own name is called and missed. The joke did not change the illness and did not stop the queue; it only moved the man further down the line. That is the shape of the verse: the mockery was real, the answer is real, and the delay in the answer gave the mocker more rope rather than more time.

---

## Verse 2:16

> They are the ones who trade guidance for misguidance. But this trade is profitless, and they are not ˹rightly˺ guided

**A TRADE WITH THE WRONG GOODS**

The sūrah turns the picture into a transaction: ***“They are the ones”*** who have made a purchase, ***“who trade guidance for misguidance.”*** The report al-Suddī carries from Ibn ʿAbbās and Ibn Masʿūd explains the sense with plain words: they went after error and abandoned guidance. Mujāhid said they believed and then disbelieved, and Qatādah said they preferred deviation, as Ibn Kathīr records.

The comparison is drawn out by al-Saʿdī, who judges it the best of its kind. The lost road is set out as goods that a buyer covets, and guidance is set out as the price he pays for them. The man was holding something precious in his hand and used it to buy something worthless, and the loss is not the value of the goods received; it is the value of what he gave up.

The question a reader is most likely to ask opens the treatment by al-Ṭabarī: how could men buy error with guidance if they were never on the guidance to begin with? He works through the earliest authorities on the point, and their answers differ in wording while agreeing that something was in the men's hands and was let go, which keeps the verse from being a riddle about a purchase that never happened. A fine detail is recorded by al-Qurṭubī from Sibawayh about the vowel kept at the end of the verb, there so that a hearer does not take it for a word of another pattern, and the care spent on that detail shows how closely the sentence was studied. None of this softens the verdict. The bargain is drawn as an ordinary market scene because that is what it was: a man who knows the price and knows the goods, and who hands one over for the other with his eyes open.

**WHAT THE EXCHANGE COSTS**

The verdict comes in two parts. First, ***“But this trade is profitless”***, and then the second settles who they are: ***“and they are not ˹rightly˺ guided”***. The merchant's loss is total, al-Jalālayn notes, since his destination is the Fire and the gain he expected never arrives. The bargain, in Ibn ʿAbbās's report, did not prosper, and the man was not guided out of the error he had chosen.

The reason the loss was avoidable is explained by Maʿārif al-Qurʾān. These were men who knew both sides. They had grown up in a society of disbelief and knew what it contained, and they had lived long enough with the Muslims to see the difference clearly. Because they saw both and chose the cheaper one anyway, the trade was not made in ignorance; it was made for the sake of a small advantage.

The Qur'an warns against the same bargain in plainer terms elsewhere: (16:95 — **“And do not trade Allah’s covenant for a fleeting gain”**), and it describes the people who made the sale, (9:9 — **“They chose a fleeting gain over Allah’s revelations, hindering ˹others˺ from His Way”**). The measure in both places is the same. What is being sold is not a commodity but a relation with God, and what is taken for it is described as passing.

Think of a farmer who takes his seed grain to market and comes home with sweets. He has a pleasant evening, and the next season he has nothing to plant. The trade was real and the goods were real; the trouble is that what he gave away was the only thing that could have grown. Since the price and the goods are both known, the loss cannot be blamed on a mistake, and that is why the sūrah treats the bargain as a verdict on the man rather than on his information.

---

## Verse 2:17

> Their example is that of someone who kindles a fire, but when it lights up all around them, Allah takes away their light, leaving them in complete darkness—unable to see

**THE FIRE LIT FROM SOMEONE ELSE'S FLAME**

The last four verses of this passage hold up two parables, and The reason there are two is explained by Maʿārif al-Qurʾān. The hypocrites were not all one kind. Some had disbelief settled deep in them and only pretended for worldly reasons, and those are shown in the first picture; others had seen the truth and held back from it out of weakness, and those are shown in the second. The first picture begins with a man in the dark: ***“Their example is that of someone”*** who goes looking for light, ***“who kindles a fire”***.

The scene is followed closely by al-Saʿdī. The man is in a heavy darkness and needs the fire badly. He lights it from something else, since the flame is not his own property and was never prepared with him. The fire brightens what is around him, he sees the dangers of the place he is standing in, feels safe, and settles. Then the light goes and he is left with the fire's burning and none of its glow.

The application is exact. The hypocrites took their light from the believers, and it was never a quality of their own hearts. It bought them real benefits for a time: their lives were spared, their property was protected, and they had a share of the community's safety. Then the borrowed light was withdrawn, and what remained was the heat.

The construction of the comparison is noted by al-Ālūsī, who explains why the sentence stands on its own without being joined by a conjunction to what comes before it. The picture is a summary of the whole account rather than a new step inside it, and a summary is placed beside a story, not within it. Al-Qurṭubī works through the shape of the same words and draws the consequence that the likeness belongs to the men and the fire to the one they are likened to, which keeps the two sides of the comparison in order instead of running them together. The reading al-Ṭabarī gives treats the scene as light borrowed and then withdrawn, since the flame is lit from a source outside the man and never becomes his property. Once that is seen, the application follows without strain, because what the hypocrites took from the community was never theirs to keep. The reason the parable is built on a fire rather than on, say, a lamp is that fire gives light and burns at the same time, and the man who has no fuel of his own can be sure of only one of those.

**THE DARKNESS AFTER THE LIGHT**

What the withdrawal leaves behind is named in the middle of the verse: ***“but when it lights up all around them, Allah takes away their light, leaving them in complete darkness”***, and the last two words say what that means for the man inside it: ***“unable to see”***. The layers of dark that close over him are counted by al-Saʿdī: the night, the cloud, the rain, and the darkness that is worst of all because it came after light.

The report held for Ibn ʿAbbās makes the same point in concrete terms. A man lights a fire in the dark to guard his family and his property. The light rises and he feels safe, and at that moment the fire is put out. The hypocrites had the same experience with the community: their outward Islam made them safe while it lasted, and the safety was not built on anything inside them.

A second image for the same end belongs to the same family of pictures: (24:40 — **“like the darkness in a deep sea, covered by waves upon waves, topped by ˹dark˺ clouds”**). In both places the darkness is not the absence of a lamp somewhere in the world; it is the absence of a lamp in the hand.

Think of a traveller who borrows a flame at a door and walks a hundred paces by it. The road was never his and the flame was never his, and when the wind takes it he is standing in the same field with the same stones. What he knew by the light he can no longer see, and the knowledge does not help him find his footing. That is the position the verse describes: the guidance was real, the benefit was real, and neither of them belonged to the man who used them. Because the light was never his, its loss costs him nothing he owned and everything he depended on, which is why the darkness after the flash is worse than the darkness before it. The man's position changed, and the reason it changed is that he had been standing in a borrowed light. Since nothing he stood on was his own, the flash lit a road he could not keep.

---

## Verse 2:18

> They are ˹wilfully˺ deaf, dumb, and blind, so they will never return ˹to the Right Path˺

**THREE SENSES CLOSED BY THE MEN THEMSELVES**

The picture closes with three faculties named one after another. They are ***“They are ˹wilfully˺ deaf, dumb”*** and the sentence continues ***“and blind, so they will never return ˹to the Right Path˺”***. The adverb matters, because it fixes the condition as chosen rather than suffered: a man who cannot hear and a man who will not hear are in different positions.

All three are read as acts of pretence in the report held for Ibn ʿAbbās: they make themselves deaf, dumb and blind. Each faculty is assigned its own loss, and al-Jalālayn does it with care. They are deaf to the truth, so they cannot receive it; dumb when goodness is to be spoken of, so they cannot pass it on; and blind to the road of guidance, so they cannot see where they are going. Because each opening is closed, the whole person is shut off from the message in every direction it could have reached him.

The wording is looked at closely by al-Qurṭubī, who notes that the three descriptions stand as predicates of an implied subject, so that the sense reads: they are the deaf, they are the dumb, they are the blind. He also records a second reading, which keeps the three descriptions in a different case, the form a speaker uses when he lists a fault he means to condemn, and the difference shows how the earliest reciters carried the verse's weight in their voices. Al-Ṭabarī treats the sentence as a delayed statement whose meaning arrives before its place, since the loss described in the parable belongs to the hereafter, and the three closures are the state the men will be found in when their covering is gone. Al-Saʿdī gives each loss its object, which keeps the description from floating free: the deafness is a deafness to good, the dumbness a dumbness toward speaking it, and the blindness a blindness toward seeing the truth. The three descriptions, al-Ālūsī observes, are plurals of a form that names a fault as a settled condition rather than a passing accident, which is why they can be applied to the men as a class and not to a single bad afternoon. The last words carry the sharpest point in the reading al-Saʿdī gives, because these men left the truth after knowing it, and a man who abandons what he knows is further from return than one who never found it.

**THE ROAD BACK THAT THEY DO NOT TAKE**
