The verse ends with a statement about return: they will not come back from the error they are in. The sentence assumes a road exists, and that is the mercy hidden inside the description. The door is not locked from the far side; the travellers have simply decided that the road behind them is not worth walking.

The Qur'an repeats the three words together in other places, and each repetition sets them beside the way out. (6:39 — **“Those who deny Our signs are ˹wilfully˺ deaf and dumb—lost in darkness”**) puts the guided and the lost in the same verse and leaves the choice with His will. (17:97 — **“And We will drag them on their faces on the Day of Judgment—deaf, dumb, and blind”**) carries the same description into the hereafter, where the condition they chose in this life becomes visible to everyone.

Think of a man in a dark house who stops his own ears with his fingers, holds his tongue, and shuts his eyes. Then he stands still and complains that nobody has called him. The house had a door and the street had a voice in it, and the only thing standing between him and the road is his own decision to keep the three gates shut. Since the three gates were shut by the men themselves, the verse does not describe a misfortune; it describes a policy, and that is why the last word is about return rather than about rescue.

The three verses together form one lesson about what a man can do to himself. A borrowed light can be extinguished, a bad trade can be regretted, and a closed ear can be opened again, and the verse warns the reader while all three doors are still within reach.

---

## Verse 2:19

> Or ˹those caught in˺ a rainstorm from the sky with darkness, thunder, and lightning. They press their fingers into their ears at the sound of every thunder-clap for fear of death. And Allah encompasses the disbelievers ˹by His might˺

**THE SECOND PICTURE: A STORM OVERHEAD**

The second parable opens with a scene of weather: ***“Or ˹those caught in˺ a rainstorm from the sky with darkness, thunder”***, and the storm grows as the sentence goes on: ***“and lightning. They press their fingers into their ears at the sound of every thunder-clap”***. The difficult word for the downpour is explained by Baghawī as rain, and anything that comes down from above may carry it, because the sky in Arabic is everything that covers a person.

A small piece of grammar is recorded by al-Qurṭubī from the older commentators. The particle rendered as or can carry the sense of and, and al-Ṭabarī and al-Farrāʾ both read it that way here, so that the two parables are offered together rather than as a choice between them. He supports the reading with lines of Arabic poetry in which the same word carries that sense.

**FINGERS IN THE EARS**

The men in the storm do the only thing they can think of: ***“for fear of death.”*** They stop their ears against the sound overhead. The gesture is a picture of the hypocrites' relation to the Qur'an: the argument is loud and close, and their answer is to shut off the sense that would carry it in.

The storm is read by Ibn ʿAbbās as the Qur'an itself. The darkness is the passage of reports that carry fear and trouble; the thunder is the rebuke that shakes a listener; and the lightning is the exposition and the promise that flashes and shows the road. The older picture is given by al-Jalālayn: the thunder is the angel in charge of the clouds, or the sound of his voice that drives them, and the lightning is the flash that follows. Either way the storm is not random weather; it is a message with noise in it.

The second picture is read by al-Ālūsī as a deliberate follow-up to the first rather than a repetition of it. The fire showed men who lose a light they had briefly; the storm shows men who never hold still under the truth at all, taking a step while it flashes and stopping when it goes, so that the two pictures divide the hypocrites by how deeply the disease had set in. The application is kept by al-Saʿdī on the men rather than the weather: the one who shelters from a storm may still come out of it alive, while the men of this verse cannot, since the power whose signs they hide from holds them in its knowledge and its grasp. The two pictures are tied by Maʿārif al-Qurʾān to the two kinds of person the sūrah is describing, those whose rejection was settled and those whose hesitation was permanent, which is why the storm belongs to the second kind. A man who moves only when the sky lights up has already decided that the road is not worth walking in the dark, and that is the state the verse leaves him in.

**THE ONE WHO ENCOMPASSES THE STORM**

The verse closes on a line that ends the escape attempt: ***“And Allah encompasses the disbelievers ˹by His might˺”***. Fingers in the ears address the noise, and nobody addresses the storm. The power sending it holds everything the men are standing in, which means there is no shelter outside the One they are trying to shut out.

Think of a man caught in an open field when the sky breaks. He can press his palms against his ears and he will hear less, and the rain will soak him exactly as much. Covering the ears changes the volume of the message and nothing about the position of the man. That is the picture, and the next verse shows how long it lasts. Because the storm comes from the sky and not from the men, the only thing they control is how much of it they hear, which is why their whole answer in the verse is a gesture at their own ears. The verse gives them no shelter to run to and no argument to make, and that is why their response is written as a posture rather than a plea.

---

## Verse 2:20

> It is as if the lightning were about to snatch away their sight. Whenever lightning strikes, they walk in its light, but when darkness covers them, they stand still. Had Allah willed, He could have taken away their hearing and sight. Surely Allah is Most Capable of everything

**WALKING BY A BORROWED FLASH**

The storm continues: ***“It is as if the lightning were about to snatch away their sight.”*** The light is so bright that it threatens the eye that uses it. The men beneath it walk in fits: ***“Whenever lightning strikes, they walk in its light”***, and then ***“but when darkness covers them, they stand still”***. Movement and stillness alternate, and neither comes from a decision they made.

Ibn ʿAbbās explains the two states with precision. When the lightning flashes and the road is visible, the hypocrites go along with what the believers go along with, since their outward faith works while it is lit. When the dark comes back, they stop, because the appearance that carried them has nothing behind it. So the walking and the standing are the same man measured at two moments, not two different people.

Al-Jalālayn reads the whole picture as a portrait of what Qur'anic argument does inside such a heart. The verses bring truths they like to hear, and they move; then a truth they dislike comes, and they stop. Because the light they walk by is not their own, the pace of their journey depends on flashes rather than on a settled road.

**THE POWER THAT COULD END IT ALL**

The verse closes with a warning inside a statement of power: ***“Had Allah willed, He could have taken away their hearing”*** and ***“and sight. Surely Allah is Most Capable of everything”***. The men have already lost the sight of the heart, and the faculties they still hold are held on loan.

The grammar of the opening is examined by al-Qurṭubī, who explains the form of the verb as one that reports a thing coming close without arriving, so that the sentence holds the men under a threat that has not yet fallen. Why the hearing and the sight carry the closing warning, rather than any other faculty, is asked and answered by al-Ṭabarī: the two were named in the two parables just before, which means the sentence closes the account where it began. The last clause is read by al-Saʿdī as a threat of punishment in this life, held out so that the men might draw back before a heavier one arrives, and Ibn Kathīr takes the same words as a statement of the power that stands behind the whole passage. Maʿārif al-Qurʾān draws the lesson the two parables were built to leave: the hypocrites are not outside God's reach, and the light and the hearing they hold are held on loan. The reason the verse ends here rather than with a command is that the reader has been shown a condition and not offered an argument, and a condition is answered by turning back.

That is the last word of the passage, and it is the right one. Everything the hypocrite has — his hearing, his sight, his place in the community, the light of faith borrowed from the believers around him — is a gift he did not create, and a gift can be withdrawn. The safety of the man in the storm was never in his fingers.

Think of a man crossing a mountain road at night behind a car whose headlights he does not control. He walks when the road is lit and stops when the car pulls away, and he never once checks what he is walking on. The borrowed flash can show a path or hide a cliff, and the man who has no light of his own has no way of telling which one he is being shown.

The two parables close the portrait of the hypocrites, and the sūrah leaves them there in the dark. What follows turns to the whole of humanity with an invitation, and the reader carries the picture with him: the borrowed light, the closed ears, and the request for a share of someone else's lamp on the day when light is no longer for sale.

---

## Verse 2:21

> O humanity! Worship your Lord, Who created you and those before you, so that you may become mindful ˹of Him˺

**A CALL THAT GATHERS EVERY GROUP**

The sūrah has finished drawing its three portraits, and now the call goes out into the street: ***“O humanity!”*** The address is not restricted to the community of believers, and the reason is given by Maʿārif al-Qurʾān: the word for humanity covers man as such, so the verse gathers the believers, the hardened rejecters and the waverers into a single audience. The same point is made by Ibn ʿUthaymīn from the shape of the sūrah. This is the first verse of al-Baqarah in which anyone is addressed directly, and although the sūrah came down in Madinah, where most addresses are made to the believers, this one is opened to all people, and the same call is made in another sūrah: (7:158 — **“O humanity! I am Allah’s Messenger to you all”**).

The two addresses are given their settings in a report that al-Baghawī carries from Ibn ʿAbbās: ***“O humanity!”*** belongs to the verses that came down in Mecca, while the address to the believers belongs to the verses that came down after the migration. The rule is recorded by al-Qurṭubī from the earliest commentators, and he adds a qualification of his own: since al-Baqarah belongs to the later period and contains this very call, the rule describes the usual case rather than a law. A finer version of the same distinction is preserved by him as well. What concerns a penalty or an obligation came down in the later period, and what concerns the accounts of earlier peoples and their punishment came down in the earlier one. The reader therefore holds a description of the two settings, not a claim that a verse cannot travel.

**THE COMMAND AND ITS GROUND**

The sentence itself is an order: ***“Worship your Lord”***. Ibn ʿAbbās, in the report al-Baghawī carries, says that every occurrence of worship in the Qur'an carries the sense of professing the oneness of God, because the word in the Book is never only a ritual. The order is read by al-Ṭabarī as addressed to the groups the sūrah has just described — the rejecters whom no warning moves, the waverers who play both sides, and the believers — so that no one is left out of the command. The proof for the command is named in the same breath: ***“Who created you and those before you”***. The One who gave them their existence is the One whose claim on their obedience stands, which is why the words name the creation of their ancestors as well as their own.

The command is set by as-Saʿdī inside the purpose for which man was made. Worship in his reading is comprehensive: it means carrying out what God has ordered, keeping away from what He has forbidden, and believing what He has reported. He adds that God commanded His servants with the very thing He created them for, and he cites the verse where that purpose is stated plainly: (51:56 — **“I did not create jinn and humans except to worship Me”**). The lordship behind the command is therefore not a bare authority. It is the care that raised them, since the One who is asking is the One who has been providing.

**WHY THE ADDRESS ITSELF IS A GIFT**

Something in the wording is worth noticing, and al-Ālūsī points it out. After speaking about the three groups in the third person, God turns and speaks to them directly. The shift shakes them toward listening, turns their hearts toward receiving, and pays for the effort that worship costs with the sweetness of being addressed. A further observation belongs to Maʿārif al-Qurʾān: the two verses do not only affirm, they argue. The claims are supported by evidence so clear that an ordinary person who uses his reason cannot help being convinced that God is one.

The last words carry the aim: ***“so that you may become mindful ˹of Him˺”***. What the particle carries is noted by al-Jalālayn. It is a particle of hope, and hope is what a creature may hold; but when God speaks it, the wish it expresses becomes a certainty. A servant who answers the command does not merely escape a punishment. He arrives at the state the verse names, which the same commentator describes as being kept in a covering and a protection from the wrath of God.

**THE RESPONSE THE HOUSE ASKS FOR**

Think of a man who wakes in a house he has no memory of entering. The fire is lit, bread is warm on the table, the roof was repaired by hands he never saw, and the road outside was cleared before he arrived. He owns nothing in the room, and everything in it was arranged for him. The fitting response is not a payment, because he has nothing to pay with; it is the acknowledgement that he is a guest in a house whose owner was kind before he was asked. That is the position the verse describes. The call to worship comes after the gift and not before it, so the command arrives as a description of the fitting answer rather than as a tax laid on a stranger.

The verse therefore reaches the reader in the middle of a life he did not start. His existence was given to him, and so was the existence of everyone who came before him. Worship here is the answer that the gift itself asks for, and the last phrase tells him what the answer produces: a man mindful of God, kept inside a fence he did not build.

---

## Verse 2:22

> ˹He is the One˺ Who has made the earth a place of settlement for you and the sky a canopy; and sends down rain from the sky, causing fruits to grow as a provision for you. So do not knowingly set up equals to Allah ˹in worship˺

**THE SENTENCE THAT CONTINUES THE FIRST**

This verse does not open a new argument; it carries on the description of the Lord who was named in the call before it. Al-Ṭabarī explains how the two are joined: the second relative clause returns to the first, so that the passage reads as one sentence — worship your Lord, your Creator and the Creator of those before you, the One who made the earth a bed for you, and who sent down water from the sky. The command and its evidence are one move, because the man being asked to obey is being shown the hand that has been arranging his life since before he could ask for anything.

**A BED, A ROOF, AND WATER**

The verse opens with a pointer before the gift is named: ***“˹He is the One˺ Who has made the earth a place of settlement for you”***. Al-Jalālayn fills the picture in. The earth is spread out like a carpet that is neither too hard to stand on nor too soft to give under a step, which is why a man can build a life on it at all. The second clause follows: ***“and the sky a canopy”***. The same commentator reads the sky here as a roof over the dwelling, so that the two halves of the sentence give the reader a floor and a ceiling.

The act of creation behind the gift is set in order by al-Baghawī. Creation is the bringing of a thing into being with no model before it, and the One who did that for the first generation can do it for the last. as-Saʿdī counts what the two gifts contain. The earth is a floor men settle on, build upon, plough and cross from place to place. The sky holds the sun, the moon and the stars, which no household can do without. Provision, in his reading, is the thing by which a man is fed, kept alive and given pleasure along the way.

**THE SUPPLY THAT ARRIVES DAILY**

The verse continues: ***“and sends down rain from the sky, causing fruits to grow as a provision for you”***. Al-Qurṭubī pauses over the word used for making, which can carry the sense of turning a thing into something else, since the earth was not always a floor for men and the fruit was not always food. The observation matters for the argument, because what is being described is a continuing arrangement rather than a single act in the distant past. The rain still falls, and the harvest still arrives on the table of people who never planted a tree.

**THE ONE THING THE GIFTS FORBID**

The verse closes with a prohibition built into the description: ***“So do not knowingly set up equals to Allah ˹in worship˺”***. as-Saʿdī explains the word for equals as peers and counterparts taken from among created things, honoured as God is honoured and loved as He is loved. Maʿārif al-Qurʾān draws the conclusion the three gifts were arranged to produce. Once a man has understood that God alone creates and provides, he has also understood that nobody else can deserve the worship that belongs to the One who keeps him alive. The point is not that a rival exists to be found. The point is where the credit is placed.

The adverb in the command is the part that closes the door on excuse, since knowingly is not the same as in ignorance. The people addressed were not being asked to discover a fact they had never met. They knew who had made them and fed them, so the sin named here is the act of a man who sets the rival in place with his eyes open. That is why the verse states the prohibition as something the men do rather than as something they believe.

**WHAT A GUEST DOES AT A TABLE**

Think of a man invited to a table he did not pay for. The bread was baked by hands he cannot see, the water was drawn from a well he did not dig, and the garden outside was planted before he was born. If he begins to thank the servant who carried the plates and gives the credit for the meal to the man who laid the cloth, his mistake is not a small one of manners; it misplaces the whole debt. That is the error the verse forbids. The gifts are real and the thanks are owed, and the only question the verse cares about is who actually sent them.

The passage goes on to show where the mistake leads. A community that credits its provision to someone other than the Provider has cut the cord between the gift and the giver, and the claim that the Book is not from God belongs to the same cut.

---

## Verse 2:23

> And if you are in doubt about what We have revealed to Our servant, then produce a sûrah like it and call your helpers other than Allah, if what you say is true

**THE CHALLENGE AND WHY IT IS FAIR**

The Book opens its case at this point, and Maʿārif al-Qurʾān marks the structure of the passage. The guidance of the Qur'an rests on two principles, the oneness of God and the prophethood of His messenger. The verses before this one established the first by naming what only God does; this verse and the next establish the second by putting the Book itself on the table. The challenge begins with the doubt it answers: ***“And if you are in doubt about what We have revealed to Our servant”***. The audience is identified by al-Ṭabarī. The words are spoken to the polytheists of Arabia and to the disbelievers among the two communities that had received books before them, and the doubt named is uncertainty about what came down on Muhammad ﷺ.

The condition attached to the challenge is what makes it fair: ***“then produce a sûrah like it”***. Two notes belong here, and al-Jalālayn supplies both. A sūrah in the usage of the Qur'an is a passage with a beginning and an end, and the shortest of them is three verses. To produce something like the Book means to match it in its eloquence, in its arrangement, and in what it reports of things no reader could have known. So the demand is not for a page of fine Arabic; it is for a page that carries the same weight in every direction at once, which is why the smallest sūrah is enough to make the test real.

**THE HELPERS ARE CALLED IN**

The verse then widens the help available: ***“and call your helpers other than Allah”***, and it closes with its own condition, ***“if what you say is true”***. as-Saʿdī draws out the comparison the verse is making. The claim was that the Book was invented, and the reply is that the men making it spoke the language at its height, had every motive to succeed, and had no shortage of allies to call on. If the claim were true, producing one sūrah would have been simple for them, because all they had to do was bring their poets and their gods and match a passage.

The purpose of the challenge is worth naming, since the words read like a contest and the point is not the contest. Al-Ālūsī observes that the verse was revealed to show the inimitability of the Qur'an, but its aim is to establish the prophethood of the man who brought it. The inability of a skilled and hostile audience to produce the like is the evidence, and the silence of the men who knew the language best is louder than their claim. Al-Qurṭubī adds a note on the word used for servant. The naming honours the man it describes, since servitude to God is the highest of ranks, and the Book that could not be imitated came down on a servant rather than on a king.

**WHAT THE CHALLENGE TAKES OFF THE TABLE**

The invitation leaves no room for the excuse a sceptic usually keeps in reserve. Nobody is asked to accept a claim on the word of another person, since the thing claimed is placed in the reader's own hand. A man who doubts the Book is invited to write its equal, and the test is not hidden inside a scholar's library. It is the sort of test a man can run with the tools of his own trade, which is why the challenge was aimed at a people whose trade was speech. Because the test is public, the failure to meet it is public as well, and that is why the verse can leave the verdict to the silence of the men who were asked.

**A TABLE OF POETS AT A WEDDING**

Think of a town of famous poets invited to a wedding and told that one line of the banquet song was written by a man they do not respect. They laugh at it, and the host offers them the table, the pens and the evening: write one line to match it, and the laughter will stand. The offer lasts all night. The pens move, the poems are spoken, and the line they mocked is not matched, because the thing they were asked to imitate was never made by a man they could imitate.

The verses that follow do not soften the offer and do not take it back. They turn instead to what waits for the men who will not answer, and the next verse puts that scene in front of them.

---

## Verse 2:24

> But if you are unable to do so—and you will never be able to do so—then fear the Fire fuelled with people and stones, which is prepared for the disbelievers

**THE ANSWER THEY WILL NOT PRODUCE**

The challenge closes with a conclusion, and al-Ālūsī notes the connective that opens it. The sentence is drawn out of what came before, so the words stand as the verdict of the test rather than as a fresh demand. What the test proved is filled in by al-Ṭabarī. If the men could not bring a sūrah like it, then they and the helpers they called on had spent everything they had and still come short, and their failure had become a matter of their own experience rather than of anyone's claim about them. A man who has tried and been unable does not need to be told that he was unable. The verse sets that failure in front of them in its opening words: ***“But if you are unable to do so”***, and the next breath closes the door: ***“and you will never be able to do so”***.

The reading in al-Jalālayn takes the second half as a parenthesis laid inside the command. The failure of the present attempt is stated, the impossibility of any future attempt is stated beside it, and then the warning follows. The order matters, because the men are being told that the way out they are counting on will never be opened.

**A WARNING BUILT ON A GUARANTEE**

The certainty of that forecast is what gives the warning its edge, and how the two halves of time are divided is worked through by al-Qurṭubī. The first clause, in his reading, covers what has already passed: whatever attempt the men made has failed. The second covers everything still to come, since nothing they produce later will succeed. He also records a second way of taking the passage, in which the sound of the sentence falls earlier and the sense runs: call your helpers besides God if what you say is true — and you will never do it. Both readings arrive at the same floor beneath the feet of the men addressed.

What kind of statement is it that can say of a whole people that they will never manage a thing? Fourteen centuries have passed since the sentence was spoken. Better speakers than the first audience have come after it, and the sentence still stands. A claim about the future that no later generation has broken is not a rhetorical flourish, because what it describes is the status of the Book itself, and the verse lays that claim down in front of the men on whom the test was first made.

**FUEL THAT IS NOT WOOD**

What waits for them is named next: ***“then fear the Fire”***, the fire ***“fuelled with people and stones”***, ***“which is prepared for the disbelievers”***. The reading in al-Jalālayn fills in the fuel. The people are the disbelievers themselves, and the stones are the idols they set beside God, so that the very things they trusted are gathered into the fire that punishes the trust. The difference between that fire and the one men know is drawn by as-Saʿdī. A fire in this world is fed with wood and dies when the wood is done; this one is of another order altogether, and it is called prepared, which means it is already made ready for the people who will arrive at it. Ibn Kathīr notes who the address covers: the pagans of Arabia, and the Jews and the Christians besides, since the challenge was made to everyone who doubted what was sent down.

Maʿārif al-Qurʾān places the verse inside the argument of the passage. Verses 21 and 22 proved the oneness of God by naming what only He does; verses 23 and 24 prove the prophethood by putting the Book itself to the test, and the fire named at the end is the answer to a refusal of that proof. The two halves of the message are therefore joined, because the God who is one is the God who sent the Book, and a rejection of either one lands at the same door.

**THE BRIDGE AND THE RIVER**

Think of a company of engineers invited to build a bridge beside one that already stands, with the same stone, the same river and the same season. They laugh at the structure, accept the contract, and the river carries away their scaffolding. The scene is not a story about weak men; it is a story about a thing that was not built by hands like theirs. That is the shape of the challenge at the end of this passage, and the fire named after it is the reason the men are told to fear rather than simply to read.

---

## Verse 2:25

> Give good news ˹O Prophet˺ to those who believe and do good that they will have Gardens under which rivers flow. Whenever provided with fruit, they will say, “This is what we were given before,” for they will be served fruit that looks similar ˹but tastes different˺. They will have pure spouses, and they will be there forever

**GOOD NEWS AFTER A WARNING**

The sūrah turns from the warning to the welcome, and the reason is stated by al-Ālūsī. After the end of the disbelievers has been described, and nothing in the passage is a sharper warning than that, the believers and their reward are named next, because the habit of this Book is to pair encouragement with the threat. as-Saʿdī gives the same reason from the other side: a servant is meant to be drawn by hope and held by fear, and a message that only frightened him would produce a creature who runs away rather than one who comes forward. The command is addressed to the Messenger and to everyone who takes his place in carrying the message.

**THE WORD FOR GLAD TIDINGS**

The order is given with a specific word: ***“Give good news ˹O Prophet˺”***. Its sense is set out by al-Ṭabarī. The report carries news that brings joy to the man who hears it, especially when the one who brings it arrives before anyone else with it. Al-Baghawī adds that the ancient usage covers every truthful report by which the skin of a face changes, and that the word was used for bad news as well as good, though good news is its common use; the verse puts it to that use. The same picture inside the word is noticed by al-Qurṭubī, since the noun for glad tidings comes from the skin that alters colour when a man hears what matters to him.

The recipients are named with two conditions: ***“to those who believe and do good”***. The pair is read by as-Saʿdī as the whole person engaged. Faith is the work of the heart and good deeds are the work of the limbs, so the second confirms the first. A claim to faith that leaves the hands idle is not the thing this verse announces a reward for, because the verse does not describe a mood; it describes a life.

**THE GARDENS AND THE RIVERS**

The promise itself is named next: ***“that they will have Gardens under which rivers flow”***. Al-Jalālayn supplies a piece of the picture. What is named is the channel through which water flows, and it carries that name because the water carves its way along it, so what the verse describes is water already in place and moving. The point of the image, therefore, is not a garden with a stream running past its wall. It is a garden whose roots are in water.

**THE FAMILIAR FRUIT THAT IS NOT THE SAME**

A strange sentence follows, and it is one of the most discussed in the sūrah: ***“Whenever provided with fruit, they will say, “This is what we were given before,””***. The reason they can say it is given in the next words: ***“for they will be served fruit that looks similar ˹but tastes different˺”***. How the resemblance works is explained in al-Jalālayn. The fruits match one another in colour and form while differing in taste, so that a settled resident recognises what is set before him and is not a stranger at his own table. Maʿārif al-Qurʾān records the two ways the commentators took it, some holding that the fruits of Paradise are alike in shape and differ in taste each time they are served, and others that they resemble the fruits of the earth in shape alone. The point both readings share is that the joy of the garden keeps renewing itself, and that these fruits share a name with the fruits of the earth while belonging to a different nature.

The verse is read by Ibn Kathīr inside the sūrah's pattern of pairing. After the punishment prepared for the enemies of God is described, the state of His loyal servants is named, and this back-and-forth between warning and promise is the reason the sūrah itself carries the name of the repeated one. The two ends are meant to be seen side by side, because a message delivered with only one of them would leave half the man untouched.

**SPOUSES AND A HOME THAT REMAINS**

The last two clauses close the picture: ***“They will have pure spouses”***, ***“and they will be there forever”***. The purity named is explained in al-Jalālayn as freedom from the bodily impurities that belong to this life, so that the companionship of the garden is not the companionship of a world that wears out. The final words carry the greatest promise of the verse: not arriving somewhere good, but staying in it. A life's worth of effort is spent in this world on things that end, and what is announced here is a home where the ending has been removed.

**THE DISH THAT LOOKS FAMILIAR**

Think of a traveller who arrives in a new country and is served a dish that looks exactly like the one his mother used to make. The shape of it reassures him, and then he eats, and the taste is something he has never met. The welcome is in the familiarity and the surprise is in the flavour. That is the structure of the verse: the gardens are made recognisable to the people who enter them, and then everything in them exceeds what the recognition promised.

---

## Verse 2:26

> Surely Allah does not shy away from using the parable of a mosquito or what is even smaller. As for the believers, they know that it is the truth from their Lord. And as for the disbelievers, they argue, “What does Allah mean by such a parable?” Through this ˹test˺, He leaves many to stray, and guides many. And He leaves none to stray except the rebellious—

**THE OBJECTION THE PARABLE PROVOKED**

The verse was revealed to answer a complaint, and al-Ṭabarī gathers the reports that give the complaint its setting. When parables were struck in this sūrah for the hypocrites, the fire that goes out and the rainstorm with its thunderclaps, some of those who heard them said that the Lord of the worlds is far above such comparisons, and these words came down in reply. The report al-Qurṭubī carries from Ibn ʿAbbās, in the narration of Abū Ṣāliḥ, places the grumbling directly after the two parables of fire and rain. al-Baghawī records the same kind of objection being raised when the parables of the fly and the spider were struck in other passages of the Book, and al-Ālūsī reports a group among the Jews who thought the majesty of God put Him above comparisons drawn from contemptible creatures.

The answer opens without apology: ***“Surely Allah does not shy away from using the parable of a mosquito”***. The language of shame is used so that no hearer can miss the rebuke hidden inside the complaint, because refusing an example is finally a refusal to be taught. as-Saʿdī reads the words that way: God is not ashamed of the truth, a parable carries wisdom, and the comparison belongs to God's teaching of His servants. Since the objection was really an objection to instruction, the verse does not negotiate with it.

**THE SMALLEST THING IN THE HAND**

A second report in al-Ṭabarī, from al-Rabīʿ b. Anas, reads the mosquito as a similitude of this world. The insect lives while it is hungry and dies when it is filled, which is the measure of a life built on appetite. The phrase that follows stretches the comparison further: ***“or what is even smaller.”*** Ibn Kathīr explains the sense as covering what is above the creature as well as what is below it, so the argument's reach includes the largest things a man can see and the smallest he cannot. The reason a mosquito can serve where a mountain would not is that the value of a comparison lies in the truth it carries, and a small creature carries a large truth as easily as a large one carries a small.

**TWO ANSWERS TO THE SAME WORDS**

The verse then divides its hearers: ***“As for the believers, they know”***, and what they know is ***“that it is the truth from their Lord.”*** as-Saʿdī reads the difference between the two groups as a difference in what they carry to the sentence. Faith receives a parable as a door into meaning, while refusal looks for a handle by which to lift it away. Because what a man brings decides what he takes, the same words produce knowledge in one hearer and an argument in another.

The argument is then quoted: ***“And as for the disbelievers, they argue”***, and it is shaped as a question, ***“What does Allah mean by such a parable?”*** The Book has already used this small creature to expose what men set beside God: (22:73 — **“can never create ˹so much as˺ a fly”**), even when every helper they have gathers for the attempt. A man who cannot make a fly has little standing to grade the examples God chooses for him.

**WHO IS LEFT TO STRAY**

The last phrases state what the parable does to both parties: ***“Through this ˹test˺, He leaves many to stray”***, and then ***“and guides many. And He leaves none to stray except the rebellious”***. The people named at the end are described in the verse that follows, and al-Ṭabarī joins the two sentences by reading the participle as those whose breaking of the covenant comes next. The reason the straying is attached to a particular kind of person is that the verse is describing a consequence rather than a trap. What follows from that is a rule the reader can hold: the parable is not the cause of the loss, because it found the loss already there. Since the picture is drawn from the smallest creature a man can see, no hearer can claim that the example stood beyond his reach.

Think of a physician who hands two patients the same pill. One takes it as the answer to his illness. The other turns it over, dislikes its size, and throws it away. The medicine did not change between the two hands, and the outcome was decided before either man swallowed.

---

## Verse 2:27

> those who violate Allah’s covenant after it has been affirmed, break whatever ˹ties˺ Allah has ordered to be maintained, and spread corruption in the land. It is they who are the ˹true˺ losers

**THE PEOPLE NAMED AT THE END OF THE PARABLE**

The verse carries on the portrait without starting a new one, and in the reading al-Ṭabarī gives, the description belongs to the same people as the rebellious mentioned a moment before. The grammatical placement is examined by al-Qurṭubī, who records that the relative clause either describes those rebels directly or stands as the predicate of an implied subject, and both readings land on the same group. Since the verse describes a condition rather than an incident, the reader is being shown the anatomy of a failure.

**THE COVENANT AND WHAT BREAKING IT MEANS**

The first charge is stated at once: ***“those who violate Allah’s covenant after it”*** ***“has been affirmed”***. al-Baghawī explains the sense with two words: the violators oppose and abandon. He also answers the question of which covenant is meant, and his answer takes the reader back to the day the whole of humanity was asked, (7:172 — **“Am I not your Lord?”**), and answered. Other authorities he cites refer it to the pledges written into the earlier Books, which carried the mark of the Messenger still to come. al-Jalālayn takes the same road, reading the covenant as the contract God made in the revealed Books: faith in the Prophet, and the ties of kinship besides.

That the covenant is affirmed before it is broken is the part of the sentence that carries the rebuke, because a man cannot break what he never bound. The affirmation is what makes the charge betrayal rather than ignorance, so the verse is aimed at the point where knowledge met intention.

**THE TIES A COMMUNITY RUNS ON**

The second charge follows: ***“break whatever ˹ties˺ Allah has ordered to be maintained”***. Maʿārif al-Qurʾān draws out the reach of that phrase: the bond between God and His servant, the bonds between a man and his parents, his relatives, his neighbours, his friends, and the wider ties within the community of believers. al-Jalālayn names kinship among them. The verse therefore does not describe a private failure of belief, because what it names is a man who has cut the threads a town lives on.

Here the language note belongs, and it is al-Baghawī's. The Arabic root behind that word gives the sense of a breaking that parts a thing from itself, the way a stick is snapped across. A covenant in the hand of a man is like that stick: it holds weight only while it is whole.

**WHAT THE LOSS COSTS**

The third charge widens the picture: ***“and spread corruption in the land.”*** That phrase has already appeared in this sūrah, where the corrupters answered a warning with a slogan about reform, and the charge returns here attached to the same temper. The verdict closes the verse: ***“It is they who are the ˹true˺ losers”***. The same three-fold failure is stated in another sūrah in almost the same words, and it ends there with a verdict of its own, (13:25 — **“it is they who will be condemned”**), and the worst abode besides. A loss measured against eternity is not a bad season that passes.

The order inside the verse is worth noticing, since the covenant is broken first, the ties are cut next, and the corruption in the land follows. Because the damage runs in that sequence, repair has to travel back through it, and a community is not restored by mending its streets before its promises. Since the ties are named as things God ordered to be maintained, cutting them is more than unkindness: it is disobedience to the One who joined them. That is why the loss is named last, because it is the end of a road that began with a word given and withdrawn.

Think of a bridge put up by a village where every family promised to keep one span in repair. One household lets its beams rot. The spans on either side begin to carry what they were never made to carry. The day the crossing goes down, every home on the far bank is cut off, and no one who neglected a beam had meant to destroy the road.

---

## Verse 2:28

> How can you deny Allah? You were lifeless and He gave you life, then He will cause you to die and again bring you to life, and then to Him you will ˹all˺ be returned

**A QUESTION THAT IS NOT ASKING FOR INFORMATION**

The sentence opens with a question, ***“How can you deny Allah?”***, and it is not asking for information. as-Saʿdī reads it as a question of amazement, rebuke and denial at once, aimed at people who were denying a Creator whose work was standing in front of them. al-Qurṭubī examines the construction of the question word, and the point he draws is that it asks about a condition rather than a fact, which is why the interrogative belongs here with the force of astonishment. The address, in the reading al-Jalālayn gives, is directed at the people of Mecca, whose unbelief persisted in the face of evidence they could not deny. The reason the question carries amazement rather than argument is that the facts it points to were never in dispute.

**FOUR STAGES IN ONE HAND**

The proof is then set out in stages: ***“You were lifeless and He gave you life”***. al-Jalālayn fills the first stage in: a drop in the loins, then life breathed into the body, first in the womb and then in the world. as-Saʿdī takes the same words as a reminder that the beginning was nothing, since a man was brought out of absence without any contribution of his own.

The stages continue: ***“then He will cause you to die”***, ***“and again bring you to life”***, ***“and then to Him you will ˹all˺ be returned”***. as-Saʿdī reads death here as the end of the appointed term and the beginning of the interval of the grave, and the second life as the raising that follows it, after which the fullest recompense is handed out. The Qur'an states the same sequence in another sūrah and adds the confession of the people who lived it: (22:66 — **“gave you life, then will cause you to die, and then will bring you back to life”**). Here the fourth stage is the return itself, and everything the argument has built ends at that door.

**WHY THE STAGES SETTLE THE ARGUMENT**

A man who has been carried through four stages by a power he did not hire has already met the One he is denying, and that is the shape of the proof. Ibn Kathīr reads the passage as a testimony to God's existence and to His work as Creator and Provider, since the life a person uses is not a thing he made. Maʿārif al-Qurʾān places the two verses of this passage among the blessings named at the turn of the sūrah, where the proof and the gift arrive together.

The reason the argument is laid out as a life story rather than an abstraction is that no hearer is a stranger to the story. Every man has watched someone die and has woken up alive himself, so the evidence is not a theory he must accept; it is an account of what has already happened to him. That is why the question at the head of the verse can be asked with amazement, since the answer was in the man's own chest from the first day. Because the last stage returns him to the One who began the sequence, the argument ends where a person must give his account. Since the story told here is the story of every hearer, the question at the head of the verse can be put to any man who reads it.

Think of a man who has been carried from a house to a market and back again by a bearer he never thanked. He was asleep for the first part of the journey and does not remember being lifted, and on the way home he announces that he walked the whole road himself. The denial costs him nothing to say, and the one who carried him knows how the journey really went.

---

## Verse 2:29

> He is the One Who created everything in the earth for you. Then He turned towards the heaven, forming it into seven heavens. And He has ˹perfect˺ knowledge of all things

**THE EARTH PUT UNDER MAN'S CHARGE**

The verse opens with a pointer: ***“He is the One”***, and the gift it names is ***“Who created everything in the earth”*** — everything, and the last word of that phrase gives the reason: ***“for you.”*** as-Saʿdī reads the sentence as a statement of bounty and mercy, since what was made was made for benefit, enjoyment and reflection. He draws a further point from the way it is phrased: because the verse arrives in the mode of favour, the default in created things is permission and purity, and what is harmful is excluded by the very purpose the verse announces.

The argument runs from the small scale to the large one. A man who is fed by the earth and sheltered by what grows on it is not a stranger dropped into a place he has no claim to, because the place was furnished before he arrived. The sense is that the whole world is called a provision rather than a scene, and since the earth was furnished before the man arrived, the gift is older than his gratitude.

**THE TURN TOWARDS THE HEAVEN**

The next sentence names a second act: ***“Then He turned towards the heaven, forming it into seven heavens.”*** al-Ṭabarī works carefully through the verb, and the reading he settles on is a turning towards, a direction of attention, supported by lines of poetry in which the same expression carries that sense. The heavens themselves are counted by al-Jalālayn, who notes that the pronoun returns to the heaven named just before and that the making covers the plural that follows. Ibn Kathīr reads the pair of clauses, earth and heaven, as evidence of the power that produced them, since the One who arranged both is the One whose claim is being argued.

The number is stated plainly, and the same number is used elsewhere without argument: (41:12 — **“So He formed the heaven into seven heavens in two Days”**), and again where the layers are described, (67:3 — **“created seven heavens, one above the other”**). What the verse is doing, therefore, is not describing an architecture for its own sake. It is naming the size of the house whose owner has been making a claim on the guest.

**KNOWLEDGE THAT CLOSES THE ARGUMENT**

The verse ends with a line that reaches back over everything in it: ***“And He has ˹perfect˺ knowledge of all things”***. The order matters, because the One who spread a table for a creature knows what the creature needs, and the One who arranged seven heavens knows what stands in them. Maʿārif al-Qurʾān notes how small the phrase is and how much it gathers, since the benefits a man draws from the earth and its produce are named in general terms and the knowledge behind them is named at the end.

Because provision and knowledge are stated in one breath, gratitude in this verse is not a transaction with a distant power. It is the fitting response of a guest to a host who both made the room and knows the guest's condition. That is why the verse places knowledge after generosity: the man who eats the fruit is told that the One who grew it knows him.

Think of a traveller who arrives at a house to find the lamp lit, the bed aired, and the food covered on the table before anyone heard him knock. Nothing in the room is his, and everything in it was arranged for him. The gratitude the verse asks for is not a payment for the meal; it is the honest naming of whose house he is standing in.

---

## Verse 2:30

> ˹Remember˺ when your Lord said to the angels, “I am going to place a successive ˹human˺ authority on earth.” They asked ˹Allah˺, “Will You place in it someone who will spread corruption there and shed blood while we glorify Your praises and proclaim Your holiness?” Allah responded, “I know what you do not know.”

**THE ANNOUNCEMENT MADE TO THE ANGELS**

The verse is framed as a recollection: ***“˹Remember˺ when your Lord said to the angels”***, and what is remembered is an announcement, ***“I am going to place a successive ˹human˺ authority on earth.”*** al-Jalālayn opens up the term for that authority: a deputy who takes the place of another and carries out the rulings of the One who appointed him, and the person meant is Adam. al-Qurṭubī examines the time particle that opens the sentence, since it points to a past event being recalled for the reader, and he gathers the reports about who the angels were told would be placed there. as-Saʿdī notes the order of the story: God told the angels about the appointment before the man was made, so the announcement itself is part of the account. Ibn Kathīr renders the appointment as the placing of generations after generations on the earth, which means the question the angels asked was about a long future. The particle that opens the recollection receives a long treatment from al-Ṭabarī, who gathers what the grammarians of the language said about it.

That word also holds a promise. A deputy is appointed for a task, which means the earth is not a lost place to be abandoned but a place with work in it, and the One who appoints is the One who will judge how the work is done.

**THE QUESTION THE ANGELS ASKED**

The angels respond with a question: ***“They asked ˹Allah˺, “Will You place in it someone”***, and their words name two harms, ***“who will spread corruption there”***, ***“and shed blood while we glorify Your praises”***, before the sentence closes with ***“and proclaim Your holiness?”*** as-Saʿdī reads the question as the speech of creatures reasoning from what they know, and his explanation is that they spoke on an assumption about what such a deputy would do. He also notes the order inside their words: the general corruption is named first and the bloodshed after it, because killing is the heaviest form the ruin can take.

Since the announcement came before the creation, the objection was raised inside a plan the angels could not see. The older reports fill in where the assumption came from. al-Jalālayn records the view that the jinn had lived on the earth before, and had shed blood there, so the angels expected the new deputy to be of the same pattern. Their own service is stated in the same breath, and this is the part of the question worth holding: they were not setting their record against God's wisdom so much as placing their own worship beside the ruin they feared.

**AN ANSWER ABOUT WHAT IS KNOWN**

The reply ends the conversation in four words: ***“Allah responded, “I know what you do not know.””*** What was known and hidden from them is set out by as-Saʿdī, and his list is long. God knew that the good to come from this creation would outweigh the harm by many times: prophets, truthful people, martyrs and the righteous would be drawn from it, the signs of God would appear, kinds of worship would exist that could not exist without it, the testing would separate the sincere from the false, and what was hidden in the enemy of God would be brought into the open. The reason the answer is a statement about knowledge rather than a defence of the appointment is that the angels were asking about outcomes they could not see. Because the reply is about knowledge, it tells the reader that the objection itself was known before it was spoken.

Maʿārif al-Qurʾān sets the passage inside the sūrah's purpose, since the story of Adam opens here as an illustration of the blessings named just before, both the visible and the hidden. A reader who has followed the argument from the earth and its provision arrives at this scene with the question already in his hand: what is this creature for? The answer given to the angels is the answer given to him, and it leaves the future with the One who knows it.

Think of a school whose staff object when the head announces a new class of pupils. They know what children of that age can do to a quiet corridor, and they are not wrong about the noise. The head does not argue about the noise. He says that he knows which of those children will become the teachers, and since he is the one who will read the examination papers, his knowledge is the only one that covers the end of the story.

---

## Verse 2:31

> He taught Adam the names of all things, then He presented them to the angels and said, “Tell Me the names of these, if what you say is true?”

**THE TEACHING THAT CAME BEFORE THE TEST**

The scene turns from the appointment to the preparation, and the verse states it plainly: ***“He taught Adam the names of all things”***. What the teaching covered is opened up by as-Saʿdī, and his answer is careful. Adam was taught the names of the things and the things named, the words and the meanings together, down to the small forms of a word beside the large, so that the knowledge was not a list recited but a grasp of the world. al-Qurṭubī reads the verb as making known, and records the view that this knowledge was placed in Adam directly, with the alternative that an angel was the means of it. al-Jalālayn reads the teaching as knowledge of the names placed into Adam's heart, and notes that most of what was shown before the angels were beings with reason. Since the knowledge came by teaching and not by discovery, the honour of it belongs to the teacher. al-Ṭabarī gathers the reports that surround the passage, including the account of the angel of death taking clay from the surface of the earth to shape the man, and the material he collects shows how the earliest narrators filled in the scene.

Maʿārif al-Qurʾān draws a wide conclusion from the same phrase. Since the teacher here is God, language itself belongs to what God created rather than to what man invented, and the many tongues spoken in the world are the later history of a capacity that came with the first man. The point matters for the story, because the test that follows is a test of knowledge, and knowledge of names is the door to every kind of knowledge after it.

**THE OFFERING AND THE QUESTION**

The next step is a presentation: ***“then He presented them to the angels”***, and the words that follow turn it into a challenge, ***“and said, “Tell Me the names of these”***. as-Saʿdī explains what was placed in front of them: the beings whose names Adam had been taught, brought out as a test to show whether the angels could identify them. The display is therefore not a lesson for the angels but an answer to the position they had taken, since the phrase that closes the verse attaches the test to a claim, ***“if what you say is true?”***, which in his reading means: true in your judgement that you are better than this deputy who is to be placed there.

The reason the test is fair is that it asks for exactly what the angels had implied they possessed, and the sūrah has already recorded the claim the words answer, (2:30 — **“I know what you do not know”**). They had set their own record of worship beside the expected ruin of the earth, and the reply puts a question to their knowledge rather than to their devotion. A claim about superiority is a claim about what a creature can do, and the verse asks them to show their work.

**WHY THE SCENE IS PLACED HERE**

The placement inside the sūrah is deliberate, and Maʿārif al-Qurʾān treats the whole account as an illustration of the gifts named before it. A reader who has just been told that the earth and its produce were made for him is now shown the first man receiving the key to all of it, which is the knowledge of what things are. The reason the story is told with such care about order is that each step answers the question raised by the angels: the appointment is announced, the preparation is named, the test is set, and the answer follows.

There is also a lesson in the shape of the test for anyone who takes himself as the measure of what God should do. The angels spoke from what they had, and what they had was less than what God knew. A creature's argument about the unknown is always built out of the corner of the unknown that has been shown to him, and the verse lets the reader see how small that corner is.

Think of a master calligrapher who brings his apprentice to a gathering of skilled readers. He sets before them a page of writing and asks them to name the hand that made it. They are masters of the pen themselves, and they cannot answer, because reading a hand is a craft and the apprentice has been taught it while they were busy with their own.

---

## Verse 2:32

> They replied, “Glory be to You! We have no knowledge except what You have taught us. You are truly the All-Knowing, All-Wise.”

**THE ANSWER OF THOSE WHO KNEW THEIR LIMIT**

The angels do not attempt the test. They answer with an act of praise, ***“They replied, “Glory be to You!”***, and the sense of that praise is explained by al-Qurṭubī: it declares God free of any weakness or ignorance that would allow anyone beside Him to know what He has kept hidden. Read inside the scene, that word is their answer to the challenge, because declaring God above a fault is the same as admitting where knowledge lives. as-Saʿdī adds the practical side of the same acknowledgement: they were declaring God free of any objection from them and of any disobedience to His command.

The sentence they continue with is a confession of limit: ***“We have no knowledge except what You have taught us. You are truly the All-Knowing, All-Wise.”*** al-Ṭabarī reads their words as a return to God and a surrender of what they had not been given to know, and Ibn Kathīr keeps the reply in that order, with the confession first and the two names after it. as-Saʿdī unfolds the two names at the end. The All-Knowing is the One whose knowledge surrounds everything, so that nothing in the heavens or the earth escapes Him. The All-Wise is the One whose wisdom is complete: He created nothing without wisdom and commanded nothing without wisdom, and wisdom in his definition is putting a thing in the place where it belongs.

**WHAT THE TWO NAMES SETTLE**

The order inside the answer is the part worth noticing, because the confession of ignorance comes between two statements about God. Limit is admitted first, then knowledge is ascribed, and the ascription is given in the form that makes the admission bearable. If the wisest creatures in the world know only what they have been taught, then the teaching is the honour and not the humiliation.

The words the angels use carry the argument of the whole scene, and al-Jalālayn notes the small point of construction that fixes it: the pronoun is emphasised so that the meaning falls where it must, You alone are the One who knows, and You alone are the One whose wisdom orders things. The reason the angels say this here rather than simply keeping silent is that their earlier question had to be answered in words. Silence would have left the impression that the position they took had been abandoned under pressure, and the verse shows instead a position given up on the merits.

**THE MANNER OF LEARNING**

The scene is a small manual on how a creature should hold what it knows, and every part of it is on display. The angels had knowledge of a kind and worship of a high order, and neither of those became the ground of a claim about what God should do. What follows from their answer is a habit of mind: the sharper a man's knowledge, the more exactly he can see the edge of it, and the edge is what the verse puts into words.

Because the confession came before the explanation, the angels kept the question open until they had heard the answer. A reader who copies the manner rather than only the words will find that this is harder than it sounds, since the temptation in every disagreement about what God does is to settle the matter with the material already in hand. Their sentence leaves the material in hand and waits for the teacher.

Think of an apprentice jeweller who is asked to weigh a stone he has never seen in front of a hall of masters. He says the truth: I have learned to weigh the stones my master set before me, and this one he has kept in his own hand. He does not lose standing by saying it, and he does not guess at a weight he cannot know.

---

## Verse 2:33

> Allah said, “O Adam! Inform them of their names.” Then when Adam did, Allah said, “Did I not tell you that I know the secrets of the heavens and the earth, and I know what you reveal and what you conceal?”

**THE DECLARATION BEFORE THE ANGELS**

The test is completed by the man who was taught, and the command is given to him by name: ***“Allah said, “O Adam!”***, and then ***“Inform them of their names.”*** The instruction is examined by al-Qurṭubī, who explains that Adam was ordered to make the angels know what they had been unable to say, after the same things had been laid before them and they had failed. al-Ṭabarī explains that God made His angels know the standing of the one He had appointed, and the declaration at the end of the scene is the proof of it. al-Jalālayn fills in the manner of the report, since the verb used here carries the sense of giving news of a thing: Adam named each thing by its appellation and set out the wisdom behind its creation, and that turns the exercise from a recitation of labels into a demonstration of understanding. The knowledge placed in him was therefore shown to be knowledge of a different order from a list learned by heart, and al-Ālūsī observes that God addressed Adam by his proper name, which is the custom of the Book with the prophets it sends, while the final Messenger is addressed by title instead, for his higher station.

**WHAT THE ANSWER PROVED**

When the report was given, the point of the whole scene became visible, and as-Saʿdī states what it proved: the superiority of Adam over the angels was now established in front of them, and with it the wisdom and knowledge of the One who had appointed him. The question the angels had asked about the earth, with its corruption and bloodshed, was answered by showing them a creature who could carry knowledge they could not. Since the appointment required a kind of deputy who could name the world and use it, the test measured exactly the qualification the office required, and the answer given a moment earlier in the scene is the reverse of this one, (2:32 — **“We have no knowledge except what You have taught us”**).

**WHAT THE UNSEEN INCLUDES**

The declaration that follows is a reminder rather than new information: ***“Then when Adam did, Allah said, “Did I not tell you”***, and what follows is the content of what He had told them, ***“that I know the secrets of the heavens”*** and ***“and the earth, and I know what you reveal”***, closing with ***“and what you conceal?”*** The report held for Ibn ʿAbbās explains the declared knowledge as the unseen that will take place in the heavens and on the earth. The argument inside the sentence is set out by as-Saʿdī: what is hidden from every creature was never hidden from God, so the visible is known by Him first and more certainly, and the whole of the objection was raised inside a knowledge that had already accounted for it.

The last two clauses look past the scene to the hearts in it. What the angels had voiced was part of what they revealed, and the reservation a question can carry is part of what a creature conceals. Since the One who judges the deputy knows both the speech and the silence, the appointment was never made on incomplete information, and the reader learns that his own reservations are known to the same Lord.

**WHY THE LESSON REACHES THE READER**

The story is told in this detail because it settles a question every generation asks. A person looks at the ruin in the world and asks why such a creature was put in charge of it, and the answer here is not that the ruin does not matter. The answer is that the knowledge on which the appointment rested was not visible to the objectors, and that some of what they were worried about belonged to a wider account than they could see.

That is why the verse ends with a claim about knowledge rather than a promise about outcomes. A reader who takes it seriously will hold his judgement about the shape of events loosely, since the account is longer than the part he can see. The earlier answer to the angels and this declaration are the same answer, given once at the beginning and once after the proof.

Think of a man who objects when a hospital appoints a young surgeon whose family he knows. He has watched the boy grow up and cannot picture him holding a knife. The appointment stands, and the first operation he is asked to perform is one the older staff could not do. The proof was always in the hands that had been trained where the objector was not looking.

---

## Verse 2:34

> And ˹remember˺ when We said to the angels, “Prostrate before Adam,” so they all did—but not Iblîs, who refused and acted arrogantly, becoming unfaithful

**THE COMMAND THAT HONOURED ADAM**

The scene closes with an order and its results: ***“And ˹remember˺ when We said to the angels, “Prostrate before Adam,””***. What the prostration meant is explained by al-Jalālayn, who reads it as a bow of greeting rather than an act of worship, so the command honoured the man without touching the worship owed to God. Maʿārif al-Qurʾān gives the reason the command was given in this visible form: the superiority of Adam had been established in argument when he named what the angels could not, and God willed to make that superiority concrete in an act performed by every angel at once. Because the order came after the demonstration, obedience to it was a recognition of what they had just seen.

**OBEDIENCE AND THE ONE EXCEPTION**

The response is stated in three words: ***“so they all did”***. as-Saʿdī notes the speed of it, since the angels carried out the command without hesitation. The exception is named straight after: ***“but not Iblîs, who refused”***, and then the reason inside him: ***“and acted arrogantly, becoming unfaithful”***. Ibn Kathīr reads the prostration as an honour given to Adam, and reads the refusal as pride followed by the loss of faith, which is the order the words themselves keep. Because the command was given in front of every angel, the refusal was public, and the loss was public with it. as-Saʿdī works the same sequence out: the refusal to obey stood in front of the pride, and the pride was the fruit of a disbelief he had been carrying inside, so the command merely brought out what was already there.

Who he was is a question the reports take up. al-Qurṭubī gathers the disagreement among the earlier authorities over whether Iblīs belonged to the angels or was of another kind, and the Book states the matter in another sūrah where the angels are named and the exception is described, (18:50 — **“who was one of the jinn”**). His own account of his reasoning is given where the exchange is told in full, (7:12 — **“You created me from fire and him from clay”**), and that sentence shows the disease from the inside: a comparison of material used as an argument against a command.

**PRIDE THAT ENDED IN DISBELIEF**

A small note from al-Baghawī belongs beside the command, and it concerns recitation rather than meaning. One early reader pronounced the word for the angels in a way that the grammarians rejected, and the disagreement shows how closely the wording was carried by the community. What the verse itself carries is the deed: a creature with knowledge sharper than a man's and a record of worship longer than a man's lifetime refused one order, and his standing went with it.

The reason the refusal matters so much in a sūrah that has spent verses on the hypocrites is that it is the same disease in a finer form. Since the offence was a single refusal, the weight of what followed warns the reader against measuring a fault by its length. A hypocrite keeps his position with a tongue that says one thing and a heart that holds another; Iblīs let his position go rather than bend. Both are described here as losing what they had, and the reader is shown that the fault was never lack of information. That is why the scene is told again in other sūrahs, since pride is the oldest fault a creature can carry. Pride asks no questions; it simply declines.

Think of a senior servant in a great house who refuses to stand when the master's guest enters the room. He knows the house better than the guest does and has served in it for years. The refusal costs him the room he loved, and the standing he was protecting went with the courtesy he refused.

---

## Verse 2:35

> We cautioned, “O Adam! Live with your wife in Paradise and eat as freely as you please, but do not approach this tree, or else you will be wrongdoers.”

**A DWELLING AND ITS WIDE PERMISSION**

After the account of the angels comes an address to the man himself: ***“We cautioned, “O Adam!”***, and the first word of the instruction is about settling: ***“Live with your wife in Paradise”***. The gift inside the instruction is noted by as-Saʿdī, who reads the creation of the wife as the completion of the favour, since a companion was made from Adam so that he could find rest and familiarity in her. The house and the company were both given before any command was laid on him, and because the gift came first, the obedience asked for was the answer to a favour already received.

The permission that follows is wide: ***“and eat as freely”***, ***“as you please, but do not approach this tree”***. al-Jalālayn identifies the wife as the woman created from the man's side, and describes the freedom of the garden as eating from its fruits wherever they chose. Ibn Kathīr keeps the same order in his rendering, where the freedom of the garden is eating with pleasure and delight wherever the two of them wished. The single prohibition is placed inside a general liberty, which is the shape of the whole arrangement: one limit in the middle of a wide table.

**THE TREE AND THE FENCE AROUND IT**

The reports differ over which tree was meant, and the Book leaves it unnamed. The commentators name several possibilities and no identification is settled, which keeps the reader's attention on the command rather than on the fruit. The wording of the prohibition is the part worth noticing: the man is not told that eating from it is forbidden so much as that he should not come near it, and as-Saʿdī explains the fence in that way. A boundary that keeps a man a step away from the thing protects him from the moment of decision, since a tree that can be walked up to is a tree that can be stood under.

The warning that closes the sentence is ***“or else you will be wrongdoers.”*** That word describes a man who steps over a known line, and the reason it is used here rather than a description of the fruit is that the wrong is located in the act and not in the object. The reason the boundary is drawn a step away from the fruit is that a command is easier to keep at a distance, and the approach is where the danger begins. What the garden would have charged him with was not curiosity about a tree but the crossing of a boundary that had been marked. That is why the warning names the wrongdoer and not the tree, because the fault would belong to the one who crossed and not to what he reached for.

**WHERE THE STORY IS HEADED**

This verse is the last quiet moment in the account, and the passages that follow it tell what happened at the tree: (20:120 — **“But Satan whispered to him”**), and then the removal from the garden, (2:36 — **“Descend from the heavens ˹to the earth˺ as enemies to each other”**). The same warning is given to the couple in another sūrah in almost these words, (7:19 — **“but do not approach this tree”**), and there the outcome is named in the same way. Since the Book tells the story more than once, the repetition is meant to fix the lesson rather than the scenery.

al-Ṭabarī draws one conclusion from the order of the events: the command to dwell in the garden was given after Iblīs was expelled from it, which settles the sequence of the story. The man was placed in a house whose troublemaker had already been put out, and what later entered it came by invitation through an argument. al-Qurṭubī reads the order of the events the same way, since the expulsion of the enemy came at his disbelief, and the command to dwell in the garden was given after he had been put out of it. The Arabic behind the prohibition carries the sense of drawing near, so the verse fences the approach before it fences the eating.

Think of a father who takes his child to an orchard he has been given and shows him the whole field. He sets one tree apart and tells the boy to keep a strip of grass between himself and it. The fence is not a stingy rule; it is the boundary drawn by someone who knows how quickly a gift can be lost by the person holding it.

---

## Verse 2:36

> But Satan deceived them—leading to their fall from the ˹blissful˺ state they were in, and We said, “Descend from the heavens ˹to the earth˺ as enemies to each other. You will find in the earth a residence and provision for your appointed stay.”

**THE SLIP THAT ENDED THE STAY**

The verse records what happened after the warning: ***“But Satan deceived them”*** and ***“leading to their fall from the ˹blissful˺ state they were in”***. al-Jalālayn reads the verb as a slip that carried them out of the garden, and records the variant recitation that makes the sense a removal rather than a stumble. al-Ṭabarī gathers the readers of the Qur'an on the same word and sets out which of them recited it one way and which the other, and the disagreement shows how carefully the community carried a single verb. The instrument he used is named by al-Qurṭubī, who reads the deception as the false promise that drew them toward the tree, and by al-Jalālayn, who records the words of the promise itself, (20:120 — **“Shall I show you the Tree of Immortality”**) and a kingdom that would not fade.

The manner of the approach is discussed by Maʿārif al-Qurʾān, since the enemy had already been expelled from the garden before the man was placed in it. The reports offer more than one way he reached them, and the point the passage presses is not the door he used but the warning it leaves: a creature that has been refused does not give up, because the argument is continued by a quieter method.

**THE DESCENT AND THE TERM THAT FOLLOWED**

What comes next is spoken from above the scene: ***“and We said, “Descend from the heavens ˹to the earth˺”***, and the terms of the new dwelling are set out in the same breath, ***“as enemies to each other. You will find in the earth a residence”***, and then the clause that limits the whole arrangement, ***“and provision for your appointed stay.”*** as-Saʿdī reads the sentence as an account of what the earth would now be for them: a place to settle and a provision to use, both of them held for a fixed term. The Book states the same descent in another sūrah with the guidance and its reward attached, (20:123 — **“Then when guidance comes to you from Me”**).

The reason the promise of provision is attached to the command to descend is that the two belong together, since the earth was never intended as a place without supplies. A man sent out of a garden into open country is still a man under care, and what follows from the verse is that his needs were arranged before his feet touched the ground.

There is one further thing in the sentence that deserves a second look. Enmity is named as a condition of the new life, and it is stated in the plural, which means the conflict was not only between the first man and his enemy. It runs through the world they were entering, and every person born into it inherits both the field and the quarrel. Since the provision and the conflict are named in one sentence, the reader is told that the two run together until the stay ends.

Think of a family moved out of a walled estate into open country, given a plot, a plough and a date on which the tenancy ends. Nothing in the new arrangement is accidental, and the papers were drawn before they arrived. That is the shape of the verse, and what stands at the end of it is the part a settled man forgets. Because the warning was given before the fall, nobody can say the ground was left unmarked, and since the stay is fixed, nothing in the new life lasts forever. Because the enemy is named as the one who caused the slip, the verse traces the harm to its source, and since the pair are warned in the same breath, the reader learns to watch the whisper rather than the tree.

---

## Verse 2:37

> Then Adam was inspired with words ˹of prayer˺ by his Lord, so He accepted his repentance. Surely He is the Accepter of Repentance, Most Merciful

**WORDS GIVEN TO A MAN WHO HAD FALLEN**

The recovery begins with a gift of speech: ***“Then Adam was inspired with words ˹of prayer˺ by his Lord”***. al-Ṭabarī explains the verb as receiving and taking, built on the idea of one thing meeting another, so what the man received he also accepted. al-Qurṭubī records both senses carried by the word: he understood and he took, and the two together describe the moment a fallen creature is taught what to say. What the words were is given by al-Jalālayn and as-Saʿdī, who read them as the prayer stated in another sūrah, (7:23 — **“Our Lord! We have wronged ourselves. If You do not forgive us and have mercy on us, we will certainly be losers”**).

The order inside that prayer is what makes it a turning rather than an excuse. The man begins by admitting the wrong and ends by naming his need, since he asks for forgiveness and mercy in the same sentence and leaves no room for a defence. Because the words came to him from his Lord and not from his own invention, the verse teaches the reader that even the speech of repentance is a gift.

**THE ACCEPTANCE AND ITS TWO NAMES**

The second half of the verse is the answer: ***“so He accepted his repentance. Surely He is the Accepter of Repentance, Most Merciful”***. as-Saʿdī notes the two stages in the mercy, since the enabling to turn and the acceptance of the turning are both works of God, and the names chosen at the end describe the One who comes back to the servant who comes back to Him. Ibn Kathīr carries the report of the man's words and the pardon that followed them, and the Book states the same recovery plainly in another sūrah, (20:122 — **“Then his Lord chose him”**), where the acceptance and the guidance are named side by side.

The reason the sentence ends with mercy rather than with the pardon alone is that the pardon answers the past while the mercy covers what comes after it.
