A man restored and left to himself would fall again; a man restored and carried falls into a hand, which is why the attribute is named and not the act alone. Since the words were given rather than discovered, the first turning of the first man became the model of every turning after it. Because the pardon followed a confession and not a defence, the pattern stands for every reader who has done wrong, and since the words were placed in his mouth, the turning itself begins as a gift. Because the acceptance is stated in the same sentence as the words, the answer arrives while the man is still asking, and since the two names describe the One who keeps coming back to those who come back, the door stays open for every reader who repeats them.

One detail is worth holding from the whole scene: the first words of the first man after his fall were not a complaint about the enemy who misled him. They were a confession, and the verse treats that confession as the door back through which everything else arrived.

Think of a boy who breaks a window and runs to his father, not with a story about who dared him, but with the plain sentence that he did it and needs help to mend it. The father's forgiveness is not a reward for good manners. The words were the truth, and the truth is what the door of a house is for.

---

## Verse 2:38

> We said, “Descend all of you! Then when guidance comes to you from Me, whoever follows it, there will be no fear for them, nor will they grieve

**A COMMAND REPEATED ON PURPOSE**

The descent is commanded a second time: ***“We said, “Descend all of you!”***. al-Qurṭubī reads the repetition as a strengthening, the way a man is told to rise and told again, and he records the further view that each repeating carried its own ruling with it. as-Saʿdī gives the same reason from the side of the passage: the command is repeated so that what follows can be attached to it, since the sentence that comes next is the charter of the new life. al-Ṭabarī works through the construction of the conditional sentence that follows, where the particle carrying the sense of if is joined to an emphasised verb. al-Jalālayn notes the letter that is folded into the following word, a small mark of the language that the readers preserved.

The repetition is also a consolation. A command given twice is not a sign of anger but of attention, since the One who sent them out is the One who keeps speaking to them. Because the second descent is joined to a promise and a warning, the reader of the whole scene is meant to hear a door closing and a road being pointed out at the same time. Since the words were spoken again to people already outside the garden, the address proves that the road was opened before the journey began. Because the promise and the warning are both attached to guidance, the way through is named before the fears are named, and since the instruction is short, nothing was left out that the new life required.

**GUIDANCE, AND THE TWO TROUBLES IT REMOVES**

The promise is attached to a condition: ***“Then when guidance comes to you from Me, whoever follows it, there will be no fear”***, and the sentence ends ***“for them, nor will they grieve”***. What guidance means here is spelled out by as-Saʿdī, who reads it as a messenger and a book, so that following it means believing in the messengers of God and in the books they brought.

The two troubles named at the end are the two directions time runs. Fear looks forward at what has not happened, and grief looks back at what cannot be changed, and the verse removes both from the person who follows the guidance. That pair is the peace the sentence is offering, and it is stated in the language of ordinary life rather than in the language of reward, because what is being described is a state a man can carry into the middle of a hard year.

One matter is settled here by Maʿārif al-Qurʾān. The descent to the earth was not a punishment that the pardon should have lifted, since the command stood after the forgiveness and only the manner of the stay had changed. The earth was always the place the story was going to be lived, and the pardon changed the state of the man who lived it.

Think of a young man leaving a guarded house for a long crossing. Before he goes, the one sending him names the guide he will meet and the two things he must not carry on the road. The instruction is short, and everything else about the journey depends on whether he keeps it.

---

## Verse 2:39

> But those who disbelieve and deny Our signs will be the residents of the Fire. They will be there forever.”

**THE OTHER SIDE OF THE SAME SENTENCE**

The promise just given has a counterpart, and the verse states it without softening: ***“But those who disbelieve”***, and then ***“and deny Our signs will be the residents of the Fire. They will be there forever.”*** Two acts are named, and al-Ṭabarī explains why both are needed. The first is the rejection itself, and the second is the refusal of the evidence, since the signs of God in his reading are the proofs of His oneness and the messages His messengers brought. A man who turns away from a proof has done more than decline an opinion.

The word for the disbelievers is examined by al-Qurṭubī in the sense it carries here, where the association of others with God is meant, and the denial that follows is attached to the signs as their particular offence. The ending of the verse is looked at closely as well, since the noun for residents is built on companionship and long attachment, and its companionship here does not end. al-Jalālayn draws the sense out: those who deny the Books of God are the inhabitants of the Fire, and remaining there means neither perishing nor leaving. The report held for Ibn ʿAbbās reads the signs as the scripture and the one who brought it, and reads the residents as the dwellers of the fire who abide in it. Ibn Kathīr renders the same verdict in the same order, with the denial of the signs followed by the abiding.

**WHY THE VERDICT IS STATED IN THE SAME BREATH**

The position of the two sentences matters more than either of them alone. The guidance and its peace are named, and the refusal and its end are named next, so that the reader receives both on the same page. Because the Qur'an addresses a person who must choose and not a spectator who is merely informed, it refuses to let one half of the report arrive without the other.

The Book repeats the same verdict in another sūrah with an added sentence about the people it covers, (98:6 — **“will be in the Fire of Hell, to stay there forever”**), and there the residents are described as the worst of created beings. That description is what the phrase about signs is doing here. The refusal was not made against an argument a reader could not check, and the sentence about forever is the length of a decision that was made in a moment.

Think of a traveller who comes to a mountain pass and finds two notices, one naming the road through and one naming the drop beyond. He may dislike the second notice, and it was still put there by the same hand that made the first. The verse keeps the two posts standing together, which is why the passage can speak about fear and grief in one line and about a fire in the next. as-Saʿdī sets the two halves of the report side by side and draws the outcome of each: the follower is given security and happiness, and the denier inherits the fire, so the verdict is placed directly after the promise rather than at the end of the sūrah. Because the two sentences stand side by side, no reader can take the promise without meeting the warning, and since both are addressed to the same hearer, neither can be left as a matter for somebody else. Because the verdict names the denial of the signs, the failure is measured by what was shown rather than by what was felt, and since the fire is described as a companion, a man is told that the company he chooses in this life is the company he keeps in the next. Because the two verdicts are given within three verses of each other, the contrast is meant to stand in the reader's mind as one picture, and since the promise is stated first, the reader is left with the invitation rather than the warning as his last thought.

---

## Verse 2:40

> O children of Israel! Remember My favours upon you. Fulfil your covenant and I will fulfil Mine, and stand in awe of Me ˹alone˺

**WHO IS BEING ADDRESSED**

The sūrah turns now to a community with a long record, and the address names it: ***“O children of Israel!”*** The man behind the name is identified by al-Ṭabarī as Jacob, the son of Isaac and the son of Abraham, and the name Israel was his. The groups being addressed are described by as-Saʿdī as the Israelite communities in Madinah and the settlements around it, with those who came after them included in the same address, and the favour named first is the one that runs through the whole history of that line.

The form of the address is examined by al-Qurṭubī, who notes the grammar of the construct and the letter dropped from the word for sons because of it, and the care spent on that point is a reminder that the Book speaks to a living community with its own language and history.

**THE FAVOUR, THE COVENANT, AND THE ANSWER**

The command is to remember: ***“Remember My favours upon you. Fulfil your covenant”***, and the promise attached to it is ***“and I will fulfil Mine”***. What the favours were is set out by al-Jalālayn, who names the rescue from Pharaoh, the parting of the sea, the shade of the clouds and the provision that came with it, and he reads the command as a call to answer the gifts with gratitude. The covenant is read by Ibn Kathīr as an exchange of obligations: the community keeps what it undertook, and God keeps what He promised, and the Book records the same covenant being taken again with the mountain raised above them, (2:63 — **“We took a covenant from you”**).

The last words carry the motive that makes the rest possible: ***“and stand in awe of Me ˹alone˺”***. The reason awe is named at the end rather than at the beginning is that a promise kept out of fear of people is not a promise kept, and a community standing in front of a powerful neighbour needs to know whom it should fear first. Ibn Kathīr reads the same closing command as the ground of the whole covenant, since a promise kept for the sake of people is not kept for its owner. Since the awe is directed to God alone, the verse frees the community from the fear of the neighbours whose approval it had been trading for. Because the favours were named before the commands, gratitude is the ground the covenant stands on, and since the gift was already given, what is asked for is a response rather than a payment. What follows from the verse is that the covenant is kept by a people whose loyalty has already been settled, because a wavering loyalty will find a reason to break its word at the first pressure.

**WHY THIS ADDRESS COMES HERE**

The placement is deliberate, since the passage has just described a community that was given guidance and told what following it brings. The reader now watches the same offer being made to a people who had received it before, and the verse asks them to handle the second gift better than the first. Because the favours are named as already given, gratitude is the ground the whole argument stands on, and ingratitude is what makes every later failure possible.

Think of an old family whose land was saved by a benefactor two generations ago. The grandchildren still live on the land he rescued, and the only thing he asks of them is to keep the agreement their grandfather signed. The request is small, the record is long, and the day they break it they will not be the first to have forgotten, only the latest.

---

## Verse 2:41

> Believe in My revelations which confirm your Scriptures. Do not be the first to deny them or trade them for a fleeting gain. And be mindful of Me

**BELIEVE, AND WHAT THE BELIEF CONFIRMS**

The next command continues the address: ***“Believe in My revelations”***, and the phrase that follows explains the relation of the new Book to the old, ***“which confirm your Scriptures. Do not be the first to deny them”***. al-Ṭabarī reads the command as the plain one of accepting what came down on Muhammad, and he reads the confirming as agreement on the matters the earlier Books had already taught. al-Jalālayn is exact on the point: the Book confirms the Torah in the affirmation of God's oneness and of prophethood, which means the community was not being asked to abandon its own scripture but to recognise its own testimony. al-Qurṭubī examines the construction of the confirming clause and reads it as a state of the revelation itself, which is why the Book can be described as verifying what the community already had in its hands.

The warning about being first is worth a second look, and Maʿārif al-Qurʾān takes it up. The denial of a messenger is a grave matter whoever commits it, and the warning here is aimed at the position of the first deniers, because a community's leaders set the pattern the rest will follow. The Book records the same community being described through the advantage it once held, (2:89 — **“when there came to them a Book from Allah which they recognized, confirming the Scripture they had”**), and the recognition in that sentence is the reason the warning is so sharp. Ibn Kathīr keeps the order of the verse in his rendering, with belief in what was sent down, the confirmation of what the community had, and the warning against being the first to deny.

**THE PRICE AND THE FEAR**

The third command names a bargain: ***“or trade them for a fleeting gain.”*** The trade is the one the sūrah has already described, and the wording here is close to another verse that forbids it in general terms, (16:95 — **“And do not trade Allah’s covenant for a fleeting gain”**). The reason the price is called fleeting is not that it was small in the hand, since a man who takes it feels the weight of it. The reason is that it does not last, and a purchase that ends cannot be worth a covenant that does not.

The verse closes with the same note the earlier address did: ***“And be mindful of Me”***. The fear named is not the anxiety of a servant who does not know his master's mind, because the commands have been spelled out and the covenant has been stated. What it names is the attention a man keeps on the One whose favour he is living in, and without it the covenant and the Book and the warnings all become documents he owns rather than relations he keeps. al-Baghawī reads this closing command as the guard on the two that came before it, since belief and honesty hold only while the fear of God stands behind them. Since the warning is aimed at the first deniers, the verse speaks to the people whose refusal sets the pattern for everyone who follows them.

Think of a clerk handed a document he can verify against the archive on his own shelf. The paper matches its copy, and the man who brought it only asks to be believed rather than opposed. The clerk's refusal will not be remembered for its argument, because there was no argument; it will be remembered as the day he had the proof in his own hand and declined to use it.

---

## Verse 2:42

> Do not mix truth with falsehood or hide the truth knowingly

**A COMMUNITY WITH A DOCUMENT IN ITS HAND**

The address to the Children of Israel continues, and the command in this verse reaches for the way they handled what they knew: ***“Do not mix truth with falsehood”***. al-Qurṭubī works through the verb used in the image. In the language of the Arabs it carries the sense of mixing, the way a man blends a clear matter with a tangled one and truth with falsehood, and the Book uses the same word elsewhere of a confusion laid over a plain account. Because the image is of a covering laid over something rather than a plain denial, the verse treats the muddling of a clear matter as a serious act. The command is attached by al-Ālūsī to the whole address before it, since it follows the call to believe in what was sent down and belongs to the same instruction.

The report held for Ibn ʿAbbās gives the scene a shape. The men being addressed were not inventing a religion from nothing; they were describing figures they had in their books, and the confusion was to run one description into another. Since the men held the description in their own books, the fault was not ignorance but the handling of what they knew. A community that owns a document faces a particular temptation, and it is rarely open denial. The danger is the blurring, until nothing can be checked.

**THE SECOND HALF OF THE SAME CRIME**

The prohibition continues with a second act: ***“or hide the truth knowingly”***. al-Jalālayn reads the two halves as one movement: the truth revealed to them is obscured with something fabricated, and the description they carry is concealed while they know it to be true. The reason the two are named together is that they feed each other. A man who has begun to blur a text can then keep silence about it honestly, since he no longer has a clear line to point at.

The failure is named by the Book again in a later sūrah, and the wording is close enough to hear both: (3:71 — **“Why do you mix the truth with falsehood and hide the truth knowingly”**). The verdict on that concealment is stated where the subject is taken up on its own, (2:159 — **“Those who hide the clear proofs and guidance that We have revealed”**), and the sentence ends with the loss of standing among the very people who would have honoured them. Maʿārif al-Qurʾān draws the rule out of the verse: it is not permitted to blend the two so that a hearer cannot tell which is which, and it is not permitted to keep the truth out of sight. Because the rule outlives the community first addressed, the warning reaches every person who holds a text and edits the account of it.

**WHY THE COMMAND IS PLACED HERE**

The placement is part of the argument. These verses are calling a community back to its own testimony, and the greatest danger to that return is not open denial but a slow mixture in which every claim keeps a portion of the truth. Ibn Kathīr treats the passage under exactly that heading, since the concealment of what a man knows is the fault the Book returns to in this address.

A reader may want to file the verse under the history of another community, and the closing of it makes that difficult. Nothing in the sentence is tied to a place or a century. What it names is the habit of a person who holds two accounts of his own life, one that he shows and one that he edits, and the same fault is available to anyone who has ever softened a fact for a reason he did not want to state.

Think of a surveyor sent to measure a boundary. He finds the old stone in the ground, and he files a report in which the measurement is exact and the marker is described as uncertain. Nothing he wrote is a lie, and the land is lost all the same. The verse forbids both halves of that report: the blurring and the silence.

---

## Verse 2:43

> Establish prayer, pay alms-tax, and bow down with those who bow down

**THREE COMMANDS IN ONE BREATH**

The verse continues the same address with three instructions: ***“Establish prayer, pay alms-tax”***, and then ***“and bow down with those”*** who worship,***“who bow down”***. al-Ālūsī notes which prayer and which giving are meant, since the words are applied here to the worship of the Muslims rather than to a rite of another community; the command brings the addressed into the practice of the believers.

The report carried by al-Ṭabarī explains why the command was needed in this place. The leaders of the community were men who urged others to pray and to give while doing neither themselves, and the verse closes that gap by ordering the teachers with the taught. Because the command reaches the very men who were giving it, the verse removes the distance that lets a person recommend a road he never travels. al-Qurṭubī counts the questions that rise out of the sentence and settles the first of them: the form of the command carries obligation, so what is asked is a duty and not a suggestion.

**THE BOWING THAT JOINS A BODY**

The third instruction is where the verse stops being about private duty: ***“and bow down”*** in the same line, ***“with those who bow down”***. al-Jalālayn reads the phrase as praying alongside those who pray, meaning the Messenger and his Companions, and he records that it came down concerning the scholars of the community who told their own kin among the Muslims to follow the new message while keeping away from it themselves. The report held for Ibn ʿAbbās is read the same way, with the five daily prayers named in full and the bowing attached to the body of worshippers.

The reason the command is not content with prayer in private is given by the word with. A man can hold a private devotion that costs him nothing but his own time, and he cannot hold a shared one without being seen, corrected and counted. Since the bowing is done alongside others, the verse is asking for a place in the line rather than a feeling in the chest.

**THE SAME THREE IN ANOTHER SCRIPTURE**

None of the three commands is new to the people addressed, and the Book says as much where it describes what their own scripture had already required: (98:5 — **“establish prayer, and pay alms-tax”**). The community was not being handed a strange law; it was being asked to stand where its own book had already pointed. Since the duty is older than the refusal, the community cannot treat the command as a burden invented for them. Because the same two duties appear in the other passage, the reader is shown one religion described in two books. Ibn Kathīr treats the verse in the same line as the ones around it, where the instruction to pray and to give follows the demand for honesty about what the earlier Books contained.

A person who takes the verse seriously will notice where the three commands land. Two of them can be carried out alone and one cannot, and the third is the measure of the other two. A man who prays and gives but will not stand in a row beside people he did not choose has kept a private religion and left the community where he found it. Because the third command costs a man the care of his own reputation, it reveals whether the other two are offered to God or to an audience. Since the bowing is shared, the people on either side of him are witnesses to what he does. Ibn ʿUthaymīn treats the three commands as a single order of worship, in which the private duty and the public line belong together.

Think of a man who washes at home before a meal and then sits at the table without eating with anyone. His cleanliness is real and his seat is empty, and the family that set the table learns something about him from the empty chair.

---

## Verse 2:44

> Do you preach righteousness and fail to practice it yourselves, although you read the Scripture? Do you not understand

**THE QUESTION THAT IS A REBUKE**

The verse asks a question and does not wait for an answer: ***“Do you preach righteousness”***, and then the charge, ***“and fail to practice it yourselves”***. al-Qurṭubī reads the form as a question that carries reproach rather than inquiry, and he records that the people meant are the scholars of the community whose teaching was sound and whose conduct was not. Their fault is stated by al-Baghawī as the ordinary one of men who tell a relative to take a road they do not walk, since the reports describe them urging those close to them to accept what the new message said while staying where they were.

The word for the good they urged is explained by as-Saʿdī and by al-Jalālayn, who read it as faith and every kind of good, so the preaching was not a small thing badly done. That is what makes the verse sharp, because the men were pointing at the right road with their hands while their feet stayed put. Since the good they urged carries faith and every act of obedience, the complaint is not about a small failure in a large project, and the message stands exposed as delivered by the wrong messenger.

**THE BOOK THAT WAS BEING RECITED**

The next words take away the defence of ignorance: ***“although you read the Scripture?”***, and then the closing question, ***“Do you not understand”***. All are pressed by Ibn Kathīr from the same construction, since the men were reciting a text they knew, and the recitation is named by as-Saʿdī as the ground of the rebuke. The verse states the reason the answer does not exist: knowledge was not the missing piece, because the knowledge was on their tongues every day. Because the text was on their tongues daily, the verse leaves a man with nothing to say in his own defence. That is why the closing question is aimed not at information but at the use of it.

The same fault is named in another place without the argument, and it is put as a question to anyone who claims to believe: (61:2 — **“Why do you say what you do not do”**). The Book is not objecting to speech about goodness; it is objecting to speech that has been separated from the life of the speaker. A congregation can be talked into many things and it cannot be talked into respect for a teacher it no longer watches.

**WHAT THE VERSE ASKS OF THOSE WHO TEACH**

The command inside the rebuke belongs to everyone who carries knowledge, and as-Saʿdī draws out the sense of understanding that the closing words demand. The faculty is named from the act of holding a thing back, since understanding is what restrains a man from what harms him, and the verse asks the teachers why the faculty that guards others has not guarded them.

The work of teaching does not stand still on its own. A person who explains good to others and neglects himself does not merely fail privately; he teaches a lesson with two faces, and the audience learns the second one better.

Think of a doctor who runs a clinic in the morning and smokes in the corridor. He knows the harm better than his patients do, and every patient who passes him learns which of the two lessons is really believed. The verse holds that gap up to the light and asks the only question that matters: if the knowledge is real, why has it not reached the man holding it?

---

## Verse 2:45

> And seek help through patience and prayer. Indeed, it is a burden except for the humble—

**A TOOL GIVEN FOR THE EFFORT**

The address turns from rebuke to help: ***“And seek help through patience”***, and then ***“and prayer. Indeed, it is a burden except”*** for the one whose heart is soft, and the verse closes by naming those: ***“for the humble”***. al-Ālūsī explains why the remedy comes here, since the community had been told to leave its misguidance and to keep the laws, and that work was heavy on them because it cost them what they loved. The two things named in the verse are given as equipment for exactly that weight.

What patience means is set out by al-Qurṭubī from the root of the word, which carries the sense of holding back and restraining. A person is described by it when he holds himself in place instead of letting go, and the Book uses the same root of a man held against his will until he died. Because the sense of the term is restraint rather than surrender, the verse is asking a person to hold himself in place. That is why patience in this passage is work rather than a mood. Patience here, then, is activity rather than passivity, and as-Saʿdī sorts it into its kinds: patience while obeying God until the duty is done, patience away from disobedience until the desire is left, and patience under a painful decree without complaint.

**THE PRAYER AND THE PRONOUN**

The second tool is named in one word: ***“and prayer”***. Two questions follow it in the sources, and both are addressed. al-Ṭabarī reads the pronoun in the next clause as returning to the prayer itself, so the sentence says that prayer is heavy except for those whose hearts are humble, and he records the other view that the pronoun covers the whole matter of the two instructions. al-Jalālayn notes why the prayer stands alone after patience was named, since the singling out of a thing in this way is how the Book marks its weight.

The reason the burden is a sign and not a fault is that difficulty is measured by the appetite it crosses. A person who has no desire to eat finds no burden in a fast, and a person who loves the world finds prayer heavy when it interrupts him. That is why the verse does not describe the prayer as light; it describes the people for whom it is light.

**THE HUMILITY THAT MAKES THE LOAD LIGHT**

The last words name the quality that decides the weight: ***“for the humble”***. Maʿārif al-Qurʾān draws the picture of the humble heart, a restfulness and a lowliness that settles a person under his Lord rather than in front of other people. The help the verse offers is placed where a man can reach it, and the Book states the same instruction elsewhere with a promise attached: (2:153 — **“Seek comfort in patience and prayer”**). The same pair is turned into a gift where the Messenger is told that his own patience comes from God, (16:127 — **“for your patience is only with Allah’s help”**).

Because the tools are named as means toward an end, the verse assumes the road will be hard rather than pretending it is not. A reader who expects the commands of God to feel easy has misread the arrangement; the verse hands him two things to carry him through the difficulty and tells him where the strength for it comes from.

Think of a man on a long climb with a heavy pack. Two things make the difference: he holds his pace and he rests at the right hours, and the traveller who does neither is the one who sits down halfway and blames the mountain. The verse puts the two habits in his hands and names the disposition that keeps a person using them.

---

## Verse 2:46

> those who are certain that they will meet their Lord and to Him they will return

**THE DESCRIPTION OF THE HUMBLE**

The verse finishes the sentence begun before it, and it defines the people whose prayer is light: ***“those who are certain”***, and then what they are certain of, ***“that they will meet their Lord”***, and the last clause, ***“and to Him they will return”***. The verse is therefore a portrait rather than a new instruction, and what it holds up is the conviction that makes the command of the previous verse work.

The word for certainty is examined at length by al-Ṭabarī, who raises the objection a reader might bring. The question he puts is how a person described by humility and obedience can be said to think he will meet his Lord, since thinking carries doubt and a doubter about the meeting with God stands outside belief. The answer he gives is that the word in the usage of the Arabs carries certainty, and al-Qurṭubī records the same position as the one held by the majority of the commentators.

**WHAT THE MEETING MEANS**

The report held for Ibn ʿAbbās reads the meeting as the sight of God and the return as the journey after death, and al-Ālūsī examines the language behind the first of those words, where it describes one body reaching another in a way that brings the two close. al-Jalālayn keeps the sense on the day of standing, with the meeting at the resurrection and the return in the world that follows it. The two clauses together cover everything a person is heading toward, since the first names what he will arrive at and the second points at the journey itself. Since the first clause lifts up what the traveller will arrive at and the second the road he is on, the two together cover everything a person is heading toward. That is why the conviction is described as the mark of the humble rather than as an achievement of the strong.

The Book places the same thought in the mouth of the believers in another passage, and it is a sentence a person says about himself rather than about anyone else: (2:156 — **“Surely to Allah we belong and to Him we will ˹all˺ return”**). Read with the verse before it, the connection is exact, because the two tools of help and the conviction here are one arrangement. as-Saʿdī places the certainty in the middle of the passage as the motive that keeps a person working, since a man who expects to meet his Lord does not treat the meeting as far off. Patience works while a man believes the road is going somewhere, and prayer works while he believes the One he addresses will meet him.

**WHY THE CONVICTION MAKES THE PRAYER LIGHT**

This is the answer the earlier verse was building toward, and it is stated here as a description rather than as advice. A person who is certain that he will stand before his Lord and that he is on his way home carries his duties differently, because he is not choosing between the present and the future. He is reading his life as a journey with a landing place, and the prayer is the time he spends facing it.

Because certainty of this kind is not a mood but a settled view of one's own direction, it can be tested by what a man does with his day. The humble in the verse are not described by feeling small; they are described by living as people who know where they are going.

Think of a passenger on a long flight who has seen the destination on the ticket and heard the announcement. The hours on board are still hours, and the passenger who checks his watch against the arrival time spends them differently from the one who is not sure where the plane is going.

---

## Verse 2:47

> O Children of Israel! Remember ˹all˺ the favours I granted you and how I honoured you above the others

**THE SAME ADDRESS, GIVEN AGAIN**

The sūrah returns once more to the Children of Israel, and the opening is repeated: ***“O Children of Israel!”***, and the command is ***“Remember ˹all˺ the favours I granted you”***. al-Ṭabarī reads the repetition as the same address he had explained earlier in the sūrah, where the favours of the forefathers were named, and al-Ālūsī gives the reason for saying it twice: the reminder is repeated for emphasis and to show how far the community had drifted from the claim of those gifts, and it prepares the reader for the obligations that follow. Because the reminder is repeated, the verse assumes that a gift can be enjoyed for generations and still go unacknowledged.

The report held for Ibn ʿAbbās names the people addressed as the children of Jacob, and Maʿārif al-Qurʾān sets out the purpose of the reminder, which is that the recognition of benefits received should move a person toward gratitude and obedience. The command to remember is therefore not a request for nostalgia; it is the first step of an argument. The instruction is built on a root that carries the sense of keeping a thing present in the mind, and Ibn ʿUthaymīn treats the repetition as a reminder given again because the first had not produced the answer it deserved.

**THE HONOUR THAT IS NAMED SECOND**

The sentence adds a particular gift: ***“and how I honoured you above the others”***. The phrase is explained by al-Qurṭubī with a limit built into it, since the preference is over the peoples of their own time rather than over every human being who would ever live. The Book states the same favour in another sūrah in words that carry the prophecy and the provision along with it, (45:16 — **“favoured them above the others”**), and again where the choice is named as a knowing one, (44:32 — **“We chose the Israelites knowingly above the others”**).

The honour was real, and the verse does not describe it as a mistake. What the reminder does is place the gift in front of the receiver while there is still time to answer it, because a favour that is remembered while it can still be used is a different thing from a favour that is only recalled as history.

**THE DUTY THAT TRAVELS WITH A GIFT**

Ibn Kathīr reads the verse as a reminder given so that gratitude would lead to obedience, and the same pattern runs through the whole address in this stretch of the sūrah: the favours are named first, the covenant is named next, and the commands follow both. The reason the order never changes is that a person who has forgotten what he was given will read every command as a burden, while a person who remembers will read it as the fitting answer to a gift.

A community can live for generations on the memory of an honour and never pay the price it carried. That is the condition the verse is trying to prevent, and the repeated address is the sound of a door being knocked on a second time.

Think of a family that still speaks about the scholarship their grandfather won, while the library he left sits unopened in the next room. The honour is real and the books are there, and the verse asks the family to remember the gift in a way that makes somebody open one of them.

---

## Verse 2:48

> Guard yourselves against the Day on which no soul will be of help to another. No intercession will be accepted, no ransom taken, and no help will be given

**A WARNING ABOUT A DAY**

The command changes from remembering to preparing: ***“Guard yourselves against the Day on which no soul will be of help to another.”*** The protection named is of the kind a man builds before a storm arrives, and al-Ṭabarī notes the two ways the sentence can be read, since the day can be described as the time in which nothing is paid on another's behalf, and the verse means that such a payment will not exist there. al-Qurṭubī reads the command as a threat rather than a simple instruction, since the warning is about the punishment of that day, and al-Ālūsī sets out the same reading from the grammar of the sentence. Because the three refusals are stated without conditions, a person is meant to stop calculating which of them might still work in his case.

The sentence that follows removes the three doors people keep in reserve: ***“No intercession will be accepted, no ransom taken”***, and then ***“and no help will be given”***. al-Jalālayn records the two ways the first of those clauses is recited, with either sense available, the intercession accepted or the intercessor received, and the sense lands in the same place either way. The report held for Ibn ʿAbbās adds the plain reading of the whole verse: the day is one on which no soul can pay anything for another, and no unbelieving soul can answer for its companion.

**THE SAME DAY DESCRIBED TWICE**

The Book describes this day in more than one place, and the wording of one description is close to this verse: (2:123 — **“And guard yourselves against the Day when no soul will be of any help to another”**). Another passage names the three losses in a different order, (2:254 — **“before the arrival of a Day when there will be no bargaining, friendship, or intercession”**). And the door that is shut for one kind of help is not shut for all of it, since the Book places intercession where it belongs, (39:44 — **“All intercession belongs to Allah ˹alone˺”**). What the verse removes is not the mercy of God but the private arrangements a person imagines he will make on the day itself. Because the supports removed are exactly the ones people gather in this world, the verse tells the reader that the assets he is building now are of a kind that will not travel. Since the mercy of God is left standing in the same passage, the closing of those doors is a correction of where a person knocks rather than a refusal to answer.

**WHY THE WARNING IS GIVEN TO A RELIGIOUS COMMUNITY**

The community being addressed had a particular reason to lean on those three supports. They were a people of a covenant, of a lineage, and of a book, and each of those could be turned into a claim on the day: the covenant as a standing guarantee, the lineage as a connection, and the book as a title. The verse removes each of them in one sentence, and it does so in the middle of the address that was reminding them of their honour.

The reason the warning is placed after the mention of the favour is that privilege is exactly where a person stops working. A man who has been given something special is tempted to believe that the gift will carry him, and the verse tells him that on that day the book in a man's hand and the family he belongs to will both be silent. Because the warning stands in the middle of a series of favours, honour and accounting are shown to be joined, so that a greater gift brings a greater reckoning behind it. Since the day is named as something to be guarded against rather than a fate to be met passively, the reader is given work while the time remains. Ibn ʿUthaymīn draws attention to the order of the losses, since the help is removed first and the payment after it, so that a man who has lost his helpers still reaches for his money.

Think of a man who arrives at a border with a letter from an old friend of the governor. The letter is genuine, the friendship was real, and the officer at the desk has orders that allow no exceptions and no exchanges. What the letter proves is that there was a friendship; it does not move the man across the line. The verse tells the reader to do his travelling while the road is still open.

---

## Verse 2:49

> ˹Remember˺ how We delivered you from the people of Pharaoh, who afflicted you with dreadful torment, slaughtering your sons and keeping your women. That was a severe test from your Lord

**THE FAVOUR SPREAD OUT IN DETAIL**

The address to the Children of Israel resumes, and the summons to remember is still running: ***“˹Remember˺ how We delivered you from the people of Pharaoh”***. The opening particle of time is linked by al-Ṭabarī to the command that came before it, so that the sentence reads as a second summons placed beside the first: remember the favour, and remember the hour in which the favour arrived. The reason for the shift from summary to detail is given by al-Ālūsī: the favours had been named in general terms and are now opened out one by one, and the detail makes the reminder sharper and the argument heavier on the hearer. Because the community is being asked to answer a call, the story of its own rescue is placed in front of it rather than argued from a distance.

al-Baghawī reads the pronoun in the address as reaching the descendants of those who were enslaved, and he treats the rescue of the forefathers as a favour counted to the children, since the children were carried out of the harm in the deliverance of the men who went before them. That is why the verse can address a later generation about an event that happened before they were born, and why the gratitude asked for here is owed by people who were never themselves in the brick pits.

**THE TORMENT THAT IS NAMED IN FULL**

The oppression is then described without softening: ***“who afflicted you with dreadful torment, slaughtering your sons”*** and ***“and keeping your women.”*** as-Saʿdī sets out the shape of that cruelty, with the boys put to death out of fear that the community would grow, and the women spared from killing but held under heavy labour and open humiliation, so that a household lived between a grave and a debt. The picture he draws is of a people reduced to the least that a human being can be while remaining alive.

Ibn ʿAbbās reads the torment in the same way, with the killing aimed at the sons and the women preserved for a life of service and shame. Because the sentence names the two halves of the suffering, the reader is not allowed to pass over it as a phrase, and the escape that follows is measured against the whole weight of what the community was leaving behind.

**THE TRIAL INSIDE THE RESCUE**

The verse closes on a line that turns the whole account: ***“That was a severe test from your Lord”***. The same rescue is carried into another sūrah with the same ending, where Moses is told to remind his people of it: (14:6 — **“Remember Allah’s favour upon you”**). The Book repeats the account of the deliverance in a third place as well, (7:141 — **“And ˹remember˺ when We rescued you from the people of Pharaoh”**).

The word for the trial is worth pausing on, since a trial is not always a loss. A long enslavement that ends in a rescue tests the people twice: first for whether they will hold on, and then for whether they will remember who opened the door. Because the ending places the hardship among the tests rather than among mere misfortunes, the reader is meant to look for what the years of suffering were shaping in the people who lived through them.

Think of a man released from a long imprisonment. The gates are open and the road is his, and the first thing the years have done to him is decide what he will do with his freedom. The verse keeps the memory of the cell in front of the freed man, because a person who forgets the pit is the one most likely to walk back toward it.

---

## Verse 2:50

> And ˹remember˺ when We parted the sea, rescued you, and drowned Pharaoh’s people before your very eyes

**THE WATER OPENED ON THEIR ACCOUNT**

The account moves to the night of the crossing: ***“And ˹remember˺ when”*** the barrier was broken, ***“We parted the sea”***. The verb used for the parting is explained by al-Qurṭubī, who records that it carries the sense of splitting, and who preserves the description of what the people saw, since each part of the water stood like a great mountain on either side of the path. The same verb is read by al-Ālūsī as a separation made between two things, with the sea treated as something cleaved and divided for the sake of the people being carried through it, and he notes that the preposition attaches the deed to them, since the opening happened because of them and for their rescue.

Ibn ʿUthaymīn keeps the sense of the verb on division and detachment: the water was split apart so that a way stood open, and the community walked through it and came out safe. Because the sea is the largest thing the story names, the reader is being shown that the obstacle standing between the people and their freedom was not removed but opened, and a road was made through the middle of it.

**THE TWO ENDS OF ONE WATER**

The second half of the verse closes the account with the fate of the pursuers: ***“rescued you”***, ***“and drowned Pharaoh’s people”*** ***“before your very eyes”***. Al-Jalālayn describes the division of the water so that the people could cross and escape the enemy, and he records both halves of the outcome, with the deliverance of the community and the drowning of the household of the tyrant. The pursuit, and the closing of the water on those who chased the community into the passage, are followed by Maʿārif al-Qurʾān.

The Book tells the same story twice more, and each telling keeps a detail: (26:65 — **“delivered Moses and those with him all together”**), and then, in the next line, (26:66 — **“Then We drowned the others”**). A third passage adds a command given to Moses at the same hour, (44:24 — **“And leave the sea parted”**), since the road was to stay open behind the people while the water waited for the army.

**WHAT THE CROSSING TEACHES**

Because the same water stands on both sides of the story, the miracle is not the changing of the sea but the arrangement of it. A path was made where no path could exist, and the pursuers who followed the path were taken by the very element that had held still for the others. That is why the verse ends with the community watching, since the deliverance was not described to them afterwards; they saw the last of it with their own eyes.

The same reading appears in Ibn ʿAbbās, with the rescuing of the people from the water and the drowning of Pharaoh and his household set side by side. The reason the memory is fixed on the eyes of the rescued is that gratitude is built out of what a person has seen, and a generation that has watched a door open for it will find it harder to treat the opening as chance. Since the two ends of the crossing are held in one sentence, the reader is meant to carry both the rescue and the ruin in a single view.

Think of a family carried across a flooded river on a bridge that appeared where no bridge had stood. Nobody in that family would describe the crossing as a lucky walk, and the water that swallowed the road behind them would be remembered as clearly as the road itself.

---

## Verse 2:51

> And ˹remember˺ when We appointed forty nights for Moses, then you worshipped the calf in his absence, acting wrongfully

**AN APPOINTMENT ON THE MOUNTAIN**

The next favour is the appointment at the mountain: ***“And ˹remember˺ when We appointed forty nights”*** ***“for Moses”***. The two ways the verb of appointment was recited among the early readers are recorded by al-Ṭabarī and al-Qurṭubī, one carrying the form of a meeting arranged from both sides and one without it, and both set out the case made for each. The form of mutual action is explained by al-Baghawī, which Arabic uses even when the deed comes from one side, as in the familiar blessing where a man says to another that God has healed him; the appointment was issued by God, and the coming to the mountain belonged to Moses, so both sides of the meeting are gathered in the form of the verb.

al-Jalālayn names what the forty nights were leading to, since that period closed with the giving of the Torah to Moses for the people to carry out. Because the appointment is counted among the favours in this stretch of the sūrah, the reader is meant to see the law itself as a gift rather than as a fence, and the Book counts it as a kindness that a people still recovering from slavery were handed a written direction for their life.

**WHAT HAPPENED WHILE HE WAS AWAY**

The sentence then turns, and the turn is the point of the verse: ***“then you worshipped the calf in his absence”***. The calf was fashioned and taken as a god while Moses was away on the mountain, as al-Jalālayn records, and he names the man behind the making of it. The same sequence is read by Ibn ʿAbbās, with the calf worshipped after Moses had gone up, and he keeps the verse's own verdict attached to the act.

The Book tells the appointment again in another sūrah, and the wording there adds a detail that this verse leaves out: (7:142 — **“We appointed for Moses thirty nights then added another ten”**), and the same verse goes on to name the period as forty nights brought to completion. The two appointments are read by al-Ālūsī as one term completed, with the nights counted in two stages until the full period stood finished.

**THE WRONG THAT FELL ON THE WRONGDOERS**

The verse closes with a verdict on the people rather than on the calf: ***“acting wrongfully”***. As-Saʿdī explains the harm of the act as returning on the one who does it, since the calf could neither hear nor answer, and the people who made it carried the loss in their own record. Since the appointment is named before the failure, the order of the account keeps the gift in front of the reader. Because the wrong is reported without any excuse attached to it, the blame stays where it belongs. The closing words are read by Ibn ʿAbbās as describing a people who placed themselves in the wrong by what they chose to worship.

Because the sentence ends with the wrong rather than with the calf, the reader is shown where the damage landed. A man who turns from his Maker does not reduce his Maker by a single thing; he damages the one who turns. That is why the verse can put a favour and a failure in one line and still call the whole memory a reminder, since the failure would be answered by a pardon in the next breath.

Think of a man who leaves a house he was given and sets up a tent beside the road, then complains that the winter is cold. The house stands where it stood, and the loss is entirely his own.

---

## Verse 2:52

> Even then We ˹still˺ forgave you so perhaps you would be grateful

**THE PARDON THAT CAME ANYWAY**

The verse opens with two words that carry the whole surprise: ***“Even then”***, and then the act itself, ***“We ˹still˺ forgave you”***. Al-Qurṭubī separates pardon from forgiveness in the language, and the difference he draws is worth keeping: pardon is the covering over of a wrong, and it may fall before a punishment or after it, while forgiveness is never joined to a punishment at all. A person who deserves a penalty and finds it lifted has been pardoned, and the same pardon was placed over a people caught in the middle of a great offence. Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the order of the verse, with the wrong acknowledged and the pardon following it, and he reads the sentence as a mercy that arrived before any claim could be made on it.

The pardon is read by al-Jalālayn as the erasing of the sin after the worship of the calf, and he keeps the order of the verse, with the offence first and the mercy after it. Because the mercy is stated without a condition attached in this sentence, the reader learns that the pardon was not earned by anything the people had done; it was placed over them while the shame of the act was still fresh.

**THE PURPOSE NAMED AT THE END**

The sentence then gives the aim of the pardon: ***“so perhaps”*** ***“you would be grateful”***. Maʿārif al-Qurʾān pauses on the word for hoping, which in the speech of God marks an expected outcome rather than a doubt, and he records that the community was forgiven only after it had offered repentance, as the later verse describes in detail. Since the act is reported without a condition attached to it, the pardon stands as a fact of the past rather than as a hope for the future. Ibn ʿUthaymīn reads the whole sequence as a chain of favours, where the pardon opens the way for the next gift and the gratitude is the response the gifts were aiming at.

The Book states the rule of that pardon in a general sentence, and the parallel is close: (4:110 — **“then seeks Allah’s forgiveness”**). The same lesson is placed in the mouths of the people themselves in another sūrah, where their remorse is recorded after the calf: (7:149 — **“If our Lord does not have mercy on us and forgive us, we will certainly be losers.”**). Because the offence and the pardon are both kept in the record, the reader is taught to hold both of them in view. That is why the closing words turn to gratitude rather than to guilt.

**WHY THE PARDON IS REMEMBERED**

Because the pardon is set beside the offence in the same reminder, the verse is teaching the reader how to hold his own record. A person who remembers only the wrong falls into despair, and a person who remembers only the mercy stops taking the wrong seriously. The verse keeps the two in one hand, and it stops at the point where the reader is expected to move.

The reason gratitude is named as the aim of the pardon is that gratitude is the one response that changes what a person does next. Since the mercy had already been given, nothing was left for the people to pay back; what remained was the shape of their next step. A forgiven community that has learned nothing will need the pardon again, and a forgiven community that has understood the gift will walk differently.

Like a debt written off by a lender who asks for nothing back, the pardon left the people free to fold the paper and forget the hand that held it. A debtor can take the paper and forget the lender, and the same debtor can take the paper and live as a man who owes his life to somebody. The second of those two responses is placed in the verse as the purpose the pardon was aiming at.

---

## Verse 2:53

> And ˹remember˺ when We gave Moses the Scripture—the standard ˹to distinguish between right and wrong˺ that perhaps you would be ˹rightly˺ guided

**THE BOOK THAT WAS HANDED OVER**

The reminder moves from the pardon to the gift of the Book: ***“And ˹remember˺ when We gave Moses the Scripture”***. Al-Qurṭubī identifies the Book as the Torah, and he records that the exegetes agree on it. The form of the verb used for the giving takes two objects in Arabic, as Ibn ʿUthaymīn notes, so that the sentence names both the man who received and the thing he was handed, and he contrasts it with the lighter verb of coming.

al-Ālūsī keeps the same identification and turns to the second noun joined to the first, since the Book is named twice in the verse for a purpose. The two names are not two books, as he reads the line, and the pairing is meant to point at two aspects of one gift: a text to be recited, and a measure by which a thing can be judged.

**THE STANDARD THAT DIVIDES**

The second name is then explained: ***“the standard”*** ***“˹to distinguish between right and wrong˺”***. The second noun is treated by al-Jalālayn as an addition that opens up the first, since the Book is the thing that separates truth from falsehood and puts a line between what is allowed and what is not. Because the Book was given before it was demanded, the guidance is shown as a mercy rather than as a wage. Since the gift came first, the reader is led by what he has been handed rather than by what he finds on his own. The same sense is given by Maʿārif al-Qurʾān: the term is the name for whatever separates truth from falsehood and distinguishes one matter from another.

al-Ālūsī collects the readings and sets them out, including the view that the second noun is another name for the same Book, joined to the first as an attribute is joined to what it describes, and the view that the separation the Book performs in the life of its followers is what the second name points at. The showing of the lawful and the unlawful, the commands and the prohibitions, is how Ibn ʿAbbās reads the pair, so that the community would know what to take and what to leave.

**A GIFT MEANT TO LEAD SOMEWHERE**

The verse closes with its aim: ***“that perhaps you would be ˹rightly˺ guided”***. The Book states the same purpose in another sūrah, and the wording is close: (23:49 — **“We certainly gave Moses the Scripture, so perhaps his people would be ˹rightly˺ guided”**). Because the gift is tied to a destination, the reader is told that a text left unread and unmoved has not yet done its work, and the giving of the Book was never an end in itself.

The reason the two names stand together in one verse is that a book and a measure ask two different things of a reader. A book asks to be read, and a measure asks to be used, and a community that recites without judging its own conduct by what it recites has taken half of the gift and left the other half on the table.

Think of a man who is given both a lamp and a set of scales. The lamp shows him the road, and the scales tell him what his goods are worth, and neither of the two does anything for him while it stays wrapped up on the shelf.

---

## Verse 2:54

> And ˹remember˺ when Moses said to his people, “O my people! Surely you have wronged yourselves by worshipping the calf, so turn in repentance to your Creator and execute ˹the calf-worshippers among˺ yourselves. That is best for you in the sight of your Creator.” Then He accepted your repentance. Surely He is the Accepter of Repentance, Most Merciful

**THE SPEECH OF MOSES TO HIS PEOPLE**

The reminder now becomes a conversation inside the community, and it opens with a tenderness that a reader might not expect after the offence: ***“And ˹remember˺ when Moses said to his people, “O my people!”*** al-Ṭabarī links this clause to the reminders before it, so that the whole sentence carries the force of a command to recall the hour when the leader stood among his own people and spoke. Al-Qurṭubī notes the sense of the noun for his people, the gathered group around him, and he records that the speech opens with a call that claims them even in the middle of the accusation.

Because the messenger addresses them as his own, the reproof arrives as a family matter rather than as a verdict from outside. Moses had gone up the mountain for their sake and come back to find them worshipping what their own hands had made, and the first word out of his mouth in the account is a word of belonging.

**THE WRONG THAT WAS DONE TO THE SELVES**

The accusation follows immediately: ***“Surely you have wronged yourselves by worshipping the calf”***. Ibn ʿAbbās reads the wrong as harm done to the wrongdoers themselves, and as-Saʿdī explains that the harm of a sin returns on the one who commits it, so the phrase turns the reader's attention away from any injury done to God and toward the injury done to the person who acts.

The Book keeps this pattern wherever it describes disobedience, and no act of a creature adds anything to the Creator or takes anything from Him. Because the loss is stated as falling on the ones who acted, the verse prepares the ground for the cure that comes next, since a person who understands that he has wounded himself will listen to the one who tells him how to be healed.

**TURNING BACK AND PAYING THE PRICE**

The instruction follows in two parts, and the first is the turning: ***“so turn in repentance to your Creator”***. Al-Qurṭubī and Maʿārif al-Qurʾān both pause on the name used for God here, the One who brings a thing into being, so that the people are told to return to the very One whose work they had ignored. The second part is the hardest sentence in the passage: ***“and execute ˹the calf-worshippers among˺ yourselves.”*** al-Ṭabarī records the command as it stands, and Ibn Kathīr describes its execution, with the community carrying it out among itself. Maʿārif al-Qurʾān explains the prescribed mode of repentance for that generation, which required those who had kept away from the calf to put to death those who had worshipped it.

The verse does not soften the requirement, and it also does not leave it as the last word. That is why the reader is told where the severity was pointing, since the next sentence names the verdict of God on the whole affair.

**WHAT THE REPENTANCE WAS WORTH**

The speech closes with a reason attached to the command: ***“That is best for you in the sight of your Creator.”*** Then the verse reports the outcome, ***“Then He accepted your repentance. Surely He is the Accepter of Repentance, Most Merciful”***. The Book records the remorse of that generation in another sūrah, where the words are put in their mouths: (7:149 — **“If our Lord does not have mercy on us and forgive us, we will certainly be losers.”**). And the invitation to return is repeated as a standing rule: (5:74 — **“Will they not turn to Allah in repentance and seek His forgiveness”**).

Because the acceptance is reported in the same verse as the command, the passage teaches that the door was opened before the people had finished walking through it. Because the whole affair ends with acceptance, the failure is treated as an episode rather than as the last word. A hard remedy was prescribed, and the One who prescribed it had already decided to receive them, which is why the sentence ends with two of the names that describe that welcome.

Think of a physician who tells a patient that the cure will cost him a painful operation, and who then stands at the door of the theatre himself. The knife is real, and so is the hand that arranged for it to be used.

---

## Verse 2:55

> And ˹remember˺ when you said, “O Moses! We will never believe you until we see Allah with our own eyes,” so a thunderbolt struck you while you were looking on

**A DEMAND RAISED TO THE MESSENGER**

The scene changes to the moment when the community turned on its own messenger: ***“And ˹remember˺ when you said, “O Moses!”*** and then, ***“We will never believe you”*** ***“until we see Allah with our own eyes,”***. The speakers are identified by al-Qurṭubī as the seventy men whom Moses had chosen, and he explains how the demand came after they had heard the speech of God and still asked for a sight of Him, even though belief in a messenger becomes binding once his signs have appeared. The same seventy are named by al-Ālūsī, chosen for the appointment of the Torah, and he records that some reports place the words after the community had returned and dealt with the worshippers of the calf.

al-Baghawī sets the scene at the mountain, where God had ordered Moses to come up with a group of the Children of Israel, and he keeps the demand in the mouths of the men who went. Because the words are addressed to a man rather than to God, the verse shows a community treating its messenger as the obstacle to be overcome, when the messenger was the very door that had been opened for it.

**THE ANSWER THAT FELL FROM THE SKY**

The consequence is stated with the same brevity: ***“so a thunderbolt struck you”*** ***“while you were looking on”***. Al-Jalālayn reads the bolt as the thing that seized them, and Ibn ʿAbbās describes the fire that came down and took them, with the sentence closing on the fact that they watched it happen. The Book describes the same demand in another sūrah, where the words of the people are reported and the answer is named in the same breath: (4:153 — **“Make Allah visible to us”**), and the same verse records the punishment that followed the demand.

The word for the demand is worth a moment, since the people asked for a sight rather than for a sign. Maʿārif al-Qurʾān follows the demand and the answer as one event, and he notes that the punishment was measured to the offence, since a demand to see God was met by a force that left them unable to see anything at all. Because the verse keeps the community alive as witnesses after the blow, the account is aimed at the people who were standing there, and their memory of the event is being used as a proof against them. Since the demand was made to a messenger rather than to God, the offence is shown to be a refusal of the road rather than a dispute about evidence. Because the punishment fell on the very men who had been chosen to witness, the lesson was written into the community itself.

**WHY THE DEMAND WAS SO SERIOUS**

A request is not usually a crime, and the verse treats this one as a disaster. The reason is that the demand was not for evidence but for a change in the terms of the relationship, since the people were telling their messenger that they would believe him only if the arrangement were reordered to suit them. Belief that waits for the evidence a person has picked in advance is not belief; it is a bargain that keeps the upper hand.

Because they had already been given a way to hear and to follow, their condition was a refusal dressed up as inquiry. That is why the answer came as it did, and why the account is placed among the favours of this stretch of the sūrah: a mercy that pulls a people back from that kind of bargaining is a gift, even when the pull hurts.

Think of a passenger who tells the pilot that he will not stay seated unless he is allowed into the cockpit to check the controls himself. The demand sounds like caution, and what it really asks for is the end of the flight.

---

## Verse 2:56

> Then We brought you back to life after your death, so that perhaps you would be grateful

**LIFE RETURNED AFTER THE FALL**

The next favour answers the last one directly: ***“Then We brought”*** ***“you back to life”*** ***“after your death”***. The verb of raising is explained by al-Ṭabarī as the giving of life, and he traces the root behind it, which carries the sense of stirring a thing out of its place, the way a man rouses a camel from the spot where it knelt so that it will rise and travel. The phrase is read by Ibn ʿAbbās as life restored after the burning that had taken them, and he keeps the order of the sentence, with the death first and the raising after it.

That the word for raising should carry the sense of movement out of a resting place is fitting in this passage, because the community was being brought out of one condition and set into another. Because the death is placed before the life in the sentence, the reader is asked to take the measure of the gift, since a people brought back from nothing owed everything to the hand that raised them.

**THE PURPOSE NAMED AT THE END**

The sentence closes with the aim of the gift: ***“so that perhaps”*** ***“you would be grateful”***. Al-Jalālayn reads the raising as a favour given so that the people would be thankful for it, and he keeps the tone of the word for hoping, which marks what the gift was aimed at rather than any uncertainty in the giver. The fear that had come over Moses when the bolt took the people he had brought with him is described by Maʿārif al-Qurʾān, since he expected to be suspected of causing their deaths; the raising of the dead answered that fear as well as their own need.

The Book states the same truth about life and death in another place, where it addresses the community that had watched it happen: (2:28 — **“You were lifeless and He gave you life”**). Ibn ʿUthaymīn treats the raising as a favour that follows the death by a clear step, and he keeps the gratitude as the response the whole account was written to produce. Because the pattern of death followed by life runs through the whole sūrah, this verse is one instance of a rule that will be applied to every reader at the end of the road.

**WHAT A GIFT LIKE THAT ASKS FOR**

Gratitude is the response named, and it is a strange word for a people who had just demanded proof. A person who is brought back from death and then thanks the one who raised him has learned something that argument could not teach, and the verse suggests that the lesson was the real gift inside the rescue. Because the gift was given without conditions, the thanks it asks for is not a payment; it is the recognition of where a life came from.

That is why the account is placed beside the demands and the disobedience in this stretch of the sūrah. A community that had been raised from the ground and then carried through the sea had no ground left for haggling, and the reminder of its own rescue was meant to close the argument before it started.

Think of a swimmer pulled out of a river by a stranger's hand. He may argue about many things afterwards, and he cannot argue about who held his arm, and the verse keeps that hand in front of the reader while it asks him for thanks.

---

## Verse 2:57

> And ˹remember when˺ We shaded you with clouds and sent down to you manna and quails, ˹saying˺, “Eat from the good things We have provided for you.” The evildoers ˹certainly˺ did not wrong Us, but wronged themselves

**SHADE OVER A PEOPLE IN THE OPEN**

The account of the wilderness opens with shelter: ***“And ˹remember when˺ We shaded you with clouds”***. The noun for the cloud is explained by al-Qurṭubī as a plural form, the way the word for a cloud and the word for clouds stand beside each other, and he records the note that the name is built on covering, since a cloud covers the sky it passes under. Al-Jalālayn describes the shade as the work of fine white clouds spread above the people to protect them from the heat of the sun while they were in the open country.

Ibn ʿAbbās gives the same picture of the white cloud over the wilderness, and he keeps the shade as the first of the two provisions named in the verse. Because the people were travelling through land that offered neither roof nor water, the shading is placed before the food, and the order shows a kindness that answered a need the community had not yet voiced. Because the provision is named before the complaint that follows later in the sūrah, the reader is told what the complaint was really about.

**FOOD THAT ARRIVED WITHOUT A FIELD**

The verse then names the provision: ***“and sent down to you manna”*** ***“and quails”***. The first is read by Ibn ʿAbbās as honey and the second as the small birds, and al-Jalālayn keeps the two as the food that came down on the people in the wilderness. The scene is placed by Maʿārif al-Qurʾān in the open country where the community wandered after it had left Egypt, and he notes how far the people were from the sorts of soil and market that sustain an ordinary town.

The clause is joined by al-Ṭabarī to the raising of the previous verse, so that the account reads as one chain of gifts handed to the same people in the same years. Because the food arrived without a harvest and the shade arrived without a building, the passage is describing provision that depended on nothing the people controlled, which is exactly the condition the reminder was meant to place in front of them.

**THE COMMAND TO EAT FREELY**

A permission is attached to the gift: ***“˹saying˺, “Eat from the good things We have provided”*** ***“for you.”*** The Book tells the same wilderness story in another sūrah, and the wording there matches this verse almost line for line: (7:160 — **“We shaded them with clouds and sent down to them manna and quails”**). The permission to eat from what God provides is stated as a standing rule for the community that believes, (2:172 — **“Eat from the good things We have provided for you”**).

Because the instruction is a permission rather than a restriction, the verse shows that the goodness of the gift was meant to be enjoyed and not merely endured. A people who are fed by a hand they cannot see are being taught who owns the field, and the freedom to eat is part of the lesson.

**THE HARM THAT NEVER REACHED THE GIVER**

The verse closes by naming where the harm of a refusal would have landed: ***“The evildoers ˹certainly˺ did not wrong Us”*** ***“but wronged themselves”***. Ibn ʿAbbās reads the sentence as a statement of fact about the ones who turned away from the provision, with no loss falling on God and the whole of it resting on them, and al-Jalālayn keeps the same sense of a harm that returns to its author.

The reason the sentence is placed after the food and the shade is that ingratitude is best exposed where a gift has just been described. A community that has been shaded and fed and still complains has not injured the provider; the damage falls on its own capacity to receive, and a favour begins to look like a right. Because the provision is named before the complaint that comes later in the sūrah, the reader is told what the complaint was really about. Since the sentence ends with the harm resting on the doers, gratitude is presented as the interest of the one who gives thanks.

Think of a traveller who is fed at a stranger's table and then finds fault with the cooking on his way out of the door. The host loses a plate of food and the traveller loses a friend, and the verse keeps the reader's eye on the second of those two losses.

---

## Verse 2:58

> And ˹remember˺ when We said, “Enter this city and eat freely from wherever you please; enter the gate with humility, saying, ‘Absolve us.’ We will forgive your sins and multiply the reward for the good-doers.”

**A CITY OFFERED AFTER THE YEARS OF WANDERING**

The favours continue with a home: ***“And ˹remember˺ when We said, “Enter this city”*** ***“and eat freely from wherever you please”***. al-Ṭabarī records the reports that name the place, and the weight of them points to the Holy House, while al-Jalālayn offers the two names that the early readers debated, the Holy House itself or the town of Jericho. Ibn ʿAbbās settles on Jericho and reads the sentence as an invitation to eat freely of what the town held, without measure and without restriction.

as-Saʿdī places the gift in the sequence of mercies rather than of punishments, and he opens out the three things the verse hands over: a place that would be a home, a provision that would be abundant, and a manner of entering that would cost the people their pride. Because the town is given after a long stretch of wandering, the reader is shown a mercy that follows failure, and the doors of a settled life are opened to a people who had worn out the desert.

**THE MANNER OF ENTERING**

The verse then sets the terms of the entry: ***“enter the gate with humility, saying, ‘Absolve us.’ We will forgive your sins”*** ***“and multiply the reward”*** ***“for the good-doers.”*** al-Jalālayn keeps the sense of the bodily posture in the first clause, since the people were commanded to bow as they went in, and Maʿārif al-Qurʾān explains the plea they were told to say, which names the request for pardon and carries the acknowledgement of sin inside it.

A second telling of the same hour is preserved in another sūrah, where the invitation and the posture are both repeated: (7:161 — **“Enter this city ˹of Jerusalem˺”**). That the promise ends with a multiplied reward shows the balance of the verse, since the entry is asked for with lowliness before it is answered with generosity.

**WHY THE LOWLINESS WAS THE POINT**

A gate is a small thing to ask a people to bend under, and the verse makes the bending the condition of the whole gift. Because the community had spent years measuring its worth by what it had suffered, the command gave it a way to set the memory down without losing the favour. The posture and the plea were the two halves of one movement: the body lowered and the tongue honest.

The noun for the town is used in the Book for any settled place, whether a village or a great city, so the promise is not pinned to one site. The reason a free provision is handed to a people who must bow to receive it is that the gift would otherwise be carried as a right. A person who enters a town with his head down has already learned who opened the gate, and the pardon named in the same sentence keeps that lesson attached to the reward that follows it. Because the entry and the forgiveness stand in one sentence, the reader is shown that the people were received before they were tested. Because the pardon is stated in the same breath as the invitation, the reader is shown that the people were not being asked to earn the town. Since the reward is promised to the good-doers, the mercy of the entry and the work that follows it are held in one hand.

Think of a family invited to live in a house they could never have bought. The owner asks them to come in through the small door at the side and to say what they need, and the house is theirs.

---

## Verse 2:59

> But the wrongdoers changed the words they were commanded to say. So We sent down a punishment from the heavens upon them for their rebelliousness

**THE WORDS THAT WERE SWAPPED**

The account turns on a single act of tampering: ***“But the wrongdoers changed the words”*** ***“they were commanded to say.”*** al-Ṭabarī explains the verb of change as an alteration, and he identifies the people named as those who did what they had no right to do, so that the offence is measured by the standing of the command rather than by the size of the edit. al-Jalālayn records the proverb they put in the place of the words they were given, and adds that they entered the town dragging themselves along the ground. Ibn ʿAbbās records the single word they substituted, a word about a crop, which shows how small the swap looked to the people who made it.

The same story is carried in another sūrah with the same verdict at the end of it, (7:162 — **“But the wrongdoers among them changed the words they were commanded to say”**). Because the substitution is named as the act of the wrongdoers rather than of the community, the reader is told that the command had reached everyone and that a part of the people answered it with contempt.

**THE ANSWER FROM ABOVE**

Theverse then reports the consequence: ***“So We sent down a punishment”*** ***“from the heavens upon them”*** ***“for their rebelliousness”***. al-Jalālayn keeps the descent of the punishment on the evildoers and names the cause as the departure from the command. Maʿārif al-Qurʾān reads the episode as a warning that follows the gift immediately, since the town and the provision were handed over in one verse and the plague is named in the next.
