As-Saʿdī reads the descent of the punishment as the answer to a word that was changed rather than to a town that was refused, since the gift had been accepted and the instruction had been altered. The reason the punishment is described as coming down rather than as being imposed is that the offence was committed in the open air of the town they were entering. A people who had been given instruction at the gate met a penalty at the gate, and the correspondence between the offence and the answer is part of what makes the record worth keeping.

**A SMALL CHANGE WITH A LARGE PRICE**

Nothing in the verse suggests that the people abandoned the town, the provision or the covenant. They kept the gift and altered the wording, which is the kind of failure that survives inside a religious community long after the doors have been opened for it. That is why the sentence stays in the sūrah as a warning: the danger is not always a refusal to enter, since a man may enter and still rewrite what he was told to say.

The wrong here is placed on the tongue, and the punishment is attached to it, because the tongue is where a community keeps its account of what it believes. A word changed at the gate becomes a habit kept for generations, and the verse treats the first substitution as the seed of the later ones. Because the punishment is tied to the alteration rather than to the entry, the reader is shown where the offence lay. Since the same verdict is recorded in another sūrah, the account is carried as a settled warning rather than as a local report.

Think of a clerk who is handed a form with one line to sign and alters the line to suit himself before filing it. The office is open, the form is accepted, and the record now says something that nobody requested.

---

## Verse 2:60

> And ˹remember˺ when Moses prayed for water for his people, We said, “Strike the rock with your staff.” Then twelve springs gushed out, ˹and˺ each tribe knew its drinking place. ˹We then said,˺ “Eat and drink of Allah’s provisions, and do not go about spreading corruption in the land.”

**A PRAYER FOR WATER IN THE OPEN COUNTRY**

The wilderness account continues with thirst: ***“And ˹remember˺ when Moses prayed”*** ***“for water for his people, We said, “Strike the rock with your staff.”*** al-Ṭabarī sets the request in the dry country and records the answer that came to Moses in the middle of it, and Maʿārif al-Qurʾān keeps the scene in the same years of wandering that produced the hunger of the manna and the quails.

Ibn ʿAbbās describes the rock itself as a stone given to Moses for the purpose, and al-Jalālayn records the description carried down about its size and its shape, along with the detail that it was the rock that moved with the people. Because the command was given to a man with a staff rather than to the sky with a cloud, the reader sees a provision that depended on obedience in the very hour it was needed.

**TWELVE SPRINGS AND TWELVE TRIBES**

The outcome is stated in a number: ***“Then twelve springs gushed out, ˹and˺ each tribe knew its drinking place.”*** as-Saʿdī ties the number to the twelve tribes of the community, so that each branch knew the water set for it and no group lived on the ration of another. The Book tells the same hour again in another sūrah, with the same two details, (7:160 — **“Then twelve springs gushed out”**), and the line records that each tribe knew its drinking place.

The reason the provision came with a place assigned to each family is that the crowd was large and the water was scarce. A provision that arrives without an order turns into a fight at the well, and the verse keeps the order inside the mercy, since the same hand that opened the rock set the queue. Because the springs were given to families rather than to a crowd, the provision was arranged to hold a community together rather than to test its patience. Since the water followed the strike of a staff, the reader is shown that the means were named by God and the act was carried out by a man.

**THE COMMAND THAT GUARDED THE GIFT**

The verse closes with an instruction that reaches past the thirst: ***“Eat and drink of Allah’s provisions”*** ***“and do not go about spreading corruption in the land.”*** al-Jalālayn keeps both halves of the sentence, with the permission to eat and drink set beside the prohibition of disorder, and as-Saʿdī reads the closing clause as the guard on the whole account, since a people fed by a miracle can still ruin the country they pass through.

Because the command is placed after the water and not before it, the reader is shown that the gift came first and the obligation followed it. The community was fed before it was warned, and the warning names the one thing that would turn a wilderness full of springs back into a quarrel.

Think of a household that is given a well in the yard. The well is a gift, and the rule about not selling the water to the neighbours at a price is what keeps the household worth living in.

---

## Verse 2:61

> And ˹remember˺ when you said, “O Moses! We cannot endure the same meal ˹every day˺. So ˹just˺ call upon your Lord on our behalf, He will bring forth for us some of what the earth produces of herbs, cucumbers, garlic, lentils, and onions.” Moses scolded ˹them˺, “Do you exchange what is better for what is worse? ˹You can˺ go down to any village and you will find what you have asked for.” They were stricken with disgrace and misery, and they invited the displeasure of Allah for rejecting Allah’s signs and unjustly killing the prophets. This is ˹a fair reward˺ for their disobedience and violations

**A PEOPLE TIRED OF THE FOOD**

The account of the wilderness turns into complaint: ***“And ˹remember˺ when you said, “O Moses!”*** and then ***“We cannot endure the same meal ˹every day˺.”*** al-Ṭabarī records the request in full and keeps the tone of the sentence, where a community addressed its prophet about the table it had been given. as-Saʿdī reads the complaint as weariness with the favour itself and a low view of it, since manna and quails had been provided without labour and the people had begun to despise what cost them nothing.

Ibn ʿAbbās names the food they were tired of as honey and quails, and he keeps the request for vegetables in the mouths of the askers. Because the sentence is a request rather than a refusal of God, the offence sits in the smallness of the appetite rather than in open denial, and the verse treats appetite as a serious matter for a community being fed by a miracle. Because the food had been provided without labour, the complaint was not about hunger but about sameness, and the boredom of a delivered people is treated here as a serious matter. Since the gift had arrived without a field or a market, the request for the produce of the earth was a request to go back to the arrangements of Egypt.

**THE LIST OF THE EARTH'S PRODUCE**

The request is then set out item by item: ***“So ˹just˺ call upon your Lord on our behalf, He will bring forth”*** ***“for us some of what the earth produces of herbs, cucumbers, garlic, lentils”*** ***“and onions.”*** al-Jalālayn keeps the list as the products of the soil and notes the smallness of the terms in the mouths of the people, and Maʿārif al-Qurʾān places the episode in the same years of wandering as the water and the shade.

The messenger's answer is reported in the same verse: ***“Moses scolded ˹them˺, “Do you exchange what is better”*** ***“for what is worse?”*** The Book repeats that rebuke as a question, which is how the sūrah has handled every failure in this stretch, and the reader is left to answer it. Since the list is drawn from the produce of the field, the request was for the ordinary food of a settled country, and the community had begun to measure its freedom by its menu. Because the refusal is answered with a question rather than with a blow, the people are given a chance to hear their own request.

**THE VERDICT THAT FOLLOWS THE TABLE**

The closing sentences describe what the complaint brought: ***“˹You can˺ go down to any village”*** ***“and you will find what you have asked”*** ***“for.” They were stricken with disgrace”*** ***“and misery, and they invited the displeasure of Allah”*** ***“for rejecting Allah’s signs”*** ***“and unjustly killing the prophets. This is ˹a fair reward˺”*** ***“for their disobedience and violations”***. The Book places the same verdict on the community in another sūrah, where the humiliation travels with them wherever they settle, (3:112 — **“They will be stricken with disgrace wherever they go”**), and the reason for the wrath is named in the same passage, (4:155 — **“killing the prophets unjustly”**).

Ibn ʿAbbās reads the killing of the prophets as part of a long record rather than a single act, and he keeps the whole verdict attached to the refusal of the signs. Because the sentence reaches past food to the murder of messengers, the reader learns that a community's appetite and its violence grow from the same root, since both begin with the belief that the gift exists to serve the one who received it.

**WHY A SMALL APPETITE IS A LARGE DANGER**

A dish is a small thing to argue about, and the verse treats the argument as the doorway to the worst line in the sūrah. Because the request was made to a prophet rather than to a market, the people were asking for a different arrangement with the One who had fed them. Since the verdict reaches the prophets as well as the table, the sentence shows where a followed appetite can lead, and the reader is left to work out the distance between the two ends of the story. The reason is that the request was not for food but for a different arrangement with God, one in which the people chose the menu and the messenger carried the order. Once that arrangement is accepted in the heart, the refusal of a sign is only a matter of time.

That is why the reproof names the exchange rather than the hunger. A man may hunger and be given bread, and a man may hunger and demand the food of Egypt, and the difference between the two is the whole of the lesson.

Think of a guest at a table who is served by the host and then asks the servant to fetch what the neighbours are eating. The food on the plate is good, and the request says something about the guest. The host may grant the dish and still mark the moment, and the verse records both the dish and the mark, which is why the account of the food ends with a line about the prophets.

---

## Verse 2:62

> Indeed, the believers, Jews, Christians, and Sabians—whoever ˹truly˺ believes in Allah and the Last Day and does good will have their reward with their Lord. And there will be no fear for them, nor will they grieve

**THE GROUPS NAMED IN ONE SENTENCE**

The sūrah pauses to name four communities in a single line: ***“Indeed, the believers, Jews, Christians”*** ***“and Sabians whoever ˹truly˺ believes in Allah”*** ***“and the Last Day”*** ***“and does good will have their reward with their Lord.”*** al-Ṭabarī identifies the first group as those who affirm the Messenger in what came to him, and al-Jalālayn lists the rest in the order of the verse, with Jewry and the Christians and the Sabaeans. as-Saʿdī records the position that the ruling concerns the People of the Book, since the Sabaeans are counted among the Christian sects in his reading.

Ibn ʿAbbās reads the promise as a reward kept with the Lord for every one of those groups who believed in God and the Last Day and did good. Because the sentence gathers four names and then states one condition, the reader is told at once that the condition is what matters, and the labels serve the condition rather than replace it.

**THE TWO PROMISES AT THE END**

The verse then turns from reward to peace: ***“And there will be no fear”*** ***“for them, nor will they grieve”***. The same pair of promises is stated in another sūrah with almost the same wording, (5:69 — **“there will be no fear for them, nor will they grieve”**), and Maʿārif al-Qurʾān reads the placing of this verse here as a correction of the impression that the account of the Israelites had left, since the judgement falls on conduct rather than on descent.

The reason fear and grief are named rather than security and joy is that the two fears of a believer are the future and the past. A person is afraid of what is coming and grieved by what has gone, and the promise covers both directions in six words, so that the one who believes and does good is spoken to where he actually lives.

**WHAT THE VERSE DOES NOT SAY**

The verse does not say that the names carry anything on their own, and it does not say that belief is enough without good action. What it does is set the two together: the heart that holds the Last Day and the hand that does what is good. Because the Book has just described a community that kept its label and broke its covenants, the reader is in a position to see the difference between a name and a life.

That is why the sentence opens with a word of emphasis rather than with a comparison. The Book is not ranking communities here; it is stating the rule by which every community is weighed, and the rule is applied to the people of this sūrah before it is applied to anyone else. Because the verse stands between two accounts of failure, it is placed where a reader needs it most, since the sūrah has just shown what happens to a community that keeps its labels and loses its conduct. Since the promise is attached to faith and good work, the two are shown to be the parts of one life rather than two separate merits.

Picture four caravans arriving at one gate. The officer at the gate does not ask for the name of the caravan; he asks whether the travellers have brought what the road required of them.

---

## Verse 2:63

> And ˹remember˺ when We took a covenant from you and raised the mountain above you ˹saying˺, “Hold firmly to that ˹Scripture˺ which We have given you and observe its teachings so perhaps you will become mindful ˹of Allah˺.”

**A COVENANT THAT CARRIED WEIGHT**

The verse returns to the pledges of the community: ***“And ˹remember˺ when We took a covenant from you”*** ***“and raised the mountain above you”***. al-Ṭabarī explains the noun for the covenant as a binding pledge, taken by an oath or an agreement or by whatever else makes a matter firm, and he keeps the raising of the mountain as part of the same event. al-Jalālayn records that the mountain was lifted above the people when they held back from accepting the Book, and as-Saʿdī describes the pledge as a heavy one, reinforced by the sight that stood over their heads.

Because the mountain is named in the same sentence as the covenant, the reader is shown a contract signed under a weight, and the Book is asking a later generation to remember what their forefathers accepted under those conditions.

**THE TWO COMMANDS THAT FOLLOWED**

The words spoken at that hour are given in the middle of the verse: ***“˹saying˺, “Hold firmly to”*** ***“that ˹Scripture˺ which We have given you”*** ***“and observe its teachings”***. The Book repeats the same command in another sūrah, where the mountain is described as a cloud that the people expected to fall on them, (7:171 — **“We raised the mountain over them”**), and the instruction is carried there in the same words. Ibn ʿAbbās reads the command as the taking of the people's consent, with the mountain fixed above their heads to make the moment real.

The second command is not a repetition of the first, since holding firmly is a posture of the will and observing is the work that follows it. Because the verse joins the two, the reader is told that the Book was not handed over for admiration; it was handed over to be carried and practised.

**THE HOPE ATTACHED TO THE COMMAND**

The sentence ends with its aim: ***“so perhaps you will become mindful ˹of Allah˺.”*** al-Jalālayn keeps the hope as the purpose of the whole arrangement, and Maʿārif al-Qurʾān describes the return of Moses from the mountain with the Torah in his hands and the reciting of it to a people who found the instructions strict. The Book names the same act elsewhere with the mountain in the sky, (4:154 — **“We raised the Mount over them”**), and the whole story is recorded so that the reader may weigh what a covenant costs.

The reason mindfulness is named as the aim is that a law observed without it becomes a set of habits. A community can keep the letter of an agreement for generations and lose the awareness that made the agreement possible, and the verse ties the practice to the state of the heart that gives the practice its value.

Think of an apprentice who is given the tools of a trade and told to hold them firmly. The tools are worth nothing in a box, and the work they are meant to produce is the reason they were handed over. Because the mountain was raised in the sight of the people, the covenant was made under conditions that left no room for a later claim of ignorance. Since the two commands were joined in one sentence, the holding and the doing are shown to be one act rather than two stages.

---

## Verse 2:64

> Yet you turned away afterwards. Had it not been for Allah’s grace and mercy upon you, you would have certainly been of the losers

**THE TURNING THAT FOLLOWED THE PLEDGE**

The verse reports the failure that came after the covenant: ***“Yet you turned away afterwards.”*** ***“Had it not been”*** ***“for Allah’s grace and mercy upon you”*** ***“you would have certainly been of the losers”***. al-Ṭabarī explains the verb of turning as the act of putting a thing behind the back, and he notes that the word is used of everyone who abandons what he was told to keep, so that the failing is described by the gesture rather than by the size of the departure.

al-Jalālayn reads the sentence briefly, with the turning away placed after the pledge and the loss avoided only by the bounty of God. Maʿārif al-Qurʾān sets out the weight of the offence, since the agreement had been taken under the mountain, and he records that the community deserved destruction and was spared.

**THE GRACE AND THE MERCY**

The second half holds out what kept the community back from ruin: ***“for Allah’s grace and mercy upon you”*** ***“you would have certainly been of the losers”***. The grace is read by Ibn ʿAbbās as the deferring of the punishment, and the mercy as the sending of the Messenger to them, so that the two names cover the length of time given to the community and the guidance placed inside it.

The Book states the same rule in another sūrah, where the grace and mercy are named as the reason a plot against the Messenger came to nothing, (4:113 — **“Had it not been for Allah’s grace and mercy”**). Because the sentence ends with the word for losers, the reader is told what the outcome would have been, and the whole of the difference between the two ends is placed in a mercy that the community had not earned.

**WHAT THE VERSE TEACHES A READER WHO TURNS**

Nobody reading this verse is a member of that community, and the sentence has been kept for everyone who signs a pledge and then walks away from it. The reason the verse reports the failure and the rescue in one line is that both are true of the same people, and a record that hides either half would teach the wrong lesson.

As-Saʿdī keeps the pledge in view at the close of the account, since the mountain had been raised as a warning and the community had still turned away from what it accepted. A community that is told only about its failure loses hope, and a community that is told only about its rescue learns to treat the pledge lightly. The verse keeps the turning and the mercy side by side, which is how the reader is meant to hold his own record, with the failure named and the mercy credited to God.

Think of a man who breaks a promise and is given more time than the promise was worth. The delay is not a payment for the broken word; it is a chance to keep it. Because the mercy is named after the offence rather than before it, the verse keeps the reader's attention on what he does with the time he has been given. Since the loss is named as the alternative to the mercy, the two outcomes stand in front of the reader as a choice rather than as a fate. The reason the sentence follows a record of pledges is that a broken pledge is the place where a person most needs to know that mercy is still at work.

---

## Verse 2:65

> You are already aware of those of you who broke the Sabbath. We said to them, “Be disgraced apes!”

**A KNOWN INCIDENT FROM THEIR OWN RECORD**

The verse addresses the community with an event it already knows: ***“You are already aware of those of you”*** ***“who broke the Sabbath.”*** ***“We said to them”*** ***“Be disgraced apes!”*** al-Ṭabarī begins with the meaning of the Sabbath itself, which he explains from the sense of rest and stillness, and he identifies the offenders as those who went past the limit on the day set for worship. al-Jalālayn names the town where it happened and the act by which the command was broken, with the fish that came on the day of rest and were absent on the other days.

The noun for the day carries the sense of rest and stillness, and the day was set apart from the working week by that rest. as-Saʿdī points the reader to the fuller account in another sūrah, where the story is spread out and the details of the trap are given, and he ties the punishment to the gravity of the offence. Because the community was trusted with a day of rest, the offence was a breach of a gift rather than of a restriction. Since the verdict is reported as a word spoken to the offenders, the reader is shown that the whole matter stood in front of God from the first moment. Ibn ʿAbbās places the incident in the time of David, and Maʿārif al-Qurʾān keeps the same setting, with the day of rest specially appointed for the community and the fishing forbidden on it.

**THE VERDICT THAT IS STILL READ**

The sentence closes with the command given to the offenders: ***“We said to them”*** ***“Be disgraced apes!”*** The Book reports the same verdict in another place with almost the same words, (7:166 — **“Be disgraced apes”**), and as-Saʿdī reads the transformation as an exemplary punishment rather than a private misfortune. Because the community already knows the story, the reader is told that the memory of the punishment had been carried in its own teaching.

The reason the account is placed here, after the covenant and the turning away, is that the Sabbath is a case of the same fault in a plainer form. A day had been set aside and a rule given with it, and the offenders found a way to keep the day while breaking the rule, which is the trick of every community that wants the name of a command without its cost.

**WHAT THE TRANSFORMATION SIGNALS**

The punishment is described in the language of the body rather than of the law, since the people were made into what they had behaved like. A community that had stopped taking its own rules seriously lost the standing that came with being human before God, and the verse keeps the fate of those men in front of every reader who finds a way around a command.

Because the verdict is stated as a word spoken rather than as a slow decline, the reader sees a single act of judgement in the middle of a history. The Book does not describe the years of habit that prepared the offence; the sentence steps from the breaking of the day to the end of the story, and the reader is left to fill in the interval for himself.

Think of a man hired to guard a house who locks the door and climbs out through a window each night. The house is still guarded in the register, and the owner of the house reads the record. Because the offence was committed with a device rather than with a refusal, the verse shows that a command can be defeated by cleverness where it could not be defeated by open denial. Since the punishment is remembered by the community itself, the story carries the force of a witness rather than of a rumour.

---

## Verse 2:66

> So We made their fate an example to present and future generations, and a lesson to the God-fearing

**WHAT WAS MADE AN EXAMPLE**

The verse explains why the account has been kept: ***“So We made their fate”*** ***“an example”*** ***“to present and future generations,”*** ***“and a lesson to the God-fearing”***. al-Ṭabarī works through the pronoun in the first clause and records two readings from Ibn ʿAbbās about what exactly was made an example, whether the punishment itself or the town that suffered it, and both readings keep the purpose of the sentence, which is that the event stands as a warning.

al-Jalālayn reads the example as a punishment that dissuades others from the same act, given for the people of that generation and for those who came after them, and The views on what exactly was made the example are set out by al-Qurṭubī, with the punishment named first and the town and the people counted among the readings. As-Saʿdī ties the punishment to the gravity of the offence, since the trap was set with full knowledge of the command. The same order is kept by Ibn ʿAbbās, with the chastisement set for those who were present and a lesson set for those who followed.

**THE TWO AUDIENCES NAMED**

The sentence names two audiences, the people of the time and the generations after them, which is a rare thing to find in a story that is told about a single town. Because the lesson is addressed to both, the reader is told that the record was never intended as local history, and the men who broke the Sabbath are made into a permanent case study for everyone who reads the sūrah.

The second audience is narrowed further in the closing words: ***“and a lesson to the God-fearing”***. The warning is not offered to everybody, since a person who has no fear of God learns nothing from a punishment except that somebody else suffered. The Book says as much in the same breath, and the effect is that the reader who trembles is the one the verse was waiting for.

**WHY THE STORY IS KEPT**

A community that keeps its history keeps the record of its own disasters, and the verse states the purpose of keeping this one. The transformation of the Sabbath-breakers is remembered not to humiliate a town that is long gone but to protect every town that will come after it, and the Book states that the punishment was aimed at what it would prevent.

The reason the lesson is attached to fear rather than to knowledge is that knowledge by itself can be filed away. A person may know the story of the apes and still find a route around his own duties, and the only audience that receives it is named in the closing words, which is the one that reads the record with a mind to obey. Because the example was set for two generations at once, the lesson is meant to travel from the people who saw the punishment to the people who only heard of it. Since the last words of the verse are aimed at the God-fearing, the record closes with an invitation rather than with a threat. The reason the sentence does not end with the fate of the offenders is that the account was kept for the living, and the reader is left with a question about his own conduct rather than with a story about a town he will never visit.

Think of a village that keeps a broken bridge in its memory and puts a marker at the point where it collapsed. The travellers who slow down at the marker are the ones the marker was placed for.

---

## Verse 2:67

> And ˹remember˺ when Moses said to his people, “Allah commands you to sacrifice a cow.” They replied, “Are you mocking us?” Moses responded, “I seek refuge in Allah from acting foolishly!”

**A COMMAND THAT SOUNDED LIKE A JOKE**

The long account of the cow opens with an instruction and a misunderstanding: ***“And ˹remember˺ when Moses said to his people”*** that ***“Allah commands you to sacrifice a cow.”*** al-Jalālayn places the scene at the point where a man had been killed among the community and no killer could be named, so the people had asked Moses to pray for the truth to be shown, and the answer that came back was this command. as-Saʿdī describes the same quarrel, with a community divided over a death and the affair growing until it threatened a greater evil, and the instruction arriving as the way out of the impasse. Al-Ālūsī reads the opening as a deliberate turn to the failings of the same people, since the favours already recited had carried the record of their opposition to the prophets inside them.

The command arrived in the middle of an unsolved murder, and that is why the story is told inside this sūrah. The Book does not present the cow as a lesson in farming; it presents a people who were given a way to end a dispute and answered it with questions.

**THE ACCUSATION AND THE REFUGE**

The answer of the people is reported in three words: ***“They replied, “Are you mocking us?”*** The charge is aimed at a prophet, and the reply that follows is one of the strongest statements in the account: ***“Moses responded, “I seek refuge in Allah from acting foolishly!”*** Ibn Kathīr keeps the exchange in the same order, with the people accusing and the messenger refusing the charge with a refuge taken in God.

Ibn ʿAbbās reads the command as applying to any cow, and he notes that the people were free to take the first animal that answered the description. Because the refusal to act was dressed up as a question about the messenger's seriousness, the verse holds a mirror to every audience that treats a clear order as though it needed a second opinion. The Book records the same refuge in another place, where the reader is taught to say it before reciting: (16:98 — **“seek refuge with Allah”**). A reading preserved by al-Qurṭubī gives the command a lighter ending than the one most reciters use, and he keeps the meaning of the sentence unchanged.

**WHY THE ACCOUNT IS PLACED HERE**

The sūrah has just finished describing the covenant, the mountain and the breaking of the Sabbath, and it now turns to a generation that stood in front of a command and argued with the man who brought it. The reason the story runs for so many verses is that the argument repeats itself with a new demand each time, which is exactly the way a reluctant community negotiates, and the Book lets the reader watch the whole negotiation rather than the outcome alone.

The charge against the messenger is the key to the passage. A person who suspects a prophet of playing games has already decided that the command is negotiable, and the refuge Moses takes shows where such a suspicion belongs, since the one thing the messenger will not accept is the suggestion that God speaks in jest. Because the command arrived while a quarrel was still open, a way out of a dispute can look like a strange demand rather than like a rescue. Since the people answered a prophet with suspicion, the account warns every community that hears a clear instruction and looks for the messenger's motive instead of the command's author.

The scene is like a household that asks a doctor for help and then questions his prescription because the medicine is bitter. The doctor is not offended by the question; he is offended by being treated as a man who prescribed it for sport.

---

## Verse 2:68

> They said, “Call upon your Lord to clarify for us what type ˹of cow˺ it should be!” He replied, “Allah says, ‘The cow should neither be old nor young but in between. So do as you are commanded!’”

**THE FIRST QUESTION THAT ADDED NOTHING**

The people come back with a request rather than with obedience: ***“They said, “Call upon your Lord to clarify”*** ***“for us what type ˹of cow˺ it should be!”*** al-Ṭabarī sets out the exchange and the answer that follows it, and Ibn ʿAbbās reads the question as an inquiry about the animal's age, which the first command had left open. The Arabic term for what the people asked about concerns the animal's state rather than its identity, as al-Ālūsī notes, since the kind of beast had already been named and only its description was still open.

The answer is given in the same measure as the question: ***“He replied, “Allah says,”*** ***“The cow should neither be old nor young”*** ***“but in between. So do”*** ***“as you are commanded!’”*** al-Jalālayn keeps the middle term of the description, with the animal neither worn out by years nor too young to bear the work, and as-Saʿdī records that the queries were multiplying while a single beast would have satisfied the command from the first day. Al-Qurṭubī records the judgement that the community made the matter hard on itself when any cow would have satisfied the command, and that the hardship was answered in kind.

**THE COST OF A SECOND QUESTION**

A question can be a road to understanding or a way of postponing an act, and the verse shows the difference by the instruction it ends with. The words at the close of the sentence are not a further clue; they tell the people to carry out what they were already told. Because the command had been given with room inside it, the second question turned freedom into a restriction, since the answer narrowed the field and left the community with less than it had before.

The Book states the same lesson about the community in the teaching of the Messenger, where the people of the wilderness are named as an example of what endless questioning produces. The point is not that inquiry is forbidden; the point is that a person who asks in order to delay ends up with a harder task than the one he was given. Because the answer narrowed the field, the mercy of a broad command can be spent by a narrow question. Since the instruction to act closes the sentence, the verse leaves no room for another round of clarification.

**WHY THE ANIMAL WAS NAMED AT ALL**

The description of the middle-aged cow is a kindness in disguise, since the answer made the matter clear and removed the excuse of uncertainty. Because the people had asked for a specification, they received one, and the clarity left them with nothing to say and everything to do. That is why the verse closes with the command to act rather than with a promise. Maʿārif al-Qurʾān draws the lesson that a broad duty would have stayed light, and that the community brought the narrow description on itself by treating a plain command as a puzzle.

A community that keeps asking for signs of God's will rarely lacks information. What it lacks is the decision to obey what is already plain to it, and the verse puts the decision at the end of the sentence where the reader cannot miss it. The reason the middle term was given at all is that a middle-aged animal stood within the reach of an ordinary household, so the command stayed possible while it became specific. Since the people had asked for a description, they received one that any attentive buyer could satisfy.

Think of a man who asks a pilot for the exact weight of the aircraft before he will fasten his belt. The belt is not fastened by the answer, and the flight leaves on time.

---

## Verse 2:69

> They said, “Call upon your Lord to specify for us its colour.” He replied, “Allah says, ‘It should be a bright yellow cow—pleasant to see.’”

**A THIRD QUESTION ABOUT A COLOUR**

The community returns with a narrower request: ***“They said, “Call upon your Lord to specify”*** ***“for us its colour.”*** Then the answer comes back with a description: ***“He replied, “Allah says,”*** ***“It should be a bright yellow cow”*** ***“pleasant to see.’”*** al-Tabarī reads the exchange as a request for more detail, and al-Jalālayn explains the colour as a deep yellow that draws the eye, the kind of tone that gives pleasure when it is seen.

Ibn ʿAbbās records the further details that the descriptions carried, with the animal's horns and hooves matching the body in colour, which is how the community arrived at a specification that no ordinary animal satisfied. Al-Qurṭubī sets out the grammar of the request, where the question word stands as the subject and the colour as the predicate. The colour is explained by al-Saʿdī as an intense yellow, and the beauty of the beast is noted as part of what made it pleasing to the eye. Because each question adds a feature, the reader watches the field of choice shrink from any cow to one particular cow, and the verse lets that shrink happen in front of him.

**THE PLEASURE IN THE DESCRIPTION**

The clause about the colour being pleasant to look at is not decoration, and the verse keeps it at the end of the sentence. A command had begun as a duty and now carries a description with beauty inside it, which shows a mercy that was still arranging the animal for the people's eyes. Because the answer came with something to enjoy, the reader is taught that obedience in this account was not meant to be grim. Al-Ālūsī notices that each clarification is traced back to God in the same words, and that the repetition is itself a mark of the help given to Moses in answering a people who kept pressing.

The people had asked for a colour to remove a doubt, and what they received was also a gift. The Book does not present the animal as a punishment; the animal was to be looked at and then sacrificed, and the colour was given so that the beast would be known on sight. Because the description carried a note of beauty, the terms of obedience in this account were not designed to humiliate. Since the question had been about appearance, the answer gave the people something to recognise rather than something to argue with.

**HOW A QUESTION CAN NARROW A LIFE**

Every further detail leaves the questioners less room to argue and less room to delay. That is why the community kept asking, since a question can feel like progress while it is actually a way of standing still. Because the answers were given each time, the fault of the people cannot be placed on a lack of guidance, and the account is a warning against the sort of inquiry that only postpones the deed.

A person may spend a lifetime searching for the exact shape of his duty and never reach the animal. The verse shows the search in its proper light: the more the community learned, the more exactly it was bound, and the clearer the command became the fewer the places to hide. The reason a question can become a way of standing still is that the asker keeps control of the pace while appearing to seek the truth. Since each answer was granted, the community carried the weight of the answers rather than the comfort of the search.

Think of a man told to buy a horse and who asks for the shade of its coat before he leaves the house. Each answer is true, and the market is closing.

---

## Verse 2:70

> Again they said, “Call upon your Lord so that He may make clear to us which cow, for all cows look the same to us. Then, Allah willing, we will be guided ˹to the right one˺.”

**A FOURTH QUESTION AND A NEW CLAIM**

The community speaks again: ***“Again they said, “Call upon your Lord”*** ***“so that He may make clear to us”*** ***“which cow, for all cows look the same to us.”*** al-Tabarī records the request in the same sequence as the earlier ones, and al-Jalālayn reads the question as an inquiry about whether the animal was working in the fields or left free, since the community had found that the descriptions seemed to match many beasts. Al-Qurṭubī counts the request as a fourth question and notes that the people still had not carried out the command after four clarifications. The words of the community are kept as they stand by al-Saʿdī, with the cows looking alike to them, and he notes that without the clause about God's will they would not have found the animal either.

The words at the end of the verse are the first sign of a change: ***“Then, Allah willing, we will be guided ˹to the right one˺.”*** Ibn ʿAbbās reads the clause as the community's own admission that the animal would only be found with the help of God, and he keeps the note of dependence in the middle of a sentence that had begun as another complaint. Al-Baghawī gives the sense of the question as a choice between a beast left to graze freely and one put to work in the fields.

**A COMMUNITY THAT STARTS TO YIELD**

The mention of God's will shows that the people had moved from argument to a kind of submission, even if the submission still came attached to a demand. Because the clause is conditional, the reader is shown a community prepared to obey once the matter is clear, and the Book lets that preparation stand as the turning point of the account. Because the clause about God's will arrives after four rounds of questions, the reader is shown what finally moved the people, and it was not information. Since the sentence still contains a demand, the submission is imperfect, and the record keeps it as it stands. Since the earlier questions had produced nothing but a longer description, the reader is meant to notice which word finally moved the people, and the word is not a further detail but a reference to God's will.

The same habit is named elsewhere in the sūrah, where a community is told that its answers lie in the commands it already holds. The Book records the exchange in order to show how a people can be standing at the gate of obedience and still conducting a negotiation.

**THE HOPE HIDDEN IN A CONDITION**

A sentence of the form "if God wills" can be a pious habit or a real surrender, and the verse leaves the reader to weigh the tone. The community had asked four times for clarity, and the answer each time had narrowed the field; the clause about God's will turns the search into something shared with the One who set the command in the first place.

Because the clause comes before the finding rather than after it, the people are shown as people who expect to be led. That expectation is the first fruit of the whole account, and it arrives in the verse before the animal is ever brought. The reason such a clause is worth noticing is that it places the outcome of the search outside the searcher, and a person who says it honestly has accepted the guidance before it comes. Since the community had been narrowing its own options with every question, the clause marks the moment when the narrowing stopped.

Think of a traveller who has asked a guide for landmarks four times and finally says that the path will be found with the guide's help. The road has not changed, and the traveller has.

---

## Verse 2:71

> He replied, “Allah says, ‘It should have been used neither to till the soil nor water the fields; wholesome and without blemish.’” They said, “Now you have come with the truth.” Yet they still slaughtered it hesitantly

**THE FINAL DETAIL AND THE WORK IT RULED OUT**

The answer arrives with the last condition: ***“He replied, “Allah says,”*** ***“It should have been used neither to till the soil”*** ***“nor water the fields”*** ***“wholesome and without blemish.’”*** al-Jalālayn keeps the clause about the animal never having been subdued for work, with the beast neither ploughing the earth nor carrying water to a field, and Ibn ʿAbbās records the same conditions along with the note that the animal was unyoked and free of any mark. Al-Qurṭubī records the description carried in the nominative, so that the clause stands as a further quality of the animal rather than as a denial of one kind of labour alone. The terms are explained one by one by al-Saʿdī, with the animal unbroken to labour, not ploughing the earth, not carrying water, sound in body and free of any mark beyond its colour, and he observes that the community's admission that the truth had come at last came after a delay that a single beast would have ended.

Because the description had now excluded every working animal, the community was left with a beast that had never served a purpose, which is exactly the kind of command that a people in love with utility resists. The verse keeps the conditions in a single sentence, so that the reader can see how much of the market had been removed by the terms. Because the animal had never worked, the command removed the usefulness a buyer would have prized, and the people were asked for a beast that had cost its owner nothing but its keep. Since the conditions described a creature kept only to be given, the reader is shown a sacrifice in the plain sense of the word. Al-Ālūsī explains the negation as a way of describing the beast, so that the clause means the animal was untouched by any labour.

**THE MOMENT OF AGREEMENT**

The answer of the people is recorded with relief: ***“They said, “Now you have come with the truth.”*** al-Ṭabarī reads the sentence as the community acknowledging the description at last, and Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the note of recognition in it. Because the people had asked for the truth four times, the moment when they recognise that they have it is worth pausing over, since nothing had changed except that they had stopped asking.

The verse does not end there. The final clause reports the state of their obedience: ***“Yet they still slaughtered it hesitantly”***. al-Jalālayn reads the closing word as the people performing the act without enthusiasm, and the account records a deed that was finally done with reluctance rather than with joy.

**WHAT THE HESITATION REVEALS**

An act can be carried out and still be marked by the state in which it was done, and the Book keeps the hesitation in the record. The community had reached the truth and had come to the animal, and something in the people was still dragging its feet. That is why the verse is placed in this sūrah, where the reader is shown how much daylight a person can put between the command and the heart.

The same pattern is used elsewhere with other people, since the reluctant deed is a common form of obedience in a community that has been arguing. The lesson of the account is not that the deed was refused; the deed was done. What the account preserves is the measure of the wrestling that went into it. Because the Book names the hesitation, God is shown to measure the manner as well as the act, and a deed performed under protest is recorded with its protest attached. Since the account keeps both the obedience and the reluctance, it teaches a reader to bring his will along with his hands.

Think of a man who signs a contract at the end of a long negotiation and sets down the pen as though it were a weight. The contract is signed, and the manner of signing is remembered by everyone in the room.

---

## Verse 2:72

> ˹This is˺ when a man was killed and you disputed who the killer was, but Allah revealed what you concealed

**THE CRIME BEHIND THE COMMAND**

The sūrah now reaches back to the reason the cow was needed: ***“˹This is˺ when a man was killed”*** ***“and you disputed who the killer was”*** ***“but Allah revealed what you concealed”***. al-Ṭabarī sets the killing before the command and keeps the dispute as the thing that brought the community to Moses, and al-Jalālayn describes the quarrel with the same order, with a body whose killer could not be named.

Ibn Kathīr keeps the crime and the disagreement together and records the sentence about God bringing out what the people were hiding. Because the community concealed what it knew, the reader is shown that the dispute was not only a search for the truth; it was also a cover for a group that had something to lose. The Arabic term for the dispute carries the sense of a charge thrown back and forth between families, and al-Qurṭubī reads the whole clause as placed before the opening of the story, so that the reader meets the crime that the command was sent to settle.

**WHY THE KILLING IS MENTIONED LATE**

The Book could have opened the story with the murder, and it opens instead with the command, which places the reader inside the community's confusion before it explains the cause. That arrangement is a mark of how the account is built: the reader meets the argument before he meets the crime, so that the command to sacrifice the cow is understood as an answer to a problem the community had brought on itself.

Because the murder is named after the exchange about the animal, the reader can see that the argument about the cow was a distraction from a heavier matter. The people were disputing a specification while the truth about a death stood in the middle of the room. Because the murder is named as the reason for everything that follows, the command is shown to be a cure for a hidden wound rather than a test invented for the day. Since the community came to Moses with the problem, the account also shows a people who wanted an answer without the exposure that came with it.

**THE TRUTH THAT WAS BURIED**

The closing clause about God revealing what had been concealed is the hinge of the whole account, since the animal's blood brings out the truth that the community had buried. Nobody in the story is required to confess; the evidence is provided by the same hand that issued the command, and the community is left with an answer it could not contest. Maʿārif al-Qurʾān records the detail that the killer had supporters who worked to hide the crime and turned the accusation on others, and that God appointed the dead body itself as the means of naming him.

That is why the verse is placed before the strike of the cow's limb in the next verse. The reader is told in advance what the miracle was for, and the wonder of the restored body is shown to serve the truth rather than to display power. The reason the truth had to be produced by a sign is that every witness in the community had a reason to shade it, and a statement from the dead man could not be argued with. Since the sign served a court rather than a crowd, the account reminds the reader that the wonders of God are aimed at the settlement of what people will not settle themselves.

Think of a village that argues for years over who moved a boundary stone while the stone itself sits under the weeds where it always was. The argument ends the day someone pulls the weeds.

---

## Verse 2:73

> So We instructed, “Strike the dead body with a piece of the cow.” This is how ˹easily˺ Allah brings the dead to life, showing you His signs so that you may understand

**THE INSTRUCTION THAT ANSWERED THE DISPUTE**

The command is given to the community with the animal now in hand: ***“So We instructed, “Strike the dead body”*** ***“with a piece of the cow.”*** al-Ṭabarī keeps the plural form of the instruction, since the people were addressed as a group, and al-Jalālayn records the strike and the result, with the dead man brought back and naming the one who had killed him before returning to his rest. A list of the parts named for the strike is collected by al-Qurṭubī, with the tongue, the tailbone, the thigh and a bone of the animal all reported, and he notes that a part of the beast was enough.

The verse then explains the purpose in the same breath: ***“This is how ˹easily˺ Allah brings the dead to life”*** ***“showing you His signs”*** ***“so that you may understand”***. Ibn ʿAbbās reads the restoration as the sign of what God will do with every dead body, and he keeps the closing clause attached to the community that had been watching instead of believing.

**THE MIRACLE MEASURED TO THE AUDIENCE**

The community had demanded proof about a murder, and what it received was a lesson about resurrection. That is the shape of the sign: the answer to the smaller question arrives carrying the larger one inside it, since the power that can return a man to tell his story is the power that will raise every body on the last day. Because the Book says the event was shown as a sign, the reader is told that the purpose of the wonder was instruction and not spectacle.

The same argument appears in another sūrah, where the revival of the earth is offered as the proof of the revival of the dead: (41:39 — **“the One Who revives it can easily revive the dead”**). The Book uses the pattern in more than one place, which shows that the reader is expected to move from what he sees to what he will meet. Because the sign was given to a community that had already argued its way through four questions, evidence is shown not to produce willing hearts on its own. Since the lesson was aimed at understanding, the account treats the mind as the field where the miracle was meant to work. Al-Ālūsī reads the clause about knowledge as a parenthesis placed between the killing and the strike, so that the reader is told plainly that concealment did not profit the community. The same strike is set by Maʿārif al-Qurʾān within the plan that God had appointed, with the dead man's body touched by a part of the animal so that the crime would be named by a means the community could not dispute. The reason this account stands in the middle of a sūrah about covenant is that the same people who could not accept a command could still be shown a body returning to life, so the sign was aimed at the quarrel that had stalled the community.

**WHY UNDERSTANDING IS THE LAST WORD**

The verse ends with the faculty rather than with the outcome, since the raising of the dead was meant to produce a settled conviction rather than a crowd. Because the command had been carried out with reluctance, the sign was given to people whose understanding was still lagging behind their obedience, and the closing word names the part of them that needed to catch up.

Understanding is therefore the destination of the whole story, and the animal and the strike are the road that led to it. The Book keeps the last word for the mind that finally takes the lesson in, which is why the account of the cow ends with a sentence about a faculty rather than about a corpse.

Think of a class that is shown an experiment instead of being told the rule. The experiment is repeated for the sake of the rule, and a student who watches the flame and misses the principle has been entertained rather than taught.

---

## Verse 2:74

> Even then your hearts became hardened like a rock or even harder, for some rocks gush rivers; others split, spilling water; while others are humbled in awe of Allah. And Allah is never unaware of what you do

**A CONDITION THAT SURVIVED THE SIGN**

The sūrah turns from the raising of the dead to the state of the hearts that watched it: ***“Even then your hearts became hardened like a rock”*** ***“or even harder, for some rocks gush rivers”*** ***“others split, spilling water”*** ***“while others are humbled in awe of Allah.”*** al-Ṭabarī reads the sentence as a description of hearts that remained stiff after the truth had been shown, and he keeps the comparison running through the kinds of stone that give water. The Arabic term for the hardness of the heart carries dryness as well as stiffness, and al-Qurṭubī explains it as the loss of the readiness to turn back and submit.

Ibn ʿAbbās reads the comparison with the same force, with the heart likened to stone and then placed below some stones, since a rock can be of use to the people around it. Because the comparison moves from one likeness to a sharper one, the reader is led from a general statement to the particular fault, and the fault is a heart that has stopped giving.

**THE STONES THAT STILL GIVE**

Three kinds of stone are named in a single sentence, and each one is credited with a kind of usefulness, since rivers come out of one, a split opens in another, and a third stands in awe. The Book compares the heart to the lowest of them and finds the heart wanting. as-Saʿdī reads the passage as a rebuke to hearts that had received the greatest signs and produced the least, since the stones of the earth had given water while the people had given argument. Al-Baghawī gives the same sense in plainer words, describing a heart dried out and stiffened with mercy leaving it, and he records the report that the community went on denying the killing even after the sign.

The Book states the same verdict on a community elsewhere, where the breach of a covenant is followed by the hardening of the heart, (5:13 — **“and hardened their hearts”**), and the warning is repeated to the believers in a sentence that asks them how long they intend to wait, (57:16 — **“Has the time not yet come for believers’ hearts to be humbled at the remembrance of Allah”**). A third passage describes the danger that follows a hardened heart, (3:7 — **“those with deviant hearts follow the elusive verses”**).

**WHAT HARDNESS LOOKS LIKE FROM INSIDE**

A heart does not announce its own hardening, and the verse gives the reader no inner sensation to go by, only a comparison and a record. The community in the story could have described itself as careful, thoughtful and slow to accept what it had not verified, and the Book describes the same state as something worse than rock. Because the signs had been given, there was nothing left to verify, and the demand for more evidence had become a way of standing still. Because the community had watched water come out of stone, the comparison left it without an excuse, since material softer than the heart had answered a purpose the heart refused. That is why the passage moves from the sign to the state of the hearts rather than to a further command, because the reader is meant to measure what a wonder did inside the people who watched it. The reason the sentence about the stones follows the comparison is that three useful objects are placed beside a useless heart. The rebuke is aimed at what the heart failed to give rather than at what it lacked in dignity.

The closing sentence removes the last comfort: ***“And Allah is never unaware of what you do”***. The line is a plain statement, and the reader is told that the condition of the heart is not a private matter. What a person does with the signs he has received is recorded, and the record is kept by the One who sent them. The same sentence closes two later passages of the sūrah (2:140) and (2:149), where the knowledge of God is stated as the reason no concealment can hold.

Think of a garden that is watered by a spring running out of a stone wall while the household inside argues about the plumbing. The water is there, the argument is long, and the beds are dry.

---

## Verse 2:75

> Do you ˹believers still˺ expect them to be true to you, though a group of them would hear the word of Allah then knowingly corrupt it after understanding it

**A HOPE THAT NEEDED CORRECTING**

The sūrah addresses the believers directly: ***“Do you ˹believers still˺ expect them to be true to you”***. al-Ṭabarī reads the question as aimed at the companions of the Messenger, who had been working hard to bring the People of the Book into the faith and had hoped for an answer. The Book steps in and closes the expectation, not because the effort was wrong but because the ground had been described in full. Al-Qurṭubī reads the question as a rebuke that closes the believers' hopes for that group, and he notes that the refusal was not the first of its kind among them.

The reason for the correction follows in the same sentence: ***“though a group of them would hear the word of Allah”*** ***“then knowingly corrupt it after understanding it”***. al-Jalālayn identifies the group as the learned men of the community who had heard the text and altered it, and Ibn ʿAbbās names the men who had stood with Moses at the mountain and still handled the words this way.

**WHAT THE VERSE ACCUSES THEM OF**

The charge is not ignorance, and the sentence says so twice over, since the corruption is described as deliberate and the understanding is named as the state in which it was done. as-Saʿdī keeps the order of the two clauses, with the hearing first and the twisting after it, so that the reader sees a change made by men who knew what they were changing. Because the book had been placed in their hands, the fault is heavier than the fault of a people who had never received it.

The Book describes the same practice in another sūrah, where a group of them is said to take words out of their context, (4:46 — **“Some Jews take words out of context”**), and the charge is stated again where the breach of the covenant is followed by the alteration of the Scripture, (5:13 — **“They distorted the words of the Scripture”**).

**WHAT THE VERSE ASKS OF THE BELIEVERS**

The sentence is a question, and the reader is expected to answer it with a change of expectation rather than with bitterness. A person who has understood this verse will still speak the truth to his neighbour, and he will stop measuring his own success by the number of answers he receives. Because the account has shown how the words were handled, the reader is protected from a hope that would have produced disappointment. Since the correction is addressed to the believers rather than to the offending group, it is meant as a kindness to the people who were still working, and the fault is described as deliberate so that no one wastes effort explaining it away. The reason the verse stands after the account of the hardened hearts is that the two failures belong to the same community, and a reader who sees both can judge an expectation by the record rather than by a hope.

The verse also protects the community from the opposite error, which is contempt for the Book that the other community carried. What is rejected here is the twisting, and the same sentence that names the fault keeps the Scripture honoured as the word of God. Because the fault was named before the question was answered, the verse teaches the believers to judge a people by its record rather than by its welcome, and the reason the correction is placed here is that a hope left unexamined would have cost the community years of effort. The reason the account has shown the handling of the words is that the believers were about to spend their strength on a hope that the record had already answered.

Think of a schoolmaster who has watched a pupil copy the answers and change them, and who tells the young teacher not to be surprised. The teaching goes on, and the surprise is what the older man is trying to spare him.

---

## Verse 2:76

> When they meet the believers they say, “We believe.” But in private they say ˹to each other˺, “Will you disclose to the believers the knowledge Allah has revealed to you, so that they may use it against you before your Lord? Do you not understand?”

**TWO CONVERSATIONS IN ONE COMMUNITY**

The next verse shows the same people in two settings: ***“When they meet the believers they say, “We believe.”*** ***“But in private they say ˹to each other˺,”*** ***“Will you disclose to the believers”*** ***“the knowledge Allah has revealed to you”*** ***“so that they may use it against you before your Lord?”*** ***“Do you not understand?”*** al-Jalālayn identifies the speakers as the hypocrites among the people of the Book, and Maʿārif al-Qurʾān describes the situation in which some of them greeted the Muslims with a claim of faith while keeping their own counsel among themselves.

The Book states the same double speech about the hypocrites of the town in another verse, where the public word and the private word are placed side by side, (2:14 — **“When they meet the believers they say, “We believe.”**).

**THE WORRY ABOUT THEIR OWN EVIDENCE**

The private conversation is about disclosure, and the fear expressed is that the Muslims will use the knowledge against them before God. That fear is the confession of the whole verse, since the people who hold a text and fear its use are admitting that the text supports the other side. Because the knowledge came from God, the community that hides it is not protecting itself from an accusation; it is protecting itself from a verdict.

Ibn Kathīr reads the passage as the closing of the believers' hopes and as a description of a group that weighed what it knew against its standing and chose its standing. The verse is honest about the calculation, and it puts the calculation in the mouths of the people instead of leaving the reader to guess. Al-Qurṭubī identifies the public claim as the speech of the hypocrites and the private counsel as the case of the Jews among them, since a group of them had entered the faith and then turned back on it.

**THE QUESTION AT THE END**

The sentence closes with a question that is asked inside the private meeting: ***“Do you not understand?”*** Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the question in the mouth of the speakers as they argue with one another, which shows that the community itself could see the absurdity of the plan. The scene is read by al-Ālūsī as a fresh addition to the record, in which a part of the community reproaches the rest for what they were about to disclose. A group that hides the truth to protect its position has already told itself what it is doing, and the Book records the argument so that the reader may hear it.

The reason the sūrah keeps this scene is that the reader will meet the same temptation in his own circle. The knowledge of God is given to be handed on, and every reason offered for keeping it back is a reason about standing rather than about truth. Because the verse ends inside the private meeting, the reader is left with the sound of a community arguing with itself about the truth it holds. Since the public claim and the private counsel are both recorded, a double life is shown being conducted in one breath. The reason the private sentence is reported without a comment of its own is that the sentence condemns itself, and a reader who overhears the exchange learns more than a lecture could teach him.

Think of a witness who knows the truth of a case and asks his friend not to mention it, because the truth would settle the matter the wrong way for him. The friend's answer is the last question of the verse.

---

## Verse 2:77

> Do they not know that Allah is aware of what they conceal and what they reveal

**A QUESTION PUT TO A PEOPLE WITH TWO ACCOUNTS**

The account of the community's double speech is interrupted by a question. The first words are ***“Do they not know”***, and the sentence goes on to state ***“that Allah is aware of what they conceal”*** ***“and what they reveal”***. al-Ṭabarī reads the question as aimed at the Jews, and he explains the two clauses as the counsel they kept among themselves and the claims they made in the open. Ibn ʿAbbās names the people addressed as the leaders of the community, and al-Baghawī gives the two verbs their plain sense, with the first covering what was hidden and the second what was shown.

Part of the rebuke sits in the grammar. al-Jalālayn notes that the interrogative here affirms rather than asks, and that the joining particle adds the second clause to the first. The knowledge of God is therefore stated over the secret word and the public word at once. Al-Ālūsī reads the sentence as a declaration of the ignorance that the previous verses had exposed, and he observes that the address to the believers is placed in the middle of the passage for a purpose. The reader is being taught the ground on which their hopes for this community were closed.

**WHY THE REBUKE IS PUT AS A QUESTION**

A question can be a request for news or a way of showing a person that he already holds the answer. This one belongs to the second kind, because the people addressed had been arguing about a text while assuming that their private meetings were private. The point of the sentence is that nothing in their counsel was out of view, so the calculation described in the verse before it was made in front of a witness.

Maʿārif al-Qurʾān draws the same conclusion, since the reminder makes no difference to what the community hides and would make none to what it shows, and the One they would be hiding from knows both before they act. The Book states the same knowledge elsewhere in a sentence addressed to every reader, (2:284 — **“Whether you reveal what is in your hearts or conceal it”**). Because the verse stands directly after the private conversation, the reader is shown that the talk among the people was watched by the One whose knowledge they were treating lightly. Since the concealment was aimed at the believers, the sentence also teaches that a community can be deceived while the record cannot.

**THE TWO-REGISTER LIFE AND ITS END**

The people in the passage had learned to fit their speech to their audience, and the verse removes the third audience they had forgotten. That is why the account does not end with the disclosure of the plan; it ends with the announcement that the plan was never hidden. Because the knowledge of God is stated as a fact rather than as a warning, the reader is not left to wonder whether the secret was noticed. The sentence closes the space in which such a plan could feel safe.

A person who keeps two accounts of himself is usually managing one of them for an audience he can see, and the question removes that audience from the reckoning. The verse asks rather than threatens, which leaves the hearer to answer for himself, and the answer is the same for the leader and for the follower. The closing words return to the two halves of the earlier description, so that nothing said in secret and nothing said in public is left outside the sentence. Since the Book has already reported both kinds of speech, the verse makes the report complete by naming the One who heard each of them.

Think of a man who locks his front door and leaves the window above it open all night. The room was never out of sight, and the lock was never the protection he thought it was.

---

## Verse 2:78

> And among them are the illiterate who know nothing about the Scripture except lies, and ˹so˺ they ˹wishfully˺ speculate

**WHO THE ILLITERATE ARE**

The verse turns from the leaders to the followers: ***“And among them are the illiterate”***. al-Ṭabarī reads the group as part of the Jewish community, the people who had no share in the Book itself. The same identification is kept by al-Baghawī in plainer words. Al-Ālūsī reads the sentence as a fresh description of a further fault, set beside the faults of the leaders described before it, so that the passage shows the community from the top and from the bottom in one frame.

The description is then narrowed: ***“who know nothing about the Scripture except lies”***. al-Jalālayn explains the clause as people who did not know the Torah but only desires handed down to them by their ancestors. The same sense is given by Ibn ʿAbbās, with the community knowing nothing of the Book beyond the unfounded things it had heard. In Arabic the word for the unlettered person covers anyone who does not grasp what he reads, whether the difficulty lies in the wording or in the meaning, as Ibn ʿUthaymīn notes. He adds that the noun for those expectations can also carry the sense of recitation without understanding, which is the reading he prefers in this verse.

**THE HABIT OF GUESSING**

The sentence closes with its own verdict: ***“and ˹so˺ they ˹wishfully˺ speculate”***. The words describe a person who has no ground under his feet and keeps a hope in the place where knowledge should be. Because the group had inherited claims rather than a text it could read, the account shows how a tradition can be handed on with the confidence of a proof. The fault is laid at the door of people who never checked what they were told.

Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the note of conjecture in the closing clause, with the community guessing rather than knowing. Ibn ʿUthaymīn keeps the same judgement and explains the expectations as desires dressed up as knowledge, which is why the verse does not accuse the group of a mistake in reading but of a substitute for reading. The Book states the same verdict on a related claim elsewhere in the sūrah, where a hope is named for what it is, (2:111 — **“These are their desires”**). Since the earlier verses had shown the leaders suppressing what they understood, the reader now sees the other end of the same community, where ignorance and confidence lived together without difficulty.

**WHY THE ILLITERATE ARE MENTIONED AT ALL**

A passage that has just exposed the scholars might have been expected to spare the ordinary people, and the verse does not spare them. The reason is that the ordinary people are shown as the market in which the leaders' work was sold, since a claim needs hearers, and the hearers in this passage had no way to test what they were given. That is why the account keeps both groups in one picture and states the fault of each in its own terms.

The lesson reaches beyond the community described. Because the temptation of the unlettered is to trust a title rather than a text, the verse warns every reader whose knowledge of his religion arrived second-hand, and it does not treat that reader as a victim alone. The way out is not learning by itself; it is the willingness to ask what a claim rests on, and the verse sets the question beside the claim.

Think of a village that trusts the signboard at a bridge because a well-dressed man put it there. The bridge is sound, the board is wrong, and nobody in the village has crossed the bridge to find out.

---

## Verse 2:79

> So woe to those who distort the Scripture with their own hands then say, “This is from Allah”—seeking a fleeting gain! So woe to them for what their hands have written, and woe to them for what they have earned

**THE WRITING HANDS AND THE WOE PRONOUNCED**

The next sentence names the deed that the previous verses had described in general terms: ***“So woe to those”*** ***“who distort the Scripture with their own hands”***. al-Ṭabarī sets out the reports on what the word of woe names, with a valley in the Fire among the sayings. He keeps the sentence as a threat aimed at the people who did the writing. Ibn ʿAbbās gives the deed its shape, with the description of the messenger in the Book altered by hands that knew what they were altering, and al-Jalālayn keeps the same charge, with the Scripture written out by the community itself.

The Arabic term for woe is a noun of threat that has no verb formed from it, as al-Ālūsī notes. Attention is drawn by Ibn ʿUthaymīn to the way the sentence begins with it, so that the threat stands at the head of the line and the offenders are introduced inside it. The construction leaves no room to read the sentence as a description, because a verdict has been pronounced in advance and the offenders are introduced inside it.

**THE PRICE AND THE CLAIM**

The words that went with the writing are supplied next: ***“then say, “This is from Allah””*** ***“seeking a fleeting gain!”*** al-Saʿdī reads the line as the exposure of a trade rather than a mistake, since the community put forward falsehood and buried what it knew, and it did both knowingly. He notes the smallness of the price, with the whole of this world from its first day to its last counted as a small sum. He observes as well that the sellers had made their invention a net for what people held in their hands.

The claim is quoted in the verse because the claim is the crime. Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the words as they were spoken, and al-Saʿdī explains the double wrong in the transaction, since the buyers had their religion confused for them and their property taken on a false ground. Because the sentence puts the price beside the claim, the reader is shown that the trade was not a lapse of judgement but a calculation about standing and income. The calculation is offered without a defence attached.

**THE DOUBLE WOE AND WHAT IT REPEATS**

The sentence of woe is not said once. It is repeated over what the hands wrote and over what the hands earned: ***“So woe to them”*** ***“for what their hands have written”*** ***“and woe to them”*** ***“for what they have earned”***. The repetition is not decoration, since the two clauses name two different objects of judgement, the product and the profit. That is why the sentence keeps the hands in view from its first word to its last: they wrote, and they earned.

Ibn ʿUthaymīn notes the motive the verse itself supplies, and he keeps the point that a person who sells his knowledge for a price pays with the thing he knew. The Book states the same trade elsewhere with a clause that fits the scene (2:174 — **“trading them for a fleeting gain”**). The same handling of a covenant is recorded in another sūrah (3:187 — **“to make it known to people and not hide it”**). Since the account has already shown the community concealing what it understood, the reader is now shown the finished product of that habit, and the product is a page for sale.

Think of a shopkeeper who sells a forged deed and keeps the real one in the drawer. The buyers hold a document, the shop holds the money, and the drawer decides the case on the day the two deeds are compared.

---

## Verse 2:80

> ˹Some of˺ the Jews claim, “The Fire will not touch us except for a number of days.” Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “Have you taken a pledge from Allah—for Allah never breaks His word—or are you ˹just˺ saying about Allah what you do not know?”

**A CLAIM ABOUT THE FIRE**

The account now reports a claim the community made about its own future: ***“˹Some of˺ the Jews claim, “The Fire will not touch us except”***. The claim continues ***“for a number of days.” Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “Have you taken a pledge from Allah”***, and the answer runs on from the claim itself. Ibn ʿAbbās fills in the figure they had in mind, with forty days counted from the period in which their ancestors had taken the calf. al-Jalālayn keeps the same number and the same reasoning, and al-Baghawī gives the claim its shape as a fixed term that would end and leave them free of the punishment. al-Ṭabarī records the whole exchange in the community's own words.

The verse does not let the claim stand. al-Saʿdī reads the answer as a reduction of the claim to two possibilities and no third, since either the community held a promise from God or it was speaking about God without knowledge. A promise from God cannot be broken, as the sentence itself states, ***“for Allah never breaks His word”***, and a claim made without knowledge is a word spoken in the dark. The last clause closes the answer: ***“or are you ˹just˺ saying about Allah what you do not know?”***.

**THE SHAPE OF THE ARGUMENT**

The argument turns on the difference between a promise and a hope. Since the community had produced no pledge, the sentence leaves it standing on the second half of the choice. The reader is shown a claim about the unseen being made by people with no warrant from the One who owns the unseen. Ibn ʿUthaymīn keeps the same judgement and calls the claim a false wish, since the wish was doing the work of evidence.

Because a term of punishment is not set by the people who fear it, the reply refutes the claim in every reading, and Maʿārif al-Qurʾān notes that the commentators read the claim itself in several ways. Al-Ālūsī keeps the sentence attached to what came before, so that the claim is shown as the confidence of the same community whose counsel had just been exposed. The Book reports the same claim about the Fire in another sūrah, (3:24 — **“The Fire will not touch us except”**), and the verse that follows this one answers both claims together.

**WHAT THE CLAIM REVEALS**

A person who expects a limited punishment has already decided that the standard will be adjusted for him, and the sentence leaves the standard where it was. Because the community had a lineage and a book, it treated both as security, and the one thing that could have made them security is named in the sentence, which is a promise from God that they did not have. That is why the answer comes as a question rather than as a denial, and the question is left standing for the reader as well.

The same habit appears wherever a person assumes that his punishment will be smaller than his deed. The claim in the verse is an extreme form of a common confidence, and the closing clause names the ground on which the confidence was built, which is speech about God without knowledge.
