Since the sentence has already removed the pledge from the argument, the reader is left with the second half of the choice, and the second half is a description of what the people were doing.

Think of a traveller who is told that the fine for the toll road is capped, and who drives through the barrier without paying. The cap he is relying on was never issued, and the barrier does not know his name.

---

## Verse 2:81

> But no! Those who commit evil and are engrossed in sin will be the residents of the Fire. They will be there forever

**THE ANSWER THAT CANCELS THE CLAIM**

The claim about a limited punishment is answered with words that overturn it: ***“But no! Those who commit evil”***. al-Ṭabarī reads the answer as the refutation of the community's claim, and Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the same sense. The correction is placed directly against the speech that had gone before, so that the claim is cancelled rather than answered. The phrase does not argue with what was said; it removes it, the way a correction is made to a sentence that had already been accepted as true.

The verdict then follows in a general sentence: ***“and are engrossed in sin will be the residents of the Fire. They will be there forever”***. al-Saʿdī reads the description as a condition that sweeps past the community in the verse to everyone who meets it. He explains the phrase about being engrossed as a sin that surrounds its owner and leaves him no way out. In his reading that state is the state of unbelief, since a person who holds faith is not enclosed by his sin in that way. He notes that a group later used the verse against Muslims, and that the verse is an argument against them rather than for them.

**RESIDENTS, NOT VISITORS**

The description chosen for the people of the Fire is that of settled inhabitants, and the sentence adds that they remain there. That is exactly what the earlier claim had denied, since the community in the passage had assumed that its stay would be short and its end known. The Book states the same settlement elsewhere in a sentence about the same Fire, (2:39 — **“will be the residents of the Fire”**), and the two passages are read together as one verdict.

Ibn ʿUthaymīn keeps the sentence as the justice of God set against the claim of the community. He notes that the rule applies without regard to lineage, since the standard is the deed and the state of the heart rather than the people from whom a person descends. al-Ālūsī reads the answer as a refutation in a form that covers the people addressed and everyone else who meets the description, which is why the sentence is phrased as a general rule rather than as a reply to one community. Because the rule is general, the reader cannot file it away as a report about someone else.

**WHY THE VERSE SPEAKS IN GENERAL TERMS**

A rebuke aimed at one community would have let every other hearer off, and the sentence is not built that way. The reason the address stays general is that the standard it describes is not local, since a person is settled in the Fire or he is not, and the difference is not the name of his community. Since the Book had just quoted a claim built on descent, the answer removes descent from the argument in a single stroke.

The verse also keeps the account honest. The community in the passage is not told that its claim was too optimistic; it is told what the claim revealed, which is a heart that had decided how much of its own deed would be counted. That is why the answer arrives as a rule rather than as an insult, and it is why the rule can be read by anyone who hears it, in any generation, without needing a share in the story's quarrel.

Think of a tenant who tells the landlord that the notice period is ten days because that is what he remembers from his father's lease. The lease is produced, the period is different, and the memory is not a clause.

---

## Verse 2:82

> And those who believe and do good will be the residents of Paradise. They will be there forever

**THE OTHER SIDE OF THE SENTENCE**

The Book does not leave the verdict at the fate of the people of the Fire: ***“And those who believe”*** ***“and do good will be the residents of Paradise. They will be there forever”***. al-Ṭabarī reads the two clauses as the second half of the answer to the community's claim, and Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the same order, with faith named before the good works that follow it. Ibn Kathīr keeps the pair together as well, with the sentence standing as the counterweight to the verse before it.

The placement is noticed by al-Ālūsī, since the people of faith are mentioned after the people of the Fire, so that the reader is given both outcomes in one frame rather than a warning alone. Ibn ʿUthaymīn explains the closing phrase as companionship with the Garden, since the word for its people implies remaining with what they have been given. He keeps the note that the Garden is what God has prepared for the mindful.

**FAITH AND WORK IN ONE CLAUSE**

The sentence binds the two halves of the description together, and it does not treat either as optional. al-Jalālayn keeps the ordering, with belief stated first and the deeds joined to it. al-Saʿdī reads the pair as the description of a life that has turned in a direction rather than as a sum of transactions. The works in the verse are not a price paid for the Garden; they are the shape that faith takes when it is real. Because the description joins belief to work in one breath, the reader is shown that a claim of faith without a change of conduct is not the state the verse is describing. The reason the deeds are named after the belief is that the root is put first and the fruit is named where it grows.

Because the two halves of the description stand in one sentence, the reader is shown that faith and work are not two accounts to be settled apart. That is why the account does not present the two outcomes as a scheme of rewards and penalties alone. The verse contrasts two kinds of residence, and it leaves the quality of the deeds with the One who sees them. Because the description of the believer is stated in general terms, the reader is invited to ask whether the description fits him rather than whether the ledger balances, and the question is left open on purpose.

**THE MEANING OF THE TWO RESIDENCES**

The two verses together form one sentence of judgement, and the Garden and the Fire are each given to the people who belong in them. The Book states the same settled life elsewhere with the same vocabulary, (18:107 — **“the Gardens of Paradise”**), and it makes the contrast between the two harvests explicit in another sūrah, (42:20 — **“the harvest of the Hereafter”**). Since the community in the story had been trading the one for the other, the reader is shown what the trade was worth.

The comfort in the verse is bound up with its severity. Because the sentence about the believers is as absolute as the sentence about the others, a person who holds faith and works is given a certainty that no claim of lineage could provide. The account closes the argument about the Fire with a promise about the Garden that stands beside it.

The case is like two brothers who are offered the same land by the same owner, one taking the deed and the other keeping the invitation letter in his pocket. Both of them had paper, and only one of them had the land.

---

## Verse 2:83

> And ˹remember˺ when We took a covenant from the children of Israel ˹stating˺, “Worship none but Allah; be kind to parents, relatives, orphans and the needy; speak kindly to people; establish prayer; and pay alms-tax.” But you ˹Israelites˺ turned away—except for a few of you—and were indifferent

**THE COVENANT AND ITS FIRST ARTICLE**

The sūrah now returns to the covenant: ***“And ˹remember˺ when We took a covenant from the children of Israel ˹stating˺, “Worship none”*** ***“but Allah; be kind to parents, relatives, orphans”*** — the opening articles of a pledge taken from the community. al-Ṭabarī reads the sentence as the record of that pledge, and al-Ālūsī places it as a further item in the account of the ancestors' conduct. The case against the community is drawn from the record rather than from an accusation. Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the first article in its own words, with the worship of God alone declared as the foundation on which everything else in the pledge rests.

Maʿārif al-Qurʾān notes that the pledge is described as taken from the children of Israel as a people, and that the few named in the closing clause as true to it were those who acted on it rather than those who carried its name. al-Saʿdī reads the articles as fundamentals of religion that God commanded in every law, because such principles carry the public interest of every time and place. He keeps the first article as the pillar on which the rest depend. The reason the pledge begins with worship is that every other article in the list takes its authority from the One who is worshipped. The duties which follow are branches of a single root.

**KINSHIP, THE POOR, AND THE TONGUE**

The covenant then turns from worship to the people who hold a claim on the community: ***“and the needy”***. The list is kept in the same order by Ibn Kathīr, with parents first and the poor brought in after the family, and Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the same sequence. The Book states the same command in another sūrah in a sentence addressed to all people (4:36 — **“associate none with Him”**). The duty to parents is recorded in a passage that names the worship of God first (17:23 — **“you worship none but Him”**).

Then comes the article that orders the community's speech: ***“speak kindly to people”***. The words chosen are not a command to tell the truth alone but a command about the manner of address, and the verse places that duty beside the duty to parents rather than in a separate class of worship. Because the sentence binds the tongue with the same authority that binds the hand, the reader is shown that a covenant with God is conducted in public as much as in private.

**PRAYER, ALMS, AND THE TURNING AWAY**

The covenant closes with the two pillars that hold a community together: ***“establish prayer; and pay alms-tax.”*** al-Ṭabarī keeps the duties inside the same sentence as the ones before them. The pair is read by al-Saʿdī as the two institutions that make the rest of the list possible. Prayer is the community's address to God, and the alms-tax is the community's address to its poor, and the pledge is not complete with one of them alone. Because the two duties are named together, the reader is shown that worship and welfare were held by one agreement, so that a community cannot be devout in one direction and careless in the other. The reason the covenant keeps circling the same list is that the failure it describes was not a rejection of the text but a refusal to be bound by it.

The verse then reports what happened to the pledge: ***“But you ˹Israelites˺ turned away”*** ***“except for a few of you”*** ***“and were indifferent”***. Ibn ʿAbbās reads the turning away as a refusal made after the pledge had been accepted. The small group of the faithful is kept in the sentence by al-Jalālayn. al-Qurṭubī collects the articles of this covenant and the reports about how each one was treated. Because the account has kept every article in view, the reader is shown a community that agreed to a list and then walked away from most of it.

The closing word describes a person who does not fight the command; he puts it aside. That is why the sentence stands after the whole covenant rather than after its first article, since the fault it names is not an argument with one duty but a settled indifference to the whole. Since the few are named as the exception, the account is careful not to write off a people for the conduct of most of them, and the verse leaves the reader with a pledge and a choice.

Think of a family that signs a written agreement about the care of an elderly parent and then moves to another town. The paper stays in the drawer, the parent stays where she is, and nobody in the family has denied the agreement out loud.

---

## Verse 2:84

> And ˹remember˺ when We took your covenant that you would neither shed each other’s blood nor expel each other from their homes, you gave your pledge and bore witness

**THE PLEDGE ABOUT BLOOD AND HOMES**

The next verse records another article of the same covenant: ***“And ˹remember˺ when We took your covenant”*** ***“that you would neither shed each other’s blood”*** ***“nor expel each other from their homes, you gave your pledge”***. al-Ṭabarī reads the two prohibitions as a covenant of the community against itself. The first clause is explained by al-Baghawī as the command that no one among them should spill the blood of another, with a reported reading that extends it to the blood of anyone at all. Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the pair together and reads the second prohibition as the neighbour's right to remain where he lives. The intent is stated plainly by al-Ālūsī, which is that no one among them should touch another by killing or by driving him out.

The two clauses are worth noticing together because they name the two ways a community destroys itself from inside. Killing is the act of a hand, and expulsion is the act of an order, and both are put in one sentence and forbidden by one pledge. In the grammar of the pledge the prohibition is carried in the form of a statement, so that the community is described as a people bound to a course rather than merely warned against one.

**THE PLEDGE THAT WAS ACCEPTED**

The verse then reports the community's answer: ***“you gave your pledge”*** ***“and bore witness”***. Ibn Kathīr keeps the acknowledgement in the sentence, with the covenant ratified by the people and witnessed against them, and al-Jalālayn keeps the same order. The wording is deliberately double, since the community agreed and then called itself to witness in the agreement. Because the sentence records the giving and the witnessing together, the reader is shown that the fault which follows is not a misunderstanding of the terms.

The conduct described in these verses is placed by al-Saʿdī among the people living at the time the revelation came down, with the two tribes of Madinah and their long quarrel. He keeps the covenant in view as the standard against which the conduct is judged. Maʿārif al-Qurʾān reads the verse as the completion of the pledge described just before it, with the remaining articles stated in the community's own hearing. Since the acceptance is reported in the same breath as the pledge, the verse leaves no room for a defence built on ignorance of the terms.

**WHY THE OLD PLEDGE IS REPEATED**

A community that has already broken a pledge is not usually served by hearing it again, and the Book repeats it for a reason. The reason is that the reader of the passage is being shown the ground of the judgement that follows, and the ground was not a new demand but an old agreement. That is why the sentence about the acceptance is kept, and it is why the two crimes are named before the account of their commission.

The pledge also has a wider use. The Book states the value of a single life in another sūrah in a sentence addressed to this same people (5:32 — **“whoever takes a life”**). The corruption that follows a broken agreement is named as a mark of those who lost their way (2:27 — **“and spread corruption in the land”**). Because the agreement and the witness have been kept side by side, the reader meets the next verse already knowing the terms on which the community is judged, and that verse supplies the conduct.

Think of a neighbourhood that draws up a rule about not building over the shared well, and every household signs it at a public meeting. The signatures are on the page years later, and the wall is built over the well.

---

## Verse 2:85

> But here you are, killing each other and expelling some of your people from their homes, aiding one another in sin and aggression; and when those ˹expelled˺ come to you as captives, you still ransom them—though expelling them was unlawful for you. Do you believe in some of the Scripture and reject the rest? Is there any reward for those who do so among you other than disgrace in this worldly life and being subjected to the harshest punishment on the Day of Judgment? For Allah is never unaware of what you do

**THE CHARGE OF CONTRADICTION**

The report turns from the pledge to the practice: ***“But here you are, killing each other”*** ***“and expelling some of your people from their homes, aiding one another in sin”*** ***“and aggression; and when those ˹expelled˺ come to you”***. al-Ṭabarī reads the sentence as the charge laid against the community for the very deeds the covenant had forbidden. The setting is filled in by al-Ālūsī, with the tribes of Madinah and their alliances pulling the community into the quarrel that the pledge had closed. Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the charge in the same sequence, with the killing, the expulsion and the help given to one side against the other.

The contradiction is stated next: ***“as captives, you still ransom them”*** ***“though expelling them was unlawful”*** ***“for you. Do you believe in some of the Scripture”***. The sentence is kept by Ibn ʿUthaymīn as the exposure of a community that honoured one part of the Book and dropped the part that stood in its way. The transaction is explained by al-Saʿdī through the two sides of the record, since the ransoming was a duty the community kept while the killing and the expulsion were duties it ignored. al-Qurṭubī notes the grammar of the opening address, where the pronoun stands as the subject of the sentence, and he keeps the charge pointed at the people who were hearing it. The two customs are set side by side in the sentence for a reason, since the community was ready to pay for a captive and not ready to spare a neighbour. That is why the charge is not that the law was kept badly but that the parts of it which suited the community were the parts it chose to keep.

**WHAT THE BOOK SAID AND WHAT THE PEOPLE CHOSE**

The verse puts a question in the middle of the passage: ***“Do you believe in some of the Scripture”*** ***“and reject the rest?”***. The question is not about the contents of the Book but about the practice of a community that treated it as a set of options. Because the same book carried the duty to ransom the captive and the prohibition on spilling blood, the two customs could not both be honoured, and the community honoured the one that cost it least.

Maʿārif al-Qurʾān reads the verse as the record of a pledge broken by the people who made it, and it lists the injunctions the community had accepted and then abandoned. Ibn ʿUthaymīn keeps the contradiction at the centre of the reading, since the same hand that paid the ransom had driven the captive's household out, and he notes that a religion treated this way is not being obeyed but used. Since the deeds have already been named, the question lands on conduct rather than on belief. It is asked in the second person, which leaves no room for the answer to be given about someone else.

**DISGRACE AND THE LONG PUNISHMENT**

The verse then states the price: ***“Is there any reward”*** ***“for those who do”*** ***“so among you other than disgrace in this worldly life”*** ***“and being subjected to the harshest punishment on the Day of Judgment?”***. al-Ṭabarī reads the sentence as the verdict of the passage. The two stages are kept in the order given by al-Ālūsī, with the humiliation met in this life and the heavier account kept for the day of judgement. Because the community had chosen the standing it was protecting, the first instalment of its punishment is the loss of that standing.

The verse closes with the sentence that also closes other passages of the sūrah: ***“For Allah is never unaware of what you do”***. Since a community that honours part of a book in public and abandons the rest in the field relies on the parts of the record that are not watched, the line answers the assumption on which the whole contradiction rested. The Book states the same knowledge at the close of a later address in this sūrah (2:149), so that the reader understands the sentence was not written for one community alone. Since the practice described in the verse is the practice of treating a text as a list of options, the closing line is the right one. It removes the assumption that the two halves of a book can be separated without the One who revealed it noticing.

Think of a committee that votes to honour the rules about punctual payment and ignores the rules about safety on site. Two meetings, one rulebook, and the minutes read out at the end show both decisions in the same handwriting.

---

## Verse 2:86

> These are the ones who trade the Hereafter for the life of this world. So their punishment will not be reduced, nor will they be helped

**THE TRADE AND THE PRICE**

The passage closes with the verdict on the people just described: ***“These are the ones”*** ***“who trade the Hereafter”*** ***“for the life of this world.”*** al-Ṭabarī reads the sentence as the identification of the people whose conduct has been set out above. Al-Ālūsī explains the trade as the preference of the lower life over the higher, with the exchange made deliberately by people who were able to gain the better portion. Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the same sense of a purchase made with the wrong currency.

The verse is read by al-Saʿdī together with the events of the one before it, and the choice is explained by him as a calculation about reputation. The people feared the disgrace of abandoning their allies more than they feared the Fire, and they chose the Fire rather than the shame. That is why the sentence is phrased as a trade rather than as a lapse, because a trader knows what he is giving up, and the verse keeps the word in place of the deed. Ibn ʿUthaymīn notes that the same trade is repeated in later generations, where people hold the world tightly and treat the Hereafter as though it were nothing. He keeps the comparison as a warning rather than as an insult aimed at one community.

**THE PUNISHMENT THAT IS NOT LIGHTENED**

What the trade costs is then stated: ***“So their punishment will not be reduced”*** ***“nor will they be helped”***. al-Ṭabarī keeps the two clauses in the order given. The first is read by al-Ālūsī as the refusal of any relief, whether by reduction or by abatement, and the second as the refusal of any party who could stand up for them. The note that the punishment remains at its full weight without a moment of relief is kept by Ibn ʿUthaymīn. He mentions the wish of the people of the Fire for a single day of respite, which the Book records in another sūrah (40:49).

The two clauses together answer the claim reported earlier in the passage, where the community expected a short stay. Because the sentence removes both the reduction and the helper, it closes the two doors through which a person imagines a lighter end, a shorter term or a friend at court. Because the trade is stated as a completed deal, the punishment is presented as the consequence of a transaction already made rather than as a penalty imposed at random.

**WHAT THE VERSE LEAVES WITH THE READER**

The sentence is worth reading with the Book's own words about the other harvest beside it. (42:20 — **“the harvest of the Hereafter”**) is named as the portion of those who seek what God has. (3:77 — **“no share in the Hereafter”**) is named as the state of those who sell their pledge for a price. The Book states both sentences as settled facts, and the reader is left with the choice that the passage has been building toward from its first question.

Because the passage has shown the conduct in detail, the reader is not asked to accept a verdict on trust. A person who trades one good for another usually believes he is gaining in the exchange, and the verse accepts the description of the trade and then states what it bought. The reason the Book keeps the two harvests side by side is that a reader who understands what was traded will understand what was lost. Because the account has shown the community's conduct in detail, the reader does not need to be told which side of the trade was heavier, and the sentence leaves the ledger where it stands. The passage ends with the people who lost both sides of the exchange, and the Book ends it without a further argument, since the conduct has been described and the reckoning has been named.

Think of a man who sells an orchard for the price of a week's meals because the week is in front of him and the trees are not. The meals are eaten, the weeks pass, and the orchard was never his again.

---

## Verse 2:187

> It has been made permissible for you to be intimate with your wives during the nights preceding the fast. Your spouses are a garment for you as you are for them. Allah knows that you were deceiving yourselves. So He has accepted your repentance and pardoned you. So now you may be intimate with them and seek what Allah has prescribed for you. ˹You may˺ eat and drink until you see the light of dawn breaking the darkness of night, then complete the fast until nightfall. Do not be intimate with your spouses while you are meditating in the mosques. These are the limits set by Allah, so do not exceed them. This is how Allah makes His revelations clear to people, so they may become mindful ˹of Him˺

## Verse 2:196

> Complete the pilgrimage and minor pilgrimage for Allah. But if prevented ˹from proceeding˺, then ˹offer˺ whatever sacrificial animals you can afford. And do not shave your heads until the sacrificial animal reaches its destination. But if any of you is ill or has a scalp ailment ˹requiring shaving˺, then compensate either by fasting, charity, or a sacrificial offering. In times of peace, you may combine the pilgrimage and minor pilgrimage then make the sacrificial offering you can afford. Whoever cannot afford that ˹offering˺, let them fast three days during pilgrimage and seven after returning ˹home˺—completing ten. These offerings are for those who do not live near the Sacred House. And be mindful of Allah, and know that Allah is severe in punishment

## Verse 2:217

> They ask you ˹O Prophet˺ about fighting in the sacred months. Say, “Fighting during these months is a great sin. But hindering ˹others˺ from the Path of Allah, rejecting Him, and expelling the worshippers from the Sacred Mosque is ˹a˺ greater ˹sin˺ in the sight of Allah. For persecution is far worse than killing. And they will not stop fighting you until they turn you away from your faith—if they can. And whoever among you renounces their own faith and dies a disbeliever, their deeds will become void in this life and in the Hereafter. It is they who will be the residents of the Fire. They will be there forever.”

## Verse 2:233

> ˹Divorced˺ mothers will breastfeed their offspring for two whole years, for those who wish to complete the nursing ˹of their child˺. The child’s father will provide reasonable maintenance and clothing for the mother ˹during that period˺. No one will be charged with more than they can bear. No mother or father should be made to suffer for their child. The ˹father’s˺ heirs are under the same obligation. But if both sides decide—after mutual consultation and consent—to wean a child, then there is no blame on them. If you decide to have your children nursed by a wet-nurse, it is permissible as long as you pay fairly. Be mindful of Allah, and know that Allah is All-Seeing of what you do

## Verse 2:259

> Or ˹are you not aware of˺ the one who passed by a city which was in ruins. He wondered, “How could Allah bring this back to life after its destruction?” So Allah caused him to die for a hundred years then brought him back to life. Allah asked, “How long have you remained ˹in this state˺?” He replied, “Perhaps a day or part of a day.” Allah said, “No! You have remained here for a hundred years! Just look at your food and drink—they have not spoiled. ˹But now˺ look at ˹the remains of˺ your donkey! And ˹so˺ We have made you into a sign for humanity. And look at the bones ˹of the donkey˺, how We bring them together then clothe them with flesh!” When this was made clear to him, he declared, “˹Now˺ I know that Allah is Most Capable of everything.”

## Verse 2:282

> O believers! When you contract a loan for a fixed period of time, commit it to writing. Let the scribe maintain justice between the parties. The scribe should not refuse to write as Allah has taught them to write. They will write what the debtor dictates, bearing Allah in mind and not defrauding the debt. If the debtor is incompetent, weak, or unable to dictate, let their guardian dictate for them with justice. Call upon two of your men to witness. If two men cannot be found, then one man and two women of your choice will witness—so if one of the women forgets the other may remind her. The witnesses must not refuse when they are summoned. You must not be against writing ˹contracts˺ for a fixed period—whether the sum is small or great. This is more just ˹for you˺ in the sight of Allah, and more convenient to establish evidence and remove doubts. However, if you conduct an immediate transaction among yourselves, then there is no need for you to record it, but call upon witnesses when a deal is finalized. Let no harm come to the scribe or witnesses. If you do, then you have gravely exceeded ˹your limits˺. Be mindful of Allah, for Allah ˹is the One Who˺ teaches you. And Allah has ˹perfect˺ knowledge of all things

## Verse 2:286

> Allah does not require of any soul more than what it can afford. All good will be for its own benefit, and all evil will be to its own loss. ˹The believers pray,˺ “Our Lord! Do not punish us if we forget or make a mistake. Our Lord! Do not place a burden on us like the one you placed on those before us. Our Lord! Do not burden us with what we cannot bear. Pardon us, forgive us, and have mercy on us. You are our ˹only˺ Guardian. So grant us victory over the disbelieving people.”
